# AI-Driven AAC — Project Context for Claude

## TONE — read this first (Ken, July 31 2026)

**Avoid anything that reads as combative, competitive, or snide.** This is a standing rule, not a style preference, and it applies to *everything* — chat replies, this file, commit messages, code comments, and generated documents. The persistent ones matter most: Ken re-reads CLAUDE.md and the git log, so a barbed sentence there keeps landing long after the conversation is over.

**Ken makes the calls; Claude's job is to inform them and then record them.** The failures are subtle and mostly come from forgetting that. Real examples from this project, kept because the abstract rule is easy to nod at and miss in practice:

- **Framing his decision as a dispute.** "Until then the disagreement is hypothetical" — he wasn't arguing, he was deciding. There is no contest to be settled. Record the decision and the reasoning behind it.
- **Grading or ratifying his judgment.** "Ken's answer is user discipline plus instruction, *which is legitimate*." "That is *a real concern*." "Deferring is *the right call*." He did not ask for a verdict on his thinking, and awarding one implies the standing to withhold it.
- **Scoring points when a concern turns out to be justified**, re-litigating a decision he has already made, or restating an objection he has already heard and overruled. Say it once, clearly, then build what he asked for.
- **Being defensive about earlier work** — mine or the project's. A mistake gets corrected and the work continues; it does not get argued about.

**Ken's suggestions are direction, not specification — do not report "changes" to them (Ken, August 3 2026).** *"My descriptions shouldn't be taken as expert programming recommendations. You're the expert programmer, not me. I just try to get us into an effective ballpark."* He sets the goal and the shape; the engineering inside it is Claude's call, and making it well is the job, not a deviation to be flagged. Prompted by his diagnostic-trace idea ("echo each call to a file"): recording every call would have been unusable — the audio frame handler runs about twelve times a second — so the build sampled decisions and counted the rest. **Correct engineering, wrongly announced** as "one change from your description", which framed a directional suggestion as a spec he had got wrong. Present that kind of judgment as *the design*, with its reasoning where the reasoning is interesting. **This is not licence to skip flagging things that matter:** a genuine risk, a real trade-off, or an interpretation that could go two ways still gets raised once, plainly — the distinction is between *"here is a decision you should know about"* and *"here is where I departed from your wording."*

**What good looks like:** state the substance plainly, flag a genuine risk once with its reasoning, give a recommendation when there is one, and then proceed. Disagreement itself is welcome and useful — Ken relies on it. It is the *framing* that must never become adversarial. Warmth is not required; neutrality is. If a sentence would sting to read a second time, rewrite it.

## PLAIN LANGUAGE — write so Ken can read it, or say nothing (Ken, August 8 2026)

Ken: *"I assume that terms like 'walking the object' are understood by programmers in this domain. I'm not one so I mostly ignore what you write. I'd like you to write in plain terms or don't bother to say anything."*

**The damage is not that jargon annoys him — it is that he stops reading.** He is the one who makes the decisions here, so a paragraph he skips is a decision made without the information in it. Writing something technically accurate that goes unread is worse than writing nothing, because it leaves both sides believing it was communicated.

**Where this applies: everything Ken reads** — chat replies, every generated document, commit messages, and this file. **Code comments are the one exception**; their reader is the next developer, so they can be as technical as they need to be. That split is also the trap: it is easy to write a careful plain-language *document* and then narrate the work in developer shorthand in the same breath. That is exactly what happened the day this rule was written — a document about avoiding jargon, explained to Ken in jargon.

**The test before sending: would someone who has never written code follow this sentence?** Ken spent three decades as a systems engineer, so systems, trade-offs, and architecture are all fair game. What he does not have is *programming* vocabulary. "It was looking in the wrong place" is fine. "It was walking the wrong object" is not. Neither are: object, instance, array, string, parse, callback, hook, seam, invariant, refactor, regex, DOM, or a bare file or function name used as though it were a thing he knows.

**"Or don't bother to say anything" is a real instruction, not exasperation.** If a detail cannot be put plainly and he does not need it to decide anything, leave it out. Length is not thoroughness. What he needs from a report is: what now works, what it cost or saved, what is still broken, and anything waiting on his call — not the route taken to get there.

## Product Name
The product is named **Conversant AAC** (decided June 18 2026). The name captures the product's core mission: transforming communication into conversation. "Conversant" carries both meanings simultaneously — fluent in conversation, and *in* conversation. The project/working directory continues to be called "AI-driven AAC" for continuity; the product-facing name in the app and documents is Conversant AAC. The product is free and open source.

## About Ken (the user)
Ken Hackbarth is President and Founder of Volksswitch.org. He has a three-decade background as a systems engineer at AT&T Bell Laboratories. He is **not** a developer or AI/AAC researcher — he is a product manager and systems thinker. He defines requirements, makes feature decisions, and recruits beta testers. Claude Code is the developer.

Published author: "Revolutionizing Augmentative and Alternative Communication with Generative Artificial Intelligence," ATOB Volume 18, Spring 2024 (pp. 100–123). This paper is the foundational vision document for this project and is stored as `ATOB_V18_Hackbarth.pdf` in this folder.

## Working Guidelines for Claude Code

**Verify UI details against source before writing about them.** When producing any document (manual, overview, spec, etc.) that describes specific UI elements — button labels, icons, layout positions, control names — read the relevant source files (`index.html`, `ui.js`, `styles.css`, etc.) first. Do not infer or assume UI details from convention or common patterns. Invented UI details (e.g., assuming a gear icon exists for Settings when the button has no icon) are silent errors that reach users. If source files are not read first, the document must not assert specific UI details — hedge or omit them instead.

**A CHANGE THAT CROSSES LAYERS GETS ONE CHECK THAT CROSSES ALL OF THEM (standing rule, Ken, August 23 2026).** Testing each layer separately does not add up to testing the feature, and **the failure is invisible in exactly the way that matters: every test passes, the coverage looks complete, and the feature is dead.** That is not hypothetical here — the number-pad button shipped in **0.7.14 announced in the release notes and doing nothing at all**, because the parser was tested by handing it JSON, the renderer was tested by handing it a button, the live model turned out to classify every case correctly, and the field was silently dropped in the one link between them that nothing had run (see the classification-whitelist entry).

- **The tell, and it is worth applying while writing the tests rather than afterwards: if every check you wrote FABRICATES the input to the layer under test, you have not tested the path.** Somewhere, one check must take the output of the previous layer as its input, all the way through.
- **What counts:** a unit test that drives the real chain end to end (parse a real model response → feed it to the engine → read the snapshot the renderer uses), a browser check that exercises the actual user path, or a live-API run. **Three tests that each cover one link do not count**, however thorough each is.
- **It is usually cheap**, because the layers are all in one process. The 0.7.15 test is ten lines and would have caught it.
- **Where a layer genuinely cannot be driven, say so in the report.** `app.js` is not testable — that is why the conversation-loop decisions were extracted into their own module — so the honest form is to exercise everything reachable, cover the rest in the browser, and **name the link that was never run** rather than describing the feature as verified.
- **This is a rule about REPORTING as much as testing.** “Verified” must mean the path ran, not that its pieces did. When it did not, the sentence to write is *which* part was exercised.

**Keep this file to what changes a FUTURE decision — it is loaded in full at the start of every session (Ken, August 7 2026).** CLAUDE.md is not a log. Every line costs context in every future session, permanently, and the cost is paid before any work starts. It reached 681 KB before the August 7 consolidation — a fifth of it a release-by-release table that nothing consulted to make a decision.

**An entry keeps three things:** the **decision**; the **reasoning that constrains future work** — why the alternative was rejected, what breaks if it is reversed; and the **failure mode to avoid**, including a mistake whose lesson transfers ("when geometry is wrong but the screen looks right, suspect the measurement basis"). **That reasoning is the point of this file and must not be trimmed** — it is what stops settled decisions being re-litigated, and Ken re-reads it. Pruning is aimed at narrative, never at *why*.

**What belongs somewhere else:**
- what shipped in a release → `CHANGELOG.md` (user-facing) and [VERSION-HISTORY.md](VERSION-HISTORY.md) (engineering)
- what was built and verified in a pass → the commit message, and [BUILD-HISTORY.md](BUILD-HISTORY.md) if it needs a durable home
- what the code does → the code, with a comment pointing here for the why
- a bug fixed on the way to the right answer, whose lesson does not generalize → the commit message

**The test:** *would someone making a decision six months from now be worse off without this sentence?* If it only records that something happened, it belongs in git.

**Supersede rather than append.** When a decision is reversed, rewrite the entry and say what it replaces — do not leave both and make the reader work out which is current. Some sections had accumulated three layers of superseded design because each pass added a note instead of editing the one below it; the placeholder ladder was rebuilt twice before anyone noticed the file still described the first version as current.
---

## Project Vision
Build an AI-driven AAC system that gives non-speaking individuals access to **real-time conversation** — currently impossible with existing AAC devices. The core problem is not words-per-minute; it is the human aversion to awkward silence (~4 seconds). AAC users are locked into near-real-time or batch communication, which marginalizes them and restricts them to transactional rather than interactional communication.

**Mechanism:** Generative AI presents response options within seconds of a communication partner's utterance. The user selects one; the device speaks it. The system speaks *as* the user, not *for* them.

---

## Target User (Initial)
Literate, non-speaking individual with cerebral palsy (CP), 16+, using **direct select** (touch/pointer) as the access method. Vision is to expand to wider range of ages, literacy levels, and accessibility profiles over time.

---

## Architecture Components (from the paper)
1. **Situational Awareness** — GPS, face/voice recognition, calendar
2. **NLP In** — Speech-to-text
3. **Statement/Response Generation** — LLM (the linchpin)
4. **Statement/Response Shaping/Tuning** — worldview model, relationship modeling, conversation history
5. **NLP Out** — Text-to-speech

---

## Build Decisions (confirmed)

**Platform:** Static web app served from GitHub Pages (or similar). User points browser at a URL. App runs entirely in-browser. No backend server.

**Target hardware:** The File System Access API (FSA) is not supported on iOS or Android, and Apple does not make a tablet-class Mac. Therefore the device must be a Windows tablet running Microsoft Edge or Google Chrome. The Microsoft Surface is the primary candidate.

> **iPad viability — the "browser DB" path (Ken asked June 9 2026; recorded, NOT a decision to switch).** The "must be Windows" rationale is over-attributed to FSA alone. The blocker is specifically FSA's **directory picker** (`showDirectoryPicker()` — pick a real, user-visible folder), which iOS will not support (all iOS browsers are forced onto WebKit, so Chrome/Edge on iPad can't add it either). But the app's *data* could live on an iPad two other ways that iOS **does** support: **IndexedDB** (a real in-browser database — the app already uses it to stash the FSA folder handle) and **OPFS** (Origin Private File System — confusingly *part of* the FSA spec and supported on iOS Safari 16+, but it's the app-**sandboxed** private filesystem, NOT the user-picked folder). So iPad storage is technically viable by swapping the `storage.js` layer off the user-folder and onto IndexedDB/OPFS. **Two real costs, why it's not a free win:** (1) it forfeits exactly what the FSA folder was chosen to give — a **user-visible, portable file** they can back up and copy between machines; the v0.2.25 "drop `worldview.json` into the folder" cross-PC flow evaporates and cross-device transfer becomes a deliberate **export/import** feature; and (2) **durability** — iOS aggressively evicts script-writable storage (historically a ~7-day cap for non-installed sites, plus eviction under storage pressure), so it would **require** PWA install (Add to Home Screen) + `navigator.storage.persist()`, and even then persistence is best-effort. **[SUPERSEDED July 29 2026 — this durability pessimism is out of date.** Safari 17+ supports the Storage API fully, and WebKit's eviction sweep **skips origins that have been granted `navigator.storage.persist()`** — so a granted persist() is an actual exemption, not best-effort, and a Home Screen install may not be *required* for durability. That matters because of a newly-found pincer: speech recognition is reported **not to work in a standalone Home Screen app**, so "install for durability" and "stay in a tab for speech" may be mutually exclusive. See `Conversant AAC iPad Architecture.docx` §3.4 and §7.1; the probe settles it.**]** **The bigger iPad blocker is NOT storage — it's speech-to-text, and it WILL NOT work as expected on iOS.** The core loop depends on the Web Speech API (`SpeechRecognition`), whose iOS Safari support has been historically absent-to-flaky; that, not storage, is the load-bearing risk with no clean fallback (TTS/`speechSynthesis` is fine on iOS — only *recognition* is the problem). **A related fact that surfaced July 9 2026:** browser `SpeechRecognition` is **cloud-based, not on-device** — Chrome ships the audio to Google, Edge to Microsoft — so it needs a live internet connection *regardless of platform* (with no internet it can't transcribe at all; the app now trips the transcript red-wash on that STT `network` error). This compounds the iOS problem: even where the Web Speech API nominally exists on iOS it's unreliable, and it's a network service either way, so partner-speech capture cannot be assumed to work on an iPad. **Bottom line:** browser DB removes the *storage* leg of the Windows-only decision; the **STT leg remains** and must be prototyped on current iPadOS Safari before any iPad bet.

**Local data storage:** File System Access API — the app is granted permission to read/write a local folder on the user's machine. All worldview data, conversation history, and profile data stays on-device. No data sent to any server except the LLM API.

**Cross-device transfer:** Later feature — user manually exports a data package and moves it via iCloud, Google Drive, or OneDrive.

**AI vendor:** Claude API (Anthropic) first. Multi-vendor support added later. User creates their own Anthropic account and supplies their own API key. The Claude API is pay-as-you-go, billed per token — there is **no ongoing free tier for API access** (the free tier on the consumer claude.ai app does not apply to the developer API). The user pays only for the conversations they actually have. API key stored locally.

> **Vendor choice is a USER decision — do not architect around Anthropic only. (Ken, June 14 2026.)** Anthropic is the *first* implementation, not the *only* one. The design must keep the LLM provider swappable: which vendor (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, a local model, …) and which model tier is ultimately the user's choice. **Architectural requirement going forward:** isolate all provider-specific concerns behind a provider-agnostic interface (a generation call that takes conversation + profile/system text and returns `{classification, options, missing_facts}`), so adding a vendor is a new adapter, not a rewrite. *Current Anthropic-specific touchpoints to abstract when we add the second vendor* (all in `app/js/llm.js`): the `api.anthropic.com/v1/messages` endpoint, the `x-api-key` / `anthropic-version` / `anthropic-dangerous-direct-browser-access` headers, the request body shape (`system` + `messages` with `user`/`assistant` roles), the model id string, and the `usage.input_tokens`/`output_tokens` response fields. Settings currently labels the field "Claude API Key" and the key validation assumes `sk-ant-…`; both become vendor-dependent. Pricing is per-vendor/per-model (`app/data/pricing.json` is currently single-rate). Keep this list current as the abstraction is built.

> **Future setting — quality-driven model tier (upgrade/downgrade). (Ken, June 14 2026.)** Add a user-facing control to move the model up or down a tier (e.g., within Anthropic: Haiku ↔ Sonnet ↔ Opus) based on the user's *perception of response-option quality* vs. cost. Today the model is hardcoded to `claude-sonnet-4-6` (chosen for the user-pays-per-token cost balance). This setting makes the intelligence ↔ cost tradeoff the user's call, surfaced in plain terms ("responses not good enough? try a stronger model — it costs more per conversation"). Pairs naturally with the multi-vendor abstraction above (tier is a per-vendor concept). Not built yet.

**AI is an enhancement layer, not a hard dependency — basic functionality survives with no LLM (Ken, July 9 2026).** A property that became explicit with the "AI unreachable" error-handling work (v0.5.79-dev): the LLM provides the *response-suggestion* layer, but the app's **basic conversational functionality does not require it.** With the AI down (or no API key set at all), the user can still (a) **validate partner speech** — the transcript displays what the partner said (shown blue/italic "uncleaned" when the AI can't tidy it), and (b) **communicate manually** — the Express Panel and "In my own words" compose/speak in the user's voice, and those turns are recorded to the transcript and saved to storage exactly as an AI-selected response would be. So the app degrades to a competent **manual AAC + live-transcript tool** when the AI is absent; the AI adds real-time *suggested* responses on top. (Caveat, not to be conflated: the *partner-capture* STT is itself a cloud service and needs internet — see the iPad note — so "no internet at all" also disables transcription, whereas "internet up, Anthropic down" keeps transcription + manual communication working. The AI-optional property is about the *LLM*, not the network.) **This belongs in the Product and Architecture overviews** (graceful-degradation / positioning) — tracked in DOC-SYNC.

**Speech-to-text transcript validation:** Displaying the transcript of what the partner said is **critical** — not optional. The user must be able to confirm the system heard correctly before selecting a response.

**End-of-utterance detection (continuous partner capture):** A silence period (the "Optional Responses silence period" setting) is a *checkpoint, not a stop*. Each time the partner pauses for that period, the speech collected so far is sent to the AI for response options and a placeholder is spoken; recording continues. Resumed speech is appended and the combined utterance is re-sent after the next pause, so each checkpoint yields a more complete option set. Recording stops only when the user selects a response, at which point the transcript is cleaned once and the exchange is stored. A "latest-wins" generation token discards superseded in-flight requests, and a persistent "Please repeat what you said." control discards a garbled capture and keeps listening. An **auto-resume-listening** setting (Settings → General; default **off**) turns partner recording back on automatically after a response is spoken, so the user need not tap Start Listening between exchanges. (Implemented June 2026; see `Continuous-Partner-Capture.docx` and Architecture Overview §9. **Refined in v0.2.32 (see Further Design Thoughts #4):** Start Listening never fires at startup, must be invoked manually at least once per session, and auto-resume is gated behind that first manual invocation (`manualListenArmed`), re-armed only after another manual Start once listening is manually turned off. This is the Phase 1 realization of the COMPLETE/INCOMPLETE/CONTINUING classifier and filler ladder above.)

**Voice/TTS:** Browser built-in TTS acceptable for MVP. Voice banking (personalized/cloned voice) is a valuable future feature.

**Communication partner:** Needs nothing. Entirely unaware of system internals. AR glasses are a future hardware form factor.

---

## Response Option Display

Response options shown to the user may appear in **compressed form** — a few words representing a fuller thought — to speed up reading. The system may also read the compressed hint aloud. The **trigger for the auditory hint is a user preference**: options include auto-playback as each option is highlighted in sequence, or on first tap (before a double-tap confirms selection). Additional triggers may be supported based on user feedback.

The user normally hears the **fully expanded response only when it is spoken** after selection. Whether the expanded text is displayed or read before confirmation may become a configurable option based on user feedback.

**The compressed form is KEPT, and which forms a card shows is now the user's setting (DECIDED Ken, August 20 2026: the short labels "have gotten good reviews — good enough to provide a way to make them visible").** The model has returned a short label ("hint") beside every response since v0.3.0 and **the card showed only the full wording for eleven months**, so nobody could judge the labels. They were made visible unconditionally on August 19 2026 as a deliberately temporary look, with the question of whether they stayed left open. **That question is now closed in their favor**, and the setting is their permanent home: **Settings → Conversation → "What a response card shows"** (`storage.loadCardTextMode`, default **`both-full`**) offers full only / short only / both-with-full-larger / both-with-short-larger.
- **Why four and not three.** Ken asked for "show both with one most prominent"; prominence has two directions and choosing one would have been a guess, so both-with-full-larger and both-with-short-larger are separate entries.
- **THE SAFETY RULE, and it is the load-bearing part: a card must never come up blank.** Only cards whose short label genuinely differs from their wording carry `has-hint`, and that class is the ONLY thing "short version only" may hide the full wording on. Openers, wind-downs, goodbyes and a not-yet-generated repair card set `hint === text` by construction ([engine.js](app/js/engine.js)), so they keep showing their words in every mode. **A blank card is a response the user cannot make** — a worse failure than a card that is too wordy. Never write a rule hiding `.response-text` without `.has-hint` in the selector.
- **Every card always CARRIES both forms; the setting is pure CSS on the palette container** (`ui.setCardTextMode`). So switching re-styles the cards already on screen — no round trip, no lost palette, and the user can flip through all four modes mid-conversation and judge them against real suggestions. **Do not reimplement this as a generation-time choice**; that would make the setting cost tokens and make it unjudgeable in the moment.
- **Geometry is identical in all four modes** (measured: same x/y/w/h), so no keyguard hole moves — this is a Rule 1 requirement, not a nicety. Text sizes swap inside the card; the card never changes shape.
- **The accessible name always carries the FULL wording**, whatever is on screen, because that is what will actually be spoken; the short label is `aria-hidden` throughout to avoid reading it twice.
- **Still unbuilt from the paragraphs above:** the *auditory* hint (speaking the short label on highlight or first tap). The visual half is settled; the spoken half remains a user preference nobody has built.

**Position carries CATEGORY, not rank (corrected Ken, August 7 2026).** This section used to read: *"Position 1 in the options list is the AI's best guess… the design goal is simply that the first option is the best option, with that accuracy improving over time."* That was the pre-engine design and the **four-slot move palette** (Conversation Engine, v0.3.0) superseded it. The four cells are **PREFERRED / DISPREFERRED / INITIATIVE / REPAIR** — *structurally distinct conversational moves* — and **PREFERRED is the aligning/affiliative second-pair-part, a CA category, NOT "the best answer."** Ken's framing, which is the reason the palette is shaped this way at all: supporting **all** the CA response categories is the point, and ranking them would defeat it. Cell positions are stable so the user can build motor automaticity — a category always sits in the same place — so **position tells you which kind of move it is, never how good it is.**
- **Consequence for instrumentation:** there is no such thing as a "position-1 hit rate." The measure is the **category selection distribution** — which is the more informative number anyway, because a category never selected is a category not earning its cell, heavy REPAIR use means something upstream is failing, and DISPREFERRED use is a *success* signal (plain AAC makes declining hard). See **Beta instrumentation and error reporting** below.
- **Position and category are confounded** by design (stable layout), so selection data cannot separate "picked because it's PREFERRED" from "picked because it's top-left." Partial disentanglement is free: the palette is **2×2 on a side dock and 1×4 on a bottom dock**, so testers on different docks see the same categories at different positions.
- **The product documents were CHECKED (August 7 2026) and are CLEAN — the stale claim was only ever here, no doc sync needed.** The Product Overview describes Preferred as *"the most natural, affiliative answer to what was said"* — a category description with no cross-category ranking. The Architecture Overview and Conversation Engine Design do call slot 1 *"the model's best guess at what this user would most plausibly say,"* which is compatible: it is a guess **at the user** within the affiliative category, not a claim that PREFERRED beats the other three. The Conversation Engine Overview spells that out — *"The 'best guess' is a guess at the user… not advice about what they ought to say. It models the person; it does not steer them."* **That paragraph is what makes the phrase safe**; read alone, the Architecture Overview line could be taken as ranking, so if that section is ever revised, keep the clarification with it.

---

## Worldview Model
- Minimum viable version: a carefully selected "getting to know you" questionnaire (~100 questions)
- Questions are completed entirely at the user's own pace; none are required
- The system provides meaningful functionality even with zero worldview questions answered
- Info gathering must be **chunked** — physically draining for CP users
- Many real-time exchanges need zero worldview context ("Hi, how are you?" needs no personalization)
- RAG will reduce token overhead as the profile grows over time
- Designing the specific questionnaire content is part of the work ahead

---

## Rollout Sequence

**Phase 1 — Core Conversation Loop (Proof of Concept)**
STT → display transcript → placeholder utterance auto-spoken → LLM generates response options → user selects → TTS speaks full response. No worldview shaping, no situational awareness.

Key Phase 1 features:
- **3 response options** by default (configurable)
- **Conversation context:** rolling window of the current session only
- **Placeholder/filler utterances** (e.g., "Just a second…", "Let me think about that") automatically generated and spoken while the user is selecting; customization supported
- **Conversation initiation:** via a set of canned conversation starters (pre-configured phrases the user can select to open a conversation); later refined by situational awareness in Phase 2
- **Compressed display / expanded speech** for response options (see above)
- **Identity / personalization — now the worldview profile (was the interim name + "About You" seed):** The June-2026 stopgap (Settings → General "Your Name" / "About You" fields injected into the prompt) has been **removed in worldview Build Step 4**. Personalization now comes entirely from the worldview questionnaire ("About Me"), injected via `worldview.buildBlock()`. The no-bracket safety rule still always applies (the prompt forbids `[Name]`/`[city]` and tells the model to phrase around any unknown detail), so the app degrades gracefully with an empty profile. See the **Worldview Questionnaire (June 2026)** section.
- **Offline review/feedback mode:** deferred — too complex even in rudimentary form
- **Response option UI layout:** requires a design discussion on how much of the screen is allocated to traditional AAC vocabulary vs. AI-facilitated response options; ultimately user-configurable

**Phase 2 — Situational Awareness**
Add location context (GPS), communication partner recognition (face/voice), and calendar integration. Canned conversation starters become contextually suggested based on location and partner identity.

**Phase 3 — Worldview Shaping and Tuning**
Integrate the worldview model. Responses shaped to reflect the individual user's personality and goals. RAG employed to manage profile size. Offline feedback/review mode introduced here.

---

## Advanced Future Capability: Streaming Response Generation
The long-term vision is for the system to begin generating and refining response options *while the communication partner is still speaking* — using partial STT transcripts as they arrive. Options would be continuously updated and narrowed as the utterance becomes clearer. This eliminates virtually all post-utterance latency. This capability requires significant design thought and is not targeted for Phase 1 or 2.

---

## Strategic Context
Prior attempts at AI-driven AAC have been shelved when funding ran out. Ken's architecture avoids this by:
- **No server infrastructure** the project must pay for
- **User-funded AI** — users bring their own API key, pay only for what they use

Ken has screenshots of and has presented live demos of a prior team's shelved product. The goal is to share this architecture with those developers as a more sustainable path forward.

**First deliverable:** A high-level architecture document suitable for sharing with those prior developers — not code.

---

## Conversation Analysis Design Layer (June 2026)

Ken collected Conversation Analysis (CA) literature and had it synthesized into `CA-Concepts-for-AAC-Architecture.docx` (on Ken's Desktop). Three design documents in this folder map those concepts to the system. **All project decisions are recorded here in CLAUDE.md** (Ken's preference — this folder syncs across machines; Claude's machine-local memory does not).

**Document convention (Ken, June 14 2026; restated August 11 2026): AMERICAN ENGLISH SPELLING ONLY** — "color", "center", "gray", "personalized", "practice", "judgment", "canceled", "maximize", not "colour", "centre", "grey", "personalised", "practise", "judgement", "cancelled", "maximise".
- **Scope is everything a user reads or hears, not just the `.docx`.** That means every generated document, `CHANGELOG.md` (which becomes the in-app "What's new" notes), `settings-help.json` (which the app **speaks aloud**), and any on-screen string in `app/`. **Code comments are out of scope** — same split as the plain-language rule: their reader is the next developer.
- **⚠ THIS RULE WAS NOT HOLDING, which is why it is now stated with its failure mode.** Ken asked for it to be noted on August 11 2026; it had been recorded since June 14 2026 and was being broken in **eight documents plus the spoken help and the release notes** — including "colour" in text written into both User Manuals *that same day*, and "practise" in four spoken-help entries the app reads out. The lesson is not that the rule was unclear: it is that **nothing checks it**, and British spellings arrive unnoticed one word at a time.
- **⚠ THE TEST'S SCOPE WAS TOO NARROW AND IT SHOWED (Ken, August 23 2026, finding “colour” eight times in a prototype and a figure source written an hour earlier).** It covered the app and the release notes, so **everything else a person reads had nothing checking it** — the documents' own source, the images inside them, and a prototype about to be emailed to a team of therapists. **The rule was never unclear; it was unenforced everywhere except the app.** Now also scanned: **string literals in `scripts/doc-generators/*.js`** (that prose IS the documents) and the **visible markup of the `.html` beside them** (captured into the figures), plus **`prototypes/`** — which is MORE exposed than the app, not less, because it goes to people outside the project. **⚠ AND THE WIDENING IMMEDIATELY EXPOSED A DETECTOR BUG worth keeping: “programming” was flagged.** “programme”→“program” is a real pair, but the **-ing and -ed forms double the m in American English too**, so the stem-plus-suffix rule matched a correct word. Any entry whose American form doubles a consonant has the same trap. **One deliberate exemption, listed by exact phrase rather than by file:** a test persona is Irish on purpose (Dublin, a GAA club, Clontarf), so *the shopping centre* and *on the telly* are his words — Americanizing them would make the persona false, exactly as it would a cited institution. A “colour” in that file still fails.
- **ENFORCED BY `tests/american-spelling.test.mjs`** over everything in git that a user reads or hears: `CHANGELOG.md`, `settings-help.json`, `app/index.html` and the **string literals** in `app/js/*.js` (literals, not whole files, so a comment saying "blue-grey" cannot fail the build). It caught three more the moment it was written — "initialised" and "neighbouring" in prompt text, "Cancelled." in an error the user can read in Troubleshooting. **The `.docx` are NOT covered** — they are git-ignored, so a test cannot depend on them; sweep those by hand during "sync docs".
- **⚠ IT IS NOT ONLY SPELLING — BRITISH VOCABULARY COUNTS, AND IT WENT UNNOTICED FOR MONTHS (Ken, August 22 2026, finding “Mum” and “the surgery” in a design document).** Every entry in the original list is a MISSPELLING, so a correctly spelled wrong word sailed past both the test and the eye. **The near-misses make this mostly unautomatable and the reason is worth stating: most British vocabulary is also good American vocabulary with a different meaning** — *surgery* is an operation, *tablets* are iPads, a *torch* burns, a *boot* is footwear, a *flat* is level, a *lift* is a ride, a *chemist* does chemistry. So `tests/american-spelling.test.mjs` gained a **short** `BRITISH_WORDS` list of words wrong in EVERY American reading (mum, lorry, petrol, fortnight, maths, car park, postcode, anticlockwise, …) and **can never catch “the surgery”**. That half is a person reading the sentence. **When writing examples, use American settings and names**: Mom not Mum, the clinic or the doctor's office not the surgery, store clerk not shop staff, pills not tablets, diner not cafe.
- **The word list is EXPLICIT, never a pattern, and that is not laziness.** `\w+ise` would condemn *advertise, exercise, surprise, compromise, supervise, promise, devise*, which are -ise on both sides of the Atlantic; a doubled-consonant rule would condemn *controlled* and *compelled*, which double in American English too. Each entry is a word we mean. **A stem ending in -e needs its own alternative**, because it drops that -e before -ing: "practise" + "ing" is *practising*, which a stem-plus-suffix match misses — one of the two words the hand sweep let through.
- **Sweep for it whenever documents are touched** — a "sync docs" pass is the natural moment. Search stems, not whole words: `grey` misses *greyed* and *greying*, `colour` misses *recolour*. The forms that actually turned up here were colour/colours/recolour, grey/greyed, favourite, judgement, maximise, cancelled, practise/practising, recognises, catalogue and behavioural.
- **Two things a naive find-and-replace gets WRONG, both hit on the first pass:** "**dialogue**" meaning a conversation is *already* correct American English (only the UI-box sense is "dialog"), and "**CALL Centre, University of Edinburgh**" is a cited institution's name — Americanizing a proper noun makes the citation false. Read the sentence before changing the word.

**Document convention (Ken, June 15 2026): every generated document's footer includes a "Page m of n" page number** (current page of total — e.g. "Page 3 of 12"), in addition to the standard "Volksswitch.org | June 2026 | For internal use" line. In docx-js use `PageNumber.CURRENT` and `PageNumber.TOTAL_PAGES`. Applies to every generated document going forward.

**Documents** (all Word, styled like Ken's papers — Arial, "Volksswitch.org | June 2026 | For internal use" footer; figures `ui-fig1..7.png` alongside, generated with Python/Pillow, regenerable on request):
1. `Conversation-Engine-Design.docx` — CA concepts → programmatic flow
2. `UI-Design.docx` — presentation layer and dynamic UI
3. `Configuration-Model.docx` — user-owned settings registry and experimentation loop

### Settled decisions — Conversation Engine

- **Sequence stack, not turn alternation**: conversation state is a stack of open sequences (adjacency pairs); repair sequences nest on top; the original obligation is restored automatically when they resolve.
- **Four-slot move palette**: PREFERRED / DISPREFERRED (hedge + account) / INITIATIVE / REPAIR — structurally distinct conversational moves, positionally stable for motor automaticity. (Refines the earlier "3 response options" default: the palette is typed by move, not just counted; option count remains configurable.)
- **Five modes**: LISTENING, RESPONDING, REPAIR-OF-SELF, INITIATING, PRE-CLOSING/CLOSING.
- **Access method independent of conversation logic**: engine emits a typed, prioritized palette descriptor; renderers (direct select now; scanning/eye gaze later) present it. Consistent with the direct-select initial target while keeping the expansion path clean.
- **System infers mode, user overrides** via persistent buttons (default set: Say again / Hold on / Pardon? / Wind down).
- **Single combined LLM call** classifies the partner's action (FPP type) AND generates options; classification is emitted first in structured output so it is inspectable. LLM stateless; app injects state.
- **Filler ladder** (refines the placeholder mechanism and the old 4 s initial delay): rung 1 acknowledgment token ≤1 s after end-of-utterance (no LLM — this is what makes transcript validation affordable), rung 2 projection filler ~2.5 s covering the confirm window, rung 3 periodic re-fill. CA: silence becomes meaningful past ~1 s.
- **Backchannels/continuers deferred**; end-of-utterance classification schema reserves COMPLETE | INCOMPLETE | CONTINUING so they slot in later without rework.
- **Highest-leverage near-term item**: REPAIR-OF-SELF mode — partner says "What?" → re-speak / rephrase / expand the user's last utterance.
- **Mode vs. floor are distinct dimensions; LISTENING is the system's *receptive posture*, not "the partner owns the floor" (refinement, Ken, June 15 2026, v0.3.2).** Ken's catch: the diagnostic panel showed `Mode: LISTENING` before any capture, and "partner has the floor" is wrong for the at-rest case. Two things were conflated. **`mode`** = what the *system* is doing (LISTENING / RESPONDING / REPAIR-OF-SELF / INITIATING / PRE-CLOSING-CLOSING). **`floor`** = whose turn it is — **open** (at rest / between turns, up for grabs), **partner** (partner producing or owed the next turn), **self** (user's turn to act). LISTENING spans *both* floor=partner (partner mid-utterance) and floor=open (startup, or the beat after a response) — so floor cannot be read off the mode, and the design doc's gloss of LISTENING as *"partner is speaking"* only covers the partner-mid-utterance case. Also: because the persistent overrides (Start conversation / Say again / Hold on / Pardon? / Wind down / "Please repeat" / the composer) are available in **every** mode, the user can seize the floor at any time — floor=partner is a *default expectation*, not an exclusive lock. **Built:** `floor` is a first-class field on the engine `ConversationState` + snapshot (`engine.FLOOR = {OPEN, PARTNER, SELF}`), set at each transition (partner capture → partner; INCOMPLETE → partner; COMPLETE → self; repair-initiator → self; selectMove/completeRepairOfSelf → open; pardon → partner; windDown/initiate → self), and shown as its own **Floor** row in the degenerate panel (open=grey, partner=orange, self=blue). The earlier **Capture (mic)** row (v0.3.1) stays — it's an I/O state, also distinct from both mode and floor (a silent-but-listening mic is still floor=open). **Mode shows "STANDBY" when genuinely at rest (v0.3.3, Ken).** Ken's follow-up: "LISTENING" reads wrong when the mic is off. Rejected relabeling Mode off the mic directly (re-couples the dimensions we just split) and the word "Holding" (collides with *floor-holding* — the filler ladder + the "Hold on" button). Instead the renderer shows **STANDBY** in the Mode cell only when the engine is *truly idle* — `capture off AND floor open AND empty stack AND no palette` — otherwise the engine's mode verbatim. **Presentation-only:** the engine's internal mode stays `MODE.LISTENING` (no sixth mode, engine untouched). Because the label now depends on both the snapshot *and* the mic state, `ui.js` keeps the last snapshot + a `capturing` flag and re-renders the Mode cell from `renderModeCell()` whenever either changes (so it flips STANDBY↔LISTENING the instant the mic toggles, not only on the next snapshot). *(Open question deferred: floor transitions are best-effort heuristics in the engine; whether the renderer should ever override them, and how lapses are handled, is unspecified.)*
- **No transcript-confirmation gate — generation fires on the silence period, not on a confirm tap (DECIDED Ken, June 15 2026).** The Conversation-Engine-Design.docx §6 line *"the generation window starts only after the user confirms (or corrects) the transcript"* — the CA-purist intersubjectivity gate — is **relaxed to a configurable default, off.** Generation starts after `n` seconds of partner silence (the existing "Optional Responses silence period" setting), with **no** explicit transcript-confirm step, to shorten the user's time-to-respond. This is not a new departure: (a) the design doc already lists **"confirmation gates"** among the *defaults of the standard profile, not fixed rules* — it is **not** an invariant (the invariants are repair path / floor-holding / closing / free composition / visibility of system speech / revertibility); and (b) the shipped **Continuous Partner Capture** already generates on each silence checkpoint with no confirm tap. **What we keep (these are what make "no gate" safe):** (1) the **transcript still displays** (CLAUDE.md "critical, not optional") — the user *sees* what was heard, just isn't required to tap-confirm; (2) the **REPAIR / "Pardon?" slot + persistent "Please repeat what you said" control** is the recovery for STT errors — the old gate caught errors *before* generation, the relaxed model catches them *after* (cheaper in time; repair is an invariant); (3) the auto-confirm-above-confidence-threshold knob was already the escape hatch — this effectively sets it to "always." The three-state transcript display may survive as **informational** (heard / generating / ready) but no longer **blocks** generation. **Setting:** "Require transcript confirmation before generating" — Profile-tier, default **off**, for the rare CA-purist user. **Docs to update (deferred):** `Conversation-Engine-Design.docx` §6 and `UI-Design.docx` transcript-gate section must stop asserting the gate as mandatory.

- **No turn-completion gate — the app never decides when the partner's turn is "done"; it generates on every pause and the USER decides by responding (DECIDED Ken, July 10 2026; reverses design §8).** After two silent-stall bugs (the user-started conversation, v0.5.81, then a later disfluent go-ahead), Ken's call: **stop trying to know when the partner has completed their turn.** Rationale: (a) STT is often wrong, so gating on a completeness judgment derived from garbled text is fragile; and (b) turn-completion is hard even for humans — asking the machine to solve it reliably was the root mistake. **New model:** every silence checkpoint generates AND *shows* a response palette, refined on each subsequent pause (latest-wins); the partner's turn is "complete" only when the **user responds or ends the conversation.** This **removes design §8's false-TRP suppression guard** entirely (and the v0.5.82 pause-to-complete fallback that briefly patched it). **CA verdict (Ken asked): it does NOT violate CA — arguably more faithful.** CA treats turn-completion as a *participant accomplishment* (projectability is a competence the parties exercise), not a property you read off an utterance; the device is not the participant, the **user** is, so deferring the "is the turn done?" judgment to the user is CA-aligned. And §8's false-TRP concern was about the *system taking action* at a false TRP — but the app now only *prepares* options (silent), never auto-acts, so there is no false-TRP action to commit. None of the project's CA invariants depend on turn_status. **What's kept:** `partner_action` (openers/closers + placeholder flavor) and `is_repair_initiator` ("What?" → repair-of-self) — "what kind of action," far more robust than "are they done"; `turn_status` stays in the LLM schema but is **informational only** (nothing gates on it). **Engine change:** `ingestClassification` no longer suppresses on turn_status; because every pause now re-ingests, a later pause refining the SAME ongoing partner turn **updates the top PARTNER FPP in place** instead of stacking a duplicate (the partner holds the floor until the user's SPP pops it). **Placeholders (Ken, decisive): unchanged and NOT user-triggered** — the user is busy reading the cards and can't be asked to initiate them. The first placeholder fires `initialDelay` seconds **after the pause**; the partner **starting to talk again aborts and resets** the ladder (`handlePartnerResumed` → `placeholders.stop()`), so a placeholder only actually speaks after real silence. **Known limitation Ken accepts "for now":** detecting partner resumption *while a placeholder is actively playing* is unreliable (the mic hears the app's own placeholder audio; the echo guard blocks detection until it finishes + the echo tail) — so a partner cutting in mid-placeholder isn't aborted until it ends. Resumption *before/between* placeholders is caught fast (first interim STT result). The robust fix is Phase-2 **partner voice recognition**. **Docs to update (deferred):** `Conversation-Engine-Design.docx` §8 must stop asserting the false-TRP suppression guard as engine behavior.

- **Closed-set (alternative-question) turns override the four-slot structure — the cards become the partner's options (DECIDED Ken, July 27 2026).** Found in the doctor Practice scenario: the partner asked whether the tiredness was "mild, moderate, or severe" and all four cards came back as variations on **mild** — the other two alternatives the partner actually offered were unreachable without the composer. **Root cause was the generation axis, not the UI:** the prompt tells the model to always return four responses typed by *structure* (PREFERRED/DISPREFERRED/INITIATIVE/REPAIR), so on a menu question it produced four structural takes on one alternative; nothing in the classification told it a closed set existed. **Decision:** classification gains **`offered_options`** — the alternatives the partner named, in the order they said them, `[]` for an ordinary turn. When it is non-empty the four structural slots are **abandoned for that turn** and the palette becomes one **`CHOICE`** card per alternative (text = a natural full sentence, "It's been pretty mild."; hint = the bare label). **Every remaining cell is then filled (Ken, July 27 2026 — a two-way question leaves two cells, and an empty cell is something the user cannot say)**, from a ranked filler set, all in the CHOICE family so the renderer keeps laying the palette out one-per-cell: **`CHOICE_OTHER`** (an answer outside the set — in-between / neither / it-depends; always included when a cell is free, and allowed **twice**, which is often the best use of a two-way question's spare cells: "About the same." *and* "It comes and goes."), then **`CHOICE_ASK`** (turn the question back — "What would you recommend?"), then **`CHOICE_REPAIR`** (ask for the choices again, for when they were long or misheard). Fillers keep the hue of the structural move they stand in for (other=amber, ask=blue, repair=purple), so color means the same thing on a closed-set turn as on any other. A 4-alternative set fills the 4-card footprint with choices alone and gets no fillers. **Why this shape:** it costs no extra round-trip and no new region — the reserved 4-cell footprint already distributes non-category palettes one-per-cell (the same branch openers/wind-downs/closings and repair-of-self use), and 8-card mode stacks two per cell automatically, so a menu of up to 8 fits. **Implementation note that is load-bearing:** `ui.showResponses` switches to category-grouping the moment **any** card carries one of the four category slots, so a choice palette must be a *pure* CHOICE/CHOICE_OTHER family — mixing in a DISPREFERRED escape hatch would silently drop every choice card. Colors reuse the existing hues (choices preferred-green, escape hatch dispreferred-amber). **Follow-on — BUILT (July 27 2026): Express Panel choice chips** — reserved Express Panel cells (blank at rest, so the keyguard holes stay honest) that fill with the offered alternatives; tapping one round-trips to the AI with a steer focused on that alternative and refreshes the four *structural* cards about it, so the user can escalate from a one-tap answer to a hedged/expanded reply about "moderate". Reuses the built Reframe seam (`llm.generateResponses({steer})` → `engine.refreshPalette`). Ken chose "cards first, chips after"; `offered_options` is carried on `state.lastClassification` for the chips to consume. **As built — the Express Panel is DYNAMIC here, a deliberate exception to Rule 1's reserved-real-estate pattern (Ken, July 27 2026).** A first cut reserved a fixed 3 cells that sat blank at rest; Ken reversed it: **no standing reservation.** With no closed set on offer the panel is exactly the phrase grid it was before the feature existed; when a set arrives the chips claim **only as many leading cells as there are choices**, the phrases shift along, and the equal number at the end fall off — then slide back when the turn ends. **What this does and does not cost (Ken corrected an earlier overstatement of mine here):** it does **not** touch keyguard usability — the grid is pixel-identical with 0, 3, or 4 chips (33 cells, same x/y/w/h, verified), and only the *content* of the leading cells changes. What it costs is the weaker, second-order motor-planning stability — which phrase sits behind a given hole for that one turn — and per the Rule 1 priority order that is explicitly subordinate to putting a quickly-selectable response in front of the user. So this is the cheap side of the trade, not a concession. A Settings → Conversation control **"Most choice buttons in the Express Panel"** (`storage.loadChoiceChipMax`, default **4**, options 0/2/3/4, 0 = off) is a **ceiling, not a reservation** — it stops an unusually long list from pushing half the panel off. `ui.renderExpressPanel` gained `choiceChips`/`choiceColor`/`onChoiceChip` (chips consume `choiceChips.length` leading cells); chips are solid green (echoing the CHOICE cards) with a dashed border to read as transient, and are **always single-tap** regardless of the double-tap setting — that safeguard guards *speaking*, and a chip doesn't speak, it asks for options. `app.handleChoiceChip` is a guided regenerate on the Reframe seam (`llm.generateResponses({focusChoice})` → `engine.refreshPalette`, so the stack/mode/floor are untouched — verified: stack depth stays 1 through a chip tap and pops to 0 on the answer). **The load-bearing subtlety:** the chip's regeneration re-sends the same partner turn, so the model would re-detect the menu and hand back the choice cards again, making the chip appear to do nothing — hence `focusChoice` emits an explicit override block ("THE USER HAS ALREADY CHOSEN… the closed-set rule does NOT apply, return `offered_options: []` and the four structural slots, every one answering with X"). Chips are cleared at every turn boundary (`commitExchange`, `startFreshListening`, `terminateConversation`) **and** gated at render time on `currentPartnerText` being non-empty, so no path can leave a dead chip on screen. **Steering persists across "New N" WITHIN a turn (Ken, July 27 2026 — bug found live: tapping the "milk" chip then pressing New-4 came back with all three options again).** `handleRegenerate` rebuilt the request from scratch and passed neither the chip focus nor the composer's typed steer, so "give me different options" silently meant "reset my steering" — the same defect hit the **composer Reframe** text. Fixed with a per-turn `activeSteer = { focusChoice, steer }` in app.js: a chip tap sets `focusChoice`, composer Reframe sets `steer`, both are re-sent by `handleRegenerate` (alongside `avoid`, so the model varies the wording *under the same steering*), and the two are independent so they can compose ("milk" + "mention I'm lactose intolerant"). Cleared by `clearTurnSteering()` at every boundary where the steered turn ends or is replaced — `commitExchange`, `startFreshListening`, `terminateConversation`, and the top of `generateOptions` (the partner said more → fresh offer). **This does NOT reopen the v0.3.20 one-shot decision:** the steer is still discarded across *turns*; it now simply survives a regenerate *within* the turn it was aimed at, which is what "New N" always meant. **The trigger was too narrow — widened from "alternative question" to "options put on the table" (Ken, July 27 2026).** Field case: *"Of course! We've got muffins, croissants, and a few different pastries today — anything jump out at you?"* came back **not** a closed set. Two independent reasons, and by the letter of the spec the model was **right**: (1) the rule and every example described an **alternative question**, where the list *is* the question ("coffee or tea?") — here the list sits in a **declarative** and the question after it is open ("anything jump out at you?"); and (2) it genuinely isn't *closed* — "a few different pastries" isn't enumerable, "we've got" is availability not a menu, and the open invitation admits items never listed. **Ken's observation is the real finding: the list-in-a-statement-plus-open-invitation form is far more common in everyday service talk than the textbook alternative question**, so restricting to the latter missed most real cases. The rule now triggers on **either** form, states that the list need not be exhaustive nor the following question an alternative question, and adds two guards: **the test is whether the partner is OFFERING the items for the user to choose from, not merely mentioning them** (a narrative list — "I picked up milk, eggs, and bread on the way home" — must stay `[]`, the obvious over-trigger risk of widening), and **a vague item is never dressed up as a definite choice** ("a few different pastries" becomes a card that *asks* — "What kind of pastries do you have?" — rather than a fabricated specific). **A question after the options is a SIGNAL, not a requirement (Ken + Claude, July 27 2026).** Ken's observation — "there is always a question after the options" — is right about the common case and is the clearest cue separating the barista offer from a narrative list, so the prompt now names it as a strong signal. It is deliberately **not** made a requirement: **declarative offers carry no question at all** ("I could do Tuesday, Wednesday, or Friday.", "There's soup, salad, or a sandwich if you're hungry." — both already prompt examples), and casual planning talk is full of them, so requiring a trailing question would drop that whole class. The load-bearing discriminator remains *offering* vs *merely mentioning*. *(This also fixed an internal inconsistency: form (b) had been worded "a list offered in a STATEMENT **with an open invitation after it**" while two of its own three examples had no invitation.)* **Live-verified against the real model** on all four cases: the barista line → `["muffins","croissants","a few different pastries"]` with the third card rendered as the asking form and a CHOICE_OTHER ("Nothing for me right now, thanks."); the classic alternative question → still detected; the narrative list and an open question ("how have you been feeling lately?") → both `[]` with the normal four structural slots.

**Sizing of the offered set — Ken's on-the-record prediction (July 27 2026), logged at his request so he can eat his words later: "I don't think that five options will ever be selected… That's too many for most people to remember. Four will be rare but possible."** So the design is tuned for **2–3 alternatives as the common case and 4 as the rare ceiling**, and the 5+ case is deliberately left to degrade gracefully rather than being engineered for. *If a real conversation ever produces a five-way alternative question, this is the note to reopen — the fix is either pagination of the choice cards or an 8-card-mode-only allowance, both previously sketched and both declined here.*

**The partner's alternatives outrank the fillers (Ken, July 27 2026).** When four alternatives are offered in 4-card mode, **all four show and the escape hatch is dropped** — never trade one of the partner's own choices for something the model added, which would be the very defect the CHOICE palette exists to fix. This was already the emergent behavior (four choices leave no spare cell), but the prompt carried a `cap - 1` clause that could have tempted the model to drop an alternative to reserve a filler cell; the rule is now explicit, with fillers conditional on genuinely spare cells, and unit-tested. **Deliberately NOT done:** chips do not page past the ceiling — the response cards already carry the full set, so nothing is unreachable; revisit only if a real menu regularly exceeds it.

- **Closing detection: `CLOSING` defined in the prompt, and a decline-the-closing card (Ken, July 27 2026).** Prompted by the offered-set finding — the same "textbook form" bias was suspected in the closing path. **Two of the three suspicions were wrong, one was right, and a fourth gap turned up.** (a) **The `looksLikeClosing` fast path is CORRECT as-is — do not widen it.** Its narrowness is a deliberate precision trade already documented in `conversation-logic.js`: a miss falls through to the AI (costs latency only), a false positive shows goodbyes while the partner is still talking. The error asymmetry is the **reverse** of the offered-set case, where widening was free. (b) **`CLOSING` was never defined in the prompt** — it appeared only in the enum, with every other classification field given explicit definition and examples. Now defined from the CA literature (Schegloff & Sacks, *Opening up closings*): the move that OPENS a closing is usually **semantically empty** ("Well…", "So…", "Anyway…", "Okay then", "Right"), plus other-attentive warrants ("I should let you go"), arrangements ("See you Tuesday"), appreciations ("It was good seeing you"), and service sign-offs ("Have a good one") — not just farewell tokens. Carries an explicit **precision guard**: ending a TOPIC is not ending the CONVERSATION ("Anyway, what did you think of the film?" is not a closing), and when unsure do NOT classify CLOSING, because a wrong CLOSING replaces the response options with goodbyes mid-conversation. (c) **New `SLOT.CLOSING_DECLINE` — "Actually, before you go —".** A pre-closing is an *offer* to end, and CA makes **declining it** maximally relevant at that exact moment; the palette previously offered only goodbyes, so the user could accept the closing or leave the palette entirely. The phrase is user-editable (`control-phrases.declineClosing`, Controls tab, single-phrase like holdOn/pardon), rendered initiative-blue against the slate goodbyes, and **pinned**: `renderStaticPalette` gained a `pin` option whose entries take the last cells and are excluded from paging, so "New N" cycles the goodbyes while the decline stays put (a card that a page turn can hide is useless). Offered **only on a PARTNER-initiated closing** — never when the user is winding down their own. Selecting it calls new **`engine.reopenFromClosing()`** (phase→BODY, mode→LISTENING, floor→OPEN), because `selectResponse` deliberately *keeps* `PRE_CLOSING_CLOSING` and the conversation would otherwise stay stuck in pre-closing with the next partner turn still read through the closing fast-path. In a real conversation it then starts listening (the partner will typically wait); **in Practice Mode it must NOT** — there is no mic, so it idles until the user says their piece. **Still deferred pending field evidence: splitting the partner's side into pre-closing vs terminal.** The user side has had wind-down and closing as two steps since v0.5.93, but the partner's side still collapses both into `CLOSING` → goodbyes, so a partner's "I should get going" skips the reciprocal wind-down. That is a structural flow change and a false pre-closing is disruptive, so it waits for real transcripts rather than a hypothesis.

### Settled decisions — UI

- Four screen regions: transcript (with mode chip), move palette, persistent controls, composer access. Geometry never reflows on mode change — "the cards changed," never "the screen changed."
- Move card anatomy: glanceable hint (primary reading target; names the action, not a truncation), full TTS text, slot badge, format tag, latency dot (filled = instant, hollow = generation round-trip).
- **Triple coding**: slot identity = position + color + text badge; never color alone. Defaults: green preferred, amber dispreferred, blue initiative, purple repair.
- Transcript is a three-state *display*: amber UNCONFIRMED → blue CONFIRMED·GENERATING → green OPTIONS READY. **It no longer gates generation by default** — per the Conversation-Engine decision above (Ken, June 15 2026), generation fires on the silence period, not on a confirm tap; the states are informational. The blocking gate (never generate from an unconfirmed transcript) survives only as a Profile-tier setting, default off.
- **Nothing is spoken on the user's behalf invisibly** — every filler/token is displayed as it plays.
- **Palette updates queue until a selection boundary**: options never change under a user mid-selection (mandatory for future scanning/dwell renderers).
- Engine→UI contract is a versioned palette-descriptor JSON with `priority` and `droppable` per move; renderers drop/page by priority, never by move type.

### Configurability philosophy (overarching)

- **As many decisions as possible are user-determined and configurable.** This population is made up of unique individuals; the user experiments with a flow and adjusts from experience. Behavioral statements in the design docs are *defaults of the standard profile*, not rules.
- **Invariants are guaranteed capabilities, not fixed behaviors**: a repair path, a floor-holding path, a closing path, free composition, visibility of system speech, and revertibility must always exist; how they manifest is configurable. Config validation enforces "at least one path" rules at save time, with explanation — never silent correction.
- Three settings tiers by *when* a change is safe — Quick (mid-conversation) / Profile (between conversations) / Setup (rare, may be supporter-assisted) — all user-owned; nothing locked away from the user.
- Experimentation loop: named profiles (bindable to Practice Mode scenarios), automatic change journal, session review metrics (descriptive, local, private — inform the user, never score them), guided trials, one-tap revert. Revertibility is the one absolute.
- **Confirm before destroying significant work (standing rule, Ken, June 15 2026).** *Every* action that can wipe away significant user work — clearing/restarting answers, deleting people or relationships, bulk resets, overwriting a saved profile — MUST include an explicit validation step before it runs. This is a build checklist item, not an afterthought: when adding any clear/reset/delete/overwrite control, wire its confirmation in the same change. (Trivial, easily-redone actions — a single-field edit/skip with Undo, resetting the usage-token counter — don't require it; the bar is "significant work.")
  - **The confirmation must NOT be the native `window.confirm()` (Ken, June 15 2026, v0.2.30).** Native confirm renders a generic dialog indistinguishable from routine browser/OS prompts — notably the File System Access folder-permission popup — so the user dismisses it on autopilot and a destructive action doesn't read as dangerous. Use the **`confirmDanger()`** modal in [confirm-dialog.js](app/js/confirm-dialog.js) instead: a deliberately distinct red danger card (⚠ mark, red title + red confirm button, `.danger-dialog` CSS), Cancel focused by default (a stray Enter cancels), `showModal()` top-layer so it sits above the full-screen About Me overlay. Returns `Promise<boolean>`; use `if (!(await confirmDanger({title, body, confirmLabel, cancelLabel}))) return;`.
  - *Audit June 15 2026:* About Me **Restart** (`onRestart`) and **Remove person** route through `confirmDanger`; the only un-confirmed destructive action is the Settings usage-counter reset, which is below the bar.

---

## Standing findings from the archived build logs

The build records these came from moved to [BUILD-HISTORY.md](BUILD-HISTORY.md) on August 7 2026. The findings stayed here because they still constrain how the app is built. Each is the short form; the archive has the full story.

**The TTS-echo defense is TWO layers, and both are load-bearing (v0.3.4 + v0.3.9).** Content matching alone cannot work: the recognizer mis-hears the app's own audio, so a mangled echo slips past `stt.isEcho`, renews the silence timer and re-fires generation — a runaway loop of stack growth, option flicker and repeated round-trips. So there is also a **trigger-level guard** — while the app is speaking (plus `ECHO_TAIL_MS`) captured audio does not reset the silence timer and no checkpoint fires. **The mic deliberately stays on** (Ken's call, over muting it) so a partner talking over a placeholder is still heard; only *unique* content renews their turn. Hardened again in v0.5.90 — see "Transcript mirrors the conversation pane". The robust long-term fix remains Phase-2 partner voice recognition.

**A placeholder must read as floor-holding "I'm thinking," never as a substantive answer (v0.3.5).** "Okay." was removed from the pool because a partner hears it as the user's actual reply to a greeting rather than as a stall. This is a **third** standing constraint alongside the two recorded under "Placeholders are gated by PARTNER SILENCE" (partner-statement independent; declarative and first-person).

**No response may begin with an empty interjection (v0.3.9).** "Ah / Oh / Um / Er / Well / So / Hmm / You know" are forbidden openings — get to the point. DISPREFERRED still opens with a softener, but one that **carries content** ("I'd love to, but…", "I wish I could —"), never a bare interjection.

**Hiding the keyboard on `pointerdown` reflows the page, so whatever slides under the pointer catches the trailing click (v0.2.24).** Tapping the keyboard's Hide button closed About Me: freeing the keyboard's space let the **Done** button slide into the exact spot Hide had occupied, and the tap's trailing click landed on it. The fix pattern is `suppressNextClick()` — a one-shot capturing listener that swallows the ghost click. Applies to any control that changes layout on `pointerdown`.

**The `<dialog>` `close` event is unreliable here (v0.2.17).** It fired for neither a programmatic `close()` nor an observed dismissal, so dialog cleanup runs **explicitly in the Close handler, plus a `cancel` listener for Escape** — never on `close`.

**`display: flex` on a `<dialog>` breaks it two ways (v0.2.9).** It drops the UA's auto-centering (restore with `inset: 0; margin: auto`); and on the *base* selector it also overrides the UA's `dialog:not([open]) { display: none }` — author origin beats UA regardless of specificity — so the panel is CSS-visible at all times, appears at startup, and Close looks like it does nothing. **Scope the flex layout to `dialog[open]`.**

**A drag handle needs `touch-action: none` (v0.2.8).** Without it the browser claims the gesture for scroll/pan and a touch drag moves the panel only a few millimeters.

**The app updates itself on launch and on Start (v0.2.10), by three mechanisms that all had to be present:** the service worker refetches with `cache: 'no-cache'`, so revalidation beats the GitHub Pages `max-age`; a `controllerchange` listener reloads once when a new worker takes control (guarded against the first-ever install, and against loops); and `handleStart` calls `reg.update()` so a redeploy reaches a session that was left open.

**"What's new" notes are BUNDLED, never fetched (v0.5.62).** `CHANGELOG.md` is the source of truth and `scripts/apply-release-notes.mjs` embeds it into `app/js/whats-new.js` at release time, so the notice works offline and the freshly-loaded app carries its own notes. **The gating rule is that a version cannot announce itself:** a null `lastSeenVersion` records the current version silently, so the release that introduces the feature shows nothing. `settings-help.json` later copied this arrangement.

## UI Layout Rules (Ken, June 21 2026) — living ruleset; layouts selectable in Settings

Ken is establishing the rules **every conversation-screen layout must obey, BEFORE choosing a concrete layout** (the 2×2-grid-vs-single-row palette decision is explicitly **on hold**). Expectation: **multiple selectable base layouts** — like the on-screen keyboard's 20 layouts, the user picks the conversation layout in Settings. **This list will grow as we work through the UI.**

1. **Static layouts.** The target users often have **limited motor control**; a **keyguard** (physical overlay with holes over the touch targets) makes them far more effective — and a keyguard is by nature **static** (fixed target positions/sizes). So the **base conversation UI must be static**: fixed geometry, **no reflow**. *(Compatible with the v0.4.2 proportional scaling — sizes scale with the screen, but the arrangement/positions stay fixed. "Static" = no dynamic reflow, NOT "fixed pixels"; card POSITIONS are fixed.)* The chosen layout is selectable in Settings.
   - **Spatial Stability is TWO requirements with a clear priority order, and they must not be conflated (Ken, July 27 2026).** **(1) FIRST and foremost: a keyguard must be usable at all.** That is a constraint on **geometry** — the number, position, and size of the cells — because a physical overlay's holes have to line up with touch targets, full stop. This is the hard requirement; violating it doesn't degrade the experience, it makes the device unusable for a keyguard user. **(2) SECOND, and weaker: stability of *what occupies* a given location**, for motor planning — the user learning "my 'yes' is the third hole." **Priority: (2) is LESS important than putting a response the user can select quickly in front of them.** So swapping the CONTENT of a cell to offer a faster path to a response is a legitimate trade; changing the GRID is not. *This is what licensed the dynamic choice chips (July 27 2026): they change which phrase sits behind a hole for one turn, but the cell count/positions/sizes are pixel-identical with 0, 3, or 4 chips, so requirement (1) is untouched.* When weighing a future "should this be reserved or dynamic?" question, ask which of the two requirements is actually at stake — a reserved-real-estate answer is only *required* when the alternative would move the geometry.

   **Reserved real estate (Ken, June 21 2026):** because the layout is static, the card slots are shown **EMPTY — space set aside — even before the conversation starts.** Options fill the already-present slots; the grid never materializes, grows, or collapses when options arrive or clear. So the keyguard holes always line up and nothing ever moves under the user. *(Implication for the current build: the palette region must always render its fixed slot cards (empty/inactive at rest), NOT the single "Response options will appear here" placeholder that changes the layout — change this when we build the first concrete layout; the slot COUNT/arrangement waits on the held 2×2-vs-row decision.)* **Corollary:** less-frequently-used features that **can** be implemented as **modal overlays should be** — keep the static base screen lean and push infrequent functions to modals (Settings & About Me already are; candidates: openers/Start-conversation, closers/Wind-down, repair-of-self, possibly the composer).
2. **Landscape only (until the field complains).** AAC apps are almost always used in landscape; we assume landscape and do NOT engineer for portrait until a real field complaint. **Consequence: vertical space is the precious resource** — spend it deliberately; horizontal space is comparatively abundant. (Reinforces why the palette single-row vs 2×2 choice matters, and why icon-only buttons (rule 4) help.)
3. **Non-editable items are labels, not text boxes.** Anything the user can't edit — partner speech/transcript, status — is a **label**, never a text input. *(Current build already renders the transcript as a `<p>` and status as a `<div>` — good; audit the rest as layouts are built.)*
4. **Buttons are icon-only.** Anything that **can** be a button shows **just an icon, no text description** (saves space — especially vertical — and aids fast recognition). **Retain accessible names** (`aria-label`/tooltip) for screen readers and learning; only the *visible* control is the icon. **Split CONFIRMED (Ken, June 21 2026):** the move/response cards are **EXEMPT** — they exist to show the response text the user reads/speaks, so they are content, not icon-buttons. The icon-only rule targets the **control buttons**: Listen, Say again, Hold on, Pardon?, Wind down, Start/End conversation, regenerate ("Show me different options"), composer Speak/Reframe/Clear. **"Show me different options" specifically becomes an icon BUTTON** (e.g. a ↻ glyph), not the current long thin full-width box. *(Needs an icon vocabulary — a dedicated pass when we build the first concrete layout.)*
5. **Button size ∝ frequency of use.** Bigger buttons for more-frequently-used actions, smaller for rarer ones — the common case gets the easiest target (motor control + Fitts's law).
6. **A "sticky" (latched/toggled-on) action is shown as a selected button** — the engaged state uses the "pressed/selected" button styling so the user can see what is currently on. (e.g. Listen while capturing; auto-resume if surfaced as a toggle.)
7. **Related concepts sit close together.** Group controls belonging to the same idea adjacently — e.g. the partner statement and the "Pardon? / please repeat" control near each other.
8. **Modal-action assets superimpose over the elements that are inert during that action (Ken, June 21 2026).** When a modal action runs — the first being **"In my own words"** (this **confirms the composer is a modal action**, not part of the static base screen, per rule 1's "push infrequent features to modals") — its asset (the on-screen **keyboard**) is **superimposed over the base screen elements that cannot be invoked during that action**, covering the inert controls while any element still usable during the modal remains. This is how a **static physical keyguard coexists with modals** (the rule-1 tension): because the base layout is static and keyguard-backed, the modal's assets must **align to the same keyguard grid/holes**, so the one physical keyguard works in both the base and the modal state. *(Open: which base elements stay live during the compose modal — e.g. End conversation, the partner transcript label — define per modal.)*
9. **Frequently-used phrases/words panel — a BASE UI element + a new configurable set (Ken, June 21 2026; corrects the earlier draft that wrongly put it in the compose modal).** A panel of the user's frequent words/phrases that is **part of the static base layout** — NOT a modal asset, and **NOT an "In my own words" starter** (it does not feed the composer; it **speaks directly**, single/double-tap per rule 10). Its buttons **show the word/phrase as text**, **truncated with an ellipsis** when too long — so, like the response/move cards, these are **content buttons EXEMPT from the icon-only rule (4)**. Joins the family of **user-owned configurable sets** (placeholders, openers, closers, keyboard layouts → and now frequent phrases/words); gets its own editor + data file later. **Starter sets seeded in `app/js/fast-phrases.js`** (CORE/MINIMAL/SOCIAL/REGULATE) modeled on `keyboard-layouts.js` — content only; not yet wired into a layout.
   - **THE keyguard-overlay principle (Ken, June 21 2026) — this is *why* it's modeled on keyboard layouts, and it ties rules 8 + 9 together.** The phrases panel must be **grid-congruent with a keyboard layout** so a **single static keyguard overlays BOTH**: fast-phrase buttons show through the holes at rest, and the compose-modal keyboard's keys show through the **same holes** during "In my own words" (rule 8). Therefore the panel sits **where the keyboard will appear — same side (the keyboard's left/right Setting) and the same grid — NOT opposite it** (this supersedes my earlier "opposite side, two columns" guess AND the later "its side is undecided" note: it's the *keyboard's* side, sharing the grid).
