Phrases fill the paired keyboard layout's cells **1:1** (one phrase per key-cell, ellipsis when long), so the holes line up. The phrase *content* (fast-phrases.js) is separable from the *geometry* (which keyboard layout it pairs with); the pairing is bound in the held base-layout pass. **Likely consequence to weigh there:** this favors a **side-docked** compose keyboard for the conversation screen (a side column preserves the precious vertical space — rule 2 — and gives the phrases panel a natural home), reconsidering the older "bottom dock during conversation" default.
10. **Activation safeguard — speak on single tap vs. double tap, configurable, with a configurable double-tap interval (Ken, June 21 2026).** A spoken action (first applied to the frequent-phrases/words buttons) either **speaks immediately on a single tap** or **requires a double tap** — user-configurable — to manage **false hits** from limited motor control; the **double-tap interval** is itself a Settings value (so it can be tuned to the user). *(Likely generalizes beyond the phrases panel to other speak/commit targets — e.g. selecting a response card — confirm scope. Connects to the existing Response-Option-Display note about a double-tap confirming a selection.)*

11. **Always strive for FIXED-dimension UI elements (Ken, June 21 2026).** Prefer fixed (device-deterministic) sizes over content-sized/auto layouts — an element that's larger than its content just carries blank space, which is fine; what's NOT fine is a size that changes with content or that reaches into another element's space. **The end goal: the Settings panel (and other overlays) match the size + location of a base-UI element so that ONE physical keyguard supports EVERY screen** (conversation, Settings, About Me). First step taken (v0.5.9): the Settings panel is fixed-size, and for a bottom keyboard dock it's a fixed full-width **top-region** panel matching the conversation main area (clears the keyboard); for a side dock it's the fixed centered panel. *(This corrects a brief auto-height experiment — auto/content sizing is exactly what to avoid.)*

12. **Constant (non-customizable) buttons are icon-only with an explanatory tooltip (Ken, July 8 2026).** Restates and generalizes Rule 4 as the governing test: if a button's function is **fixed** (the user can't edit what it does/says), its face shows **only an icon** and it carries an explanatory accessible name (`aria-label` + `title` tooltip) — Listen, Start/End conversation, the repeat/hold/pardon/wind-down overrides, Settings, regenerate ("New N"), the composer Speak/Reframe/Cancel, **and "In my own words"** (the Express Panel space cell — a constant control, so it now shows a compose/pencil icon, not its text label). The contrapositive is the exemption: buttons whose face is **user content** — the response/Response-Palette cards (AI-authored text the user reads/speaks) and the Express Panel phrase/partner/feeling buttons (user-editable) — keep their **text**. **Applies across all conversation modes** (the base screen is static, so a button's face is constant across LISTENING/RESPONDING/REPAIR/INITIATING/PRE-CLOSING anyway). *Scope note:* the Settings & About Me overlays (the keyguard's "open window", supporter-assisted, infrequent) keep text buttons for readability — the rule targets the keyguard-backed conversation control surface; revisit if Ken wants those iconified too. The pre-start **Start** button stays text (onboarding affordance).

13. **No zoom, anywhere in the app (Ken, July 31 2026) — a corollary of Rule 1, and the app must MEASURE from the layout viewport.** Pinch-to-zoom and double-tap-to-zoom are disabled. A zoomed page and a physical keyguard are incompatible by definition: the holes are cut in plastic and cannot scale, so any zoom — in practice an accidental one — moves every control out from under its hole. **This is not the accessibility loss it would be in an ordinary page:** text size is a per-surface user setting (Settings → Text Size) and button size is its own setting, which serves the same need without breaking the overlay.
    - **Blocking it takes THREE mechanisms because no one of them covers every platform.** (a) The viewport meta (`maximum-scale=1, user-scalable=no`) — honored by Android/desktop, and **deliberately IGNORED by iOS Safari since iOS 10** on accessibility grounds, so it is *not* the mechanism that matters on the iPad. (b) **`touch-action: pan-x pan-y`** on `body` and on `#appKeyboard` — allows scrolling in both directions and nothing else, dropping BOTH zoom gestures. **NOT `manipulation`**, which is the more familiar value but only drops *double-tap* and still permits pinch — `#appKeyboard` had exactly that hole in it, on the surface most likely to be touched with two fingers by accident. Because `touch-action` resolves as the intersection down the ancestor chain, a laxer value on any descendant re-opens the gesture. (c) **`blockZoomGestures()`** in `app.js` — `preventDefault` on Safari's non-standard `gesturestart`/`gesturechange`/`gestureend` (the only thing that works on iOS) plus multi-touch `touchmove` for Chromium touchscreens. Single-touch is untouched, so scrolling and every button behave exactly as before.
    - **The measurement half, and why it is the load-bearing part: never size or measure the UI from `window.innerWidth`/`innerHeight`.** In Safari (iPad included) those report the **visual** viewport, which shrinks under pinch, *and* `devicePixelRatio` scales with the zoom. Use **`document.documentElement.clientWidth/clientHeight`** (the layout viewport, which zoom cannot move; identical to `innerWidth` where there is no zoom, so it is a no-op on desktop) — the helpers `layoutVW()`/`layoutVH()` in `app.js`. **The bug this fixed:** `applyButtonSizing()` sized the regions from `innerWidth` and baked the result into `--conv-dock-w` as a **px value**, recomputed only on resize or a settings change — so one run while zoomed permanently shrank the Express Panel and un-zooming did not put it back. That is Ken's "the Express Panel kept shrinking as I worked in Settings; restarting restored it."
    - **Anything that measures the screen for a physical artifact must refuse to run while zoomed** rather than emit a plausible-looking wrong file — the error only shows up after the plastic is cut. `generateScreenOpenings` checks `zoomScale()` and states the screenshot size its coordinates assume (a **resized screenshot** — mail clients shrink attachments by default — produces exactly this misalignment, growing with distance from the top-left, and is otherwise invisible).
    - **CONFIRMED on the iPad (Ken, July 31 2026): disabling zoom fixed BOTH symptoms** — the Express Panel stopped shrinking, and the keyguard openings now line up. **The diagnostic lesson is worth more than the fix: "the layout was displayed properly when I generated it" did NOT rule out zoom, and I wrongly treated it as though it did.** A zoomed page looks *correct* — every element keeps its relative position and proportion, it is simply larger — so the screen can look perfect while `devicePixelRatio` and `innerWidth` are both silently wrong and every measurement taken from them is uniformly off. **When geometry is wrong but the screen looks right, suspect the measurement basis, not the layout.**

14. **No per-control help text in the app — explanation lives in the manuals (Ken, August 1 2026).** *"There's way too much explanatory (hint) text in the Settings panel. Remove all text associated with individual controls. Text describing a settings tab as a whole can stay."* Applied: 38 hint paragraphs removed from `index.html`, plus the per-control help generated in `app.js` (the data-folder hint, the Deepgram cost line, the screen-openings destination, the end-practice note), `control-phrases-editor.js` (all six section descriptions and the "Say again" note) and `express-editor.js` (the insert-position hint).
    - **A tooltip is NOT the fallback, and this is the load-bearing reason.** A tooltip needs hover; this app targets **direct select**, so a tapping user can never summon one. There is therefore no in-app home for per-control help that does not spend the panel space the removal exists to reclaim — which is what sends it to the manuals rather than to some cheaper in-app affordance.
    - **The three categories that STAY, because they are not explanation:** (a) **tab-level descriptions** (Practice, Express Panel, Controls) — explicitly kept; (b) **live status regions** (storage durability, settings-profile and backup status, partner-voice status, the error/voice counts, the usage-since date) — these report what just happened or what is currently true, which a manual cannot do; (c) **empty-state messages** (e.g. the Express Panel editor's "No items yet"). When adding a control, the test is *"does this text say what the control IS, or what is happening RIGHT NOW?"* — the first goes in the manual, the second stays.
    - **The removed text was staged in `SETTINGS-HELP-TEXT.md`**, verbatim and grouped by tab, so the manual pass lost nothing. **That file was staging, not a permanent record, and was deleted in `22d47a1` once both manuals carried it** — recover it with `git show 13fa157:SETTINGS-HELP-TEXT.md` if the wording is ever needed again. Several entries are platform-split (the Speech tab especially), and per the DOC-SYNC standing rule both manuals must be updated in the same pass.
    - **Do not reintroduce per-control prose.** A new setting ships with a label clear enough to stand alone, and its explanation goes to the manuals in the same change.
    - **AMENDED August 2 2026 — what this rule forbids is per-control TEXT ON SCREEN, and spoken help satisfies it.** The rule's reasoning was that a tooltip needs hover, which a direct-select user cannot produce, so there was no in-app home for per-control help that did not spend the panel space the removal existed to reclaim. **Tap-then-tap needs no hover and audio costs no space**, so the Settings "?" (Rule 17) is the affordance the rule was missing, not a reversal of it. A new setting now ships with a spoken-help entry in the same change — and a test enforces it. The panel stays exactly as decluttered as Ken asked.

15. **The Express Panel button is the size reference for every other button in the app (Ken, August 2 2026).** *"I'd like the size of the buttons in the Express Panel to drive the size of all other buttons in the UI — that's because the user has the most control over an 'acceptable' button size in the Express Panel (and the keyboard)."* The reasoning is the load-bearing part: the Express Panel and keyboard are where the user actually tunes size to their own motor control, so a button there is the one size in the app we *know* they have accepted. Everything else should inherit from it rather than from a number we picked.
    - **The ratio is 70%, both axes, and NOT 100% (Ken's own qualification).** An Express cell is also a function of how many keys the chosen layout shows at once, so a 3-column side layout produces very wide cells that no Settings button needs to match.
    - **`--btn-min-dim` remains the absolute floor, via `max()` — do not replace it.** Measured at the bottom-dock default: an Express cell is **54 px** tall while the standing floor is 4rem ≈ **56 px**, so a bare 70% rule (37.8 px) would have made Settings buttons *smaller* than they already were. The larger of the two always wins, which also means the rule can only ever increase a target.
    - **Derived from CSS vars, never measured.** `--ep-cell-w/h` are computed in `styles.css` from `--kbd-rows`/`--kbd-cols`, `--dock-w`/`--dock-h`, `--grid-gap` and `--safe-bottom` — the same numbers the dock grid lays out from — so they are correct before the panel has rendered, while the keyboard covers it, and while Settings sits over it, none of which a `getBoundingClientRect()` survives. Verified exact against the rendered panel in both docks (bottom: derived 142.2 × 54 vs measured 142 × 54; side: 76.8 × 102.9 vs 77 × 103).
    - **Width is capped by a PERCENTAGE, which is what makes it self-adjusting.** `min-width: max(--btn-min-dim, min(--ui-btn-min-w, 45%))` — the percentage resolves against the *column box* under the bottom dock's 3-column Settings panel and against the content box under the side dock's single column, so a wide Express cell can never force a control wider than the column holding it, with no JS and no knowledge of the column count. In the pathological case (side dock at maximum button size, Settings content 84 px wide) the cap clamps a 148 px floor down to 56 px and the action rows wrap; measured horizontal overflow is **identical with and without the rule**.
    - **One deliberate exception: the − / + slider steppers**, which get the height floor and a square `--btn-min-dim` width instead. It is geometric, not a preference — two steppers at the full Express-derived width would leave the slider in the same row no room to drag.
    - **This also fixed a gap nobody had noticed: `<select>` was matched by none of the button rules** (they all say `button`), so every dropdown in Settings was **33 px** tall, the smallest control in the app. Selects are now covered.
    - **EXTENDED August 2 2026 to the Settings TITLE-BAR buttons, reversing their exemption (Ken).** *"The help and close buttons on the Settings tab should also be sensitive to the minimum button height/width. Otherwise it becomes easy to hit the close button when you really want to hit the help button. That will make the Settings title bar taller."* The old exemption traded target size for panel height and was defensible **while the X was alone up there** — a mis-hit had nothing to land on. Adding the **?** beside it changed the sum: the likely mis-hit is now Help → Close, which does not merely do the wrong thing, it **dismisses the panel and loses the user's place**. They are now **square at `--ui-btn-min-h`** (2.1rem → 56 px at the bottom-dock default, 72 px on a side dock), square rather than `--ui-btn-min-w` wide because they are icon buttons and because two of them must still fit when a wide side dock squeezes the panel. The header grows 37 px → 65 px, which Ken accepted up front. **The gap between them has a floor** (`max(--grid-gap, 0.4rem)`): they are transparent at rest, so with `--grid-gap` at its default 0 nothing at all would separate two unmarked targets.
    - **⚠ REGRESSION FOUND AND FIXED THE SAME DAY — Rule 15 had been silently DEAD since the screen-edge-margin commit.** An edit closed the `--ep-cell-*` comment and then left two more prose lines ending in a **second `*/`**. CSS has no nested comments, so the parser read `…that axis. */ body.conv-bottom` as one invalid selector and **dropped the entire rule** — `--ep-cell-w/h` were never set, `--ui-btn-min-w/h` fell back to `--btn-min-dim`, and every Express-derived size quietly collapsed to its floor (the Copy button went 100 px → 56 px). **No error, no console warning, and the app looked completely normal.** It surfaced only because a measurement came back 56 when it should have been 100.
        - **A dropped CSS rule is invisible by construction, which is why it now has a tripwire:** `tests/styles.test.mjs` checks comment nesting, brace balance, and that the load-bearing tokens are still declared — including that *some* `body.conv-bottom` / `body.conv-side` rule still sets `--ep-cell-*`. Verified to catch the real bug and name the line. *(Writing it also caught a bug in itself: `indexOf('body.conv-bottom {')` finds the FIRST such rule, which sets `--dock-h`, not the one being checked — hence the "search all blocks" form.)*
        - **⚠ ITS MIRROR IMAGE, and check this one FIRST because it costs nothing to rule out (August 20 2026): in the preview pane a TRANSITIONED property reads as its START value forever.** When the Browser pane is hidden the page stops compositing frames, so a transition is queued and never advances — `getComputedStyle` keeps returning the pre-change value however long you wait, and **even an inline `!important` reads as unchanged**, which looks impossible and is the tell. Properties NOT in the `transition` list apply instantly, so one rule appears to half-apply: `#privacyBtn.private-on` reported an amber border and white text on a white fill, i.e. an invisible glyph on the privacy toggle. **There was no bug.** Set `transition: none` inline and re-read before believing any transitioned value — background, box-shadow, opacity, transform. *(A property with a running `animation` does resolve, which is why the Listen button measured correctly and the privacy button did not — the difference is not the CSS.)*
        - **The general lesson: when a measurement disagrees with the CSS you just read, suspect that the rule is not applying at all** rather than that the maths is wrong. Same shape as the July 31 zoom finding — the screen looked right while the numbers were silently wrong.

16. **"Screen edge margin" — the whole app insets from the physical screen edges, DOCK INCLUDED, because a tight case opening leaves a keyguard nothing to sit on (Ken, August 2 2026).** *"Some tablet cases have openings that fit very tightly to the active area of the screen. In order to create a keyguard for these cases, there needs to be a way to increase the space around the outside of the app."* Settings → **Keyguard Design**, `appMarginPos`, default **0** (unchanged appearance), slider 0–3rem.
    - **The distinction from "Keyboard separation" is the whole point, and conflating them is the mistake to avoid.** Keyboard separation is an **internal** gap (dock ↔ main content) and deliberately does **NOT** move the dock — that is why it never disturbed the keyguard. Screen edge margin deliberately **DOES** move the dock: the Express Panel and keyboard sit hardest against the screen edge, so a margin that spared them would not clear the case lip it exists for. *(This was reached the hard way: a first cut extended Keyboard separation to pad the transcript and response regions, which Ken then corrected — the regions were never the issue, the perimeter was, and the dock has to come with it. That cut was reverted in full.)*
    - **It therefore MOVES EVERY KEYGUARD HOLE, and that is intended.** Setup-tier, re-cuts the keyguard, exactly like button size — the opposite of the two separation controls beside it. `generateScreenOpenings` reads live element positions, so it picks the new geometry up with no change.
    - **The load-bearing implementation detail: the solver budgets against the REDUCED viewport.** `applyButtonSizing` computes `VW/VH` as `layoutVW/VH() − 2 × appMargin` before deriving anything. Insetting in CSS alone would have pushed the fixed dock back over the margin instead of shrinking it into what is left — verified: at a 29 px margin the side dock comes out **366 px = exactly 30% of the reduced viewport**, and the outermost Express button sits 29 px off every screen edge.
    - **Where it is applied, and why each one is needed:** `<main>` padding (covers the main area and, being an in-flow flex child, the bottom dock); the **side** dock explicitly (`position: fixed`, so `<main>`'s padding cannot reach it); `#settingsDialog`'s `inset` (Rule 11 — one keyguard must overlay the conversation screen *and* Settings, so they cannot disagree); the side/bottom dock reservations, which add it back on the edge where a longhand overrides the shorthand; and the `--ep-cell-*` derivations, whose unconstrained axis is now the viewport less twice the margin.
    - **The two controls compose without doubling** (Ken: *"where ever it is already applied, it should not add more spacing"*) — measured with both non-zero: dock 29 px off the screen edge, main content 22 px off the dock, never 51.

17. **Spoken help in Settings — tap the "?", then tap a control to hear what it does (Ken, August 2 2026). BUILT.** The affordance UI Layout Rule 14 was missing: per-control explanation with no on-screen text. `help-mode.js` + `settings-help.js`; 85 phrases; **adding a Settings control without a spoken-help entry fails `tests/settings-help.test.mjs`**, so this cannot rot.
    - **The words live at the project ROOT in [`settings-help.json`](settings-help.json) and are NEVER deployed.** `scripts/apply-settings-help.mjs` embeds them into `app/js/settings-help.js` between markers — the same arrangement as `CHANGELOG.md` → `whats-new.js`. Edit the JSON, re-run the generator; **the test compares the two and fails on drift**, so a forgotten regeneration cannot ship quietly (that trap has bitten this project before, which is why the guard exists rather than a note).
    - **Write for LISTENING.** A listener cannot skim or re-read, so entries are 15–25 words and lead with what the control *does* — the user has just tapped it and can see its name. The test caps at 35 words. **Do not paste from the manuals**; several of those entries run past eighty words.
    - **The safety property, enforced in the CAPTURE phase: an armed tap must never change the control.** A checkbox that flips while you ask what it does is worse than no help, and this population taps imprecisely — the very reason the feature is attractive. Focus is suppressed too: reaching a text field would raise the on-screen keyboard over the panel being asked about. Verified live: a select does not open, a range does not move, a text field does not focus.
    - **Barge-out and the same-target exception (Ken).** Any tap during playback stops the voice — *"a natural way for people to indicate that they're done early"*. **Tapping the SAME control or group also lets the action through; anywhere else is swallowed.** Principled, not merely convenient: *the tap you can trust is the one that already landed on target deliberately*; a tap elsewhere is the one that might be a stray, which is what the swallow protects against. Safe because **every destructive Settings button already routes through `confirmDanger()`** — including Import and Restore, which confirm one level down in `importPackageText` — so a pass-through can at worst raise the red card.
    - **CARVE-OUT: sliders never pass through.** On `<input type="range">` the tap COORDINATE is the value, so "stop talking" and "set this to 90%" are the same gesture. Rule: pass-through only where a tap is a **discrete** action. It costs nothing — every slider's **− / +** steppers are discrete and do pass through (verified live).
    - **One-shot, not sticky (Ken's reasoning, better than mine):** help disarms after each answer *"to avoid the situation where the user only wants to be reminded about one setting's usage… but can't figure out how to turn off the help message so that they can actually modify the value."* Being trapped in help mode is worse for this population than an extra tap. The `?` shows **armed** (Rule 6) and **speaking** — the second state is not decoration: without it a swallowed tap reads as the app being broken.
    - **Tapping a TAB speaks its purpose and does NOT select it** (Ken). One-shot means the next tap navigates.
    - **Voice: the PRACTICE PARTNER's, on every platform and BOTH backends (Ken: *"This sounds like the right thing to do regardless of platform"*).** Help passes exactly what `advancePracticePartner` passes — `{ voiceURI: pickPartnerVoice(), auraModel: pickAuraPartnerVoice() }`.
        - **Why it generalizes:** help and the practice partner have the same two requirements, and they pull against each other — the voice must be audibly **not the user's** (or the app explaining itself sounds like the user saying it) and it must be **intelligible**. `pickPartnerVoice` already resolves that tension: excludes novelty voices, prefers a different voice in the *same language*, falls back to the user's own when no usable alternative exists. One selection serves both features, with no third voice and nothing extra to configure.
        - **The iPad made the tension visible; it did not cause it.** Ken's original catch — *"there's only one non-silly voice available on an iPad. If they choose the one non-silly voice as their own, then a tool tip would be read in what might be an unintelligible voice"* — corrected my recommendation of a plain device voice. But "any voice that is not theirs" is wrong on Windows too, just less catastrophically, which is why the rule is platform-agnostic rather than an iPad branch.
        - **The first cut got the paid path wrong, and Ken's remark is what surfaced it:** it passed only `voiceURI`, which `tts.speak` ignores on the Deepgram backend — so a user on a paid voice heard help in their **own Aura voice**, intelligible but indistinguishable from themselves, losing half the point. Verified after the fix that the help call and the practice call are character-identical.
        - **Consequence, so it is not a surprise:** help and the practice partner sound alike, and **Settings → Speech → "Practice partner voice" changes both.** That coupling is the user's only lever over the help voice; decoupling would leave them none.
        - **Cost is explicitly not a concern (Ken) — do not re-raise it.**
    - **THE BUG WORTH REMEMBERING: one tap fires `pointerdown` AND `mousedown` AND `click`.** Deciding on each ran the action three times, so the "?" armed and instantly disarmed itself and looked completely dead. Only the first event of a gesture decides (`gestureDecided`); the rest re-apply that decision. Any future capture-phase interception on this surface has the same trap.
    - **Scope: Settings only** (Ken: *"until we see real value elsewhere. I don't want anything in the conversation UI that could slow or trip someone up."*).

18. **Settings tabs are ONE column, and each heading is a collapsible section (Ken, August 11 2026). This REPLACES the August 3 2026 rule, which made a tab drop to one column only when its controls would overlap; multi-column is gone entirely and the overlap measurement with it.** Ken: *"the two-column, reflow, layouts don't work well. Let's do these kinds of tabs as a single column. Then, treat every section as an expandable/collapsible section like the 'How I talk with them' section… the top-most section is expanded by default and the others default to collapsed. Expanding a section doesn't cause the other sections on that tab to collapse. The expanded/collapsed status of a section is remembered within a session. A new session begins with the tab sections in their default state."*
    - **Columns existed for one reason — fitting the denser tabs without scrolling, which matters for this audience — and sections buy that back without the two costs.** Reflow moved controls around as the panel resized, so nothing stayed where it was learned; and multi-column has a failure mode with no symptom, **an item too wide for its column is not shrunk, it is painted across the gap ON TOP of the next column's controls**, with the panel not scrolling and no width looking wrong. *Keep that failure recorded even though the layout is gone — it is the reason not to reach for multi-column again.*
    - **ALL sections start CLOSED (Ken, August 11 2026, explicitly retracting his own earlier "the top-most section is expanded by default").** A tab then opens as a plain list of what is on it, with nothing already claiming the space, and every tab behaves identically.
    - **EVERY control is inside a section (Ken, corrected the same day): *"all controls should be included in a section. If no other section makes sense then create one just for that control."*** A first cut left the five lone self-labelling controls inline, on the reasoning that they have no heading and inventing one would print their own words twice. **The right answer is a heading naming the TOPIC rather than repeating the control** — "Full screen" over the *Use the whole screen* checkbox, "Saving conversations" — which costs no duplication and leaves nothing loose between sections. Where two singletons were genuinely one idea they were merged instead (*Resume listening automatically* + *Play a chime* → **Listening**). Guarded by a test: a group with no heading now fails.
    - **SECTIONS NEST, and a section may hold sub-sections AND loose controls — the combination carries meaning (Ken, August 11 2026).** *"Supporting sub-sections means that the Reset sizes button doesn't need to be placed into its own section. It will be tabbed as much as the other three subsections which implies that it affects the other three."* So **"Button sizes and gaps"** holds *Button size / Button spacing / Minimum spacing* as sub-sections plus **"Reset buttons and gaps to default" as a loose control at the same indent** — and that shared indent is what says it acts on all three, where a heading of its own would have made it look like a fourth setting beside them. **This is a layout idiom to reuse, not a one-off**: the same shape was applied to **"Screen openings"** (*Title Bar Height* as a sub-section, *Generate Screen Openings* loose), which is the other place a control acts on its siblings. `buildSections()` recurses into each body, so depth is not limited to two.
    - **Indentation is what makes a heading ENCLOSE rather than merely precede** (Ken's word). The body is indented under the arrow with a 2px rule down its left; a sub-section's heading is lighter and smaller than its parent's but keeps the full `--btn-min-dim` height, because it is still a tap target. Measured: parent heading 148px, its body 159px, sub-headings 173px, **the loose Reset button also 173px** — the property that carries the meaning.
    - **⚠ ALL-CLOSED BREAKS EVERY "TAKE ME TO THAT SETTING" JUMP, AND THE BREAKAGE IS SILENT — the control still works, it just stops arriving.** The pre-start **"Add API key to Settings"** button landed on the General tab with the API Key section shut, so it promised the field and delivered a list of headings (Ken asked; confirmed). `revealSetting(id)` opens the section holding a control **and every section above it**, since a sub-section is no use opened inside a shut parent. It deliberately does **not focus** the control — focusing a text field raises the on-screen keyboard over the panel, which is why `openSettings` parks focus on the header. **Any future jump into Settings carries the same obligation.** *(The Express tap-to-define jump is unaffected: the Express tab is an editor with no sections.)*
    - **⚠ A SECTION HOLDING SUB-SECTIONS NEEDS ITS OWN `data-help`**, because `resolveTap` collects *every* control under the `.setting-group` it landed in — sub-sections included — so the "they all mean the same thing" fallback cannot fire. The test parser had to learn nesting for the same reason: splitting markup on the opening tag attributed a loose control that *follows* a sub-section to that sub-section instead of to its parent, so it now walks div depth and subtracts direct children. Verified it fails when a parent's `data-help` is removed.
    - **⚠ THE HEADING MUST BE A `<span>`, NOT THE GROUP'S `<label>` — this is the whole finding, and it is invisible until someone taps the words.** Moving the label up as the title looked perfect and was half-dead: **a click whose target is a `<label>` inside a `<summary>` does not toggle the details.** That is the spec'd activation behaviour, so a form can live in a summary. The arrow and the grey background opened the section and *the title did not* — Ken found it immediately. The heading is therefore a span carrying the label's **text**, the label is removed, and the control it named keeps its accessible name through `aria-labelledby` (verified on five controls). Two things follow that a rebuild must keep: **only the text becomes the title, so any element nested in the heading has to be let out first** or the id it carries is destroyed and whatever writes to it fails silently (the error-log count did exactly this; it now sits outside the heading in the markup as well); and this also removes the older hazard the label form had, that `<label for>` inside a summary **forwards the click to its control** and would have raised the on-screen keyboard over the panel the user was only trying to open.
    - **The arrow is 1.9rem, not the 1.2rem inherited from About Me** (Ken: it "should be larger"). At the smaller size it read as punctuation rather than as the control it is. Fixed width so titles line up whether open or closed. The size is shared with "How I talk with them", which wanted it too.
    - **THE WRAPPING LIVES IN [`sections.js`](app/js/sections.js), shared by the Settings tabs and the CONTROLS editor**, which builds its sections at runtime instead of declaring them in `index.html`. Ken: *"I think it can be organized into sections by the heading label. Right?"* — right, and its six `.cpe-section` groups already carry exactly one heading each.
    - **⚠ I HAD TALKED KEN OUT OF THIS ON A FALSE PREMISE and it is worth naming, because the same mistake is easy to repeat: I said the Controls editor "rebuilds its whole contents on every keystroke".** It does not — `commit(false)` on a text edit deliberately skips the re-render so the field keeps focus, and only add / reorder / delete rebuild. **Check which commits re-render before citing a rebuild as an obstacle.**
    - **OPEN STATE IS KEYED BY POSITION WITHIN A NAMED SCOPE, not held in the DOM.** The first cut used the DOM as the memory, which is fine for panels that are built once and wrong for an editor that rebuilds itself: an add or a reorder would have slammed every section shut mid-edit. **Position, not title**, because the Buttons & Keyboard tab has two sections both called "Express Panel & keyboard layout" and a title key would tie them together. Verified that an open section survives typing, reordering and adding a card.
    - **⚠ THE CONTROLS HEADINGS SHIPPED SILENT UNDER THE "?" AND THE TEST DID NOT NOTICE — Ken asked, which is the only reason it was found.** Two failures compounded. (a) **The coverage test scans `index.html`**, and these six sections are built at runtime, so they were outside it entirely; it now also reads the section keys out of `control-phrases-editor.js`, and was verified to fail and name the section when one entry is removed. (b) **`resolveTap` invented a key rather than declining.** Its group fallback added the string `'?'` for a control with no id, so a group of *several* id-less fields — which is exactly what an editor builds — collapsed to one bogus key, "resolved", and looked up nothing. **A control that cannot be named must make the group NOT resolve**, so the group is forced to carry its own `data-help`. **The general lesson: a coverage test that reads one file only covers what that file declares** — anything a module builds at runtime is invisible to it and needs its own source scanned.
    - **⚠ IDEMPOTENCE IS BY CONSTRUCTION — a group already holding a `<details>` is skipped — NOT by a flag on the container.** A flag would be exactly wrong here: `controlEditor.render()` wipes `innerHTML`, and a `dataset` attribute set on the container itself **survives that**, so the second render would silently skip wrapping and the tab would come back a flat list. *(Verified: nine sections on the Buttons tab, still nine after leaving and returning.)*
    - **A section header must SPEAK under the "?" (Rule 17), and that is what the new `resolveTap` fallback is for.** Most sections hold one idea — a slider with its steppers, a select, one radio group — and the control's own phrase already says what the section is for, so writing a second near-identical entry per section would be duplication that can drift. `resolveTap` therefore falls back to the group's controls **only when they all resolve to the same key**; where they disagree the group needs its own `data-help`. All 40 headings resolve to a phrase today, and `tests/settings-help.test.mjs` fails if a new one would not. **The failure it catches is silent** — add a second, unrelated control to a headed group and its header simply stops speaking, with no error and the "?" merely appearing not to work there.
    - **The stress case is unchanged and was already documented: side dock at maximum button size leaves the Settings content 84 px wide and its rows overflow horizontally.** That is pre-existing and content-driven (the API-key row, its buttons) — the section header itself measures 65 px and fits. One column can only ever reduce this, since one column is wider than three.
    - **Per-control hint text stays gone (Rule 14), including on section headings** — that is why "How I talk with them" lost its second line the same day. A section explains itself through the spoken "?", not through prose on screen.

19. **"Use the whole screen" — Settings → General, default ON (Ken, August 3 2026).** Ken asked whether an installed desktop app could launch full screen, and placed the control himself: *"This belongs on the General tab and default on."* Vertical space is the scarce axis (Rule 2), and the reclaimed strip is a PWA title bar plus the OS taskbar — roughly 80px, ~9% of a Surface's height.
    - **It MOVES EVERY KEYGUARD HOLE, which is measured, not assumed.** Comparing 720px and 800px of viewport height: the dock's cells keep their exact size and x positions (142×54) but **translate down by the full height gained** (505→585), and the Command Bar's cells change height (72→80). A keyguard aligns to the physical bezel, so every hole moves. That makes this Setup-tier in effect — the same class as button size and screen edge margin (Rule 16) — even though it sits on General.
    - **Default reversed to OFF after Ken lived with it (August 3 2026), and the reasoning is the useful part.** It shipped ON in 0.6.3; Ken then found he *missed the window title bar* — *"It's the easiest way to close the app and doesn't lose much vertical real estate."* Three things follow, and the third is the one worth keeping. (a) **Fullscreen cannot keep the title bar** — the Fullscreen API is all-or-nothing, so "fullscreen with a title bar" does not exist. (b) **Maximising the window is the better deal for most people**: it costs only the title bar's height and keeps the close button, and Ken sets the **taskbar to auto-hide**, so a maximised window already reaches the screen edges. (c) **Starting OFF teaches the control as an expand/contract toggle** rather than a state the user arrives in and has to find their way out of — which matters because the way out is buried in Settings. **Reinforced Aug 3 2026 (Ken):** the browser's own full-screen notification — *"&lt;address&gt; — To exit full screen, press [Esc]"* — draws the Esc key as **what looks like a tappable button and is not**; it is browser chrome, so a tap does nothing, and a touch-only user has no Esc key to press either. So the one exit instruction the platform volunteers is unusable for the target user, and the checkbox really is the only way back. The app cannot style, move, or suppress that message, and it fires **on Start**, not only on the toggle — so with the setting on it greets every session. *(Tracked for both User Manuals in DOC-SYNC; unmeasured on iPad Safari.)* Stored choices from 0.6.3 are respected either way; only "never touched it" changes meaning.
    - **The consequence for the keyguard is unchanged and still the reason it is a setting at all** — whichever mode the user settles in, the openings file must be generated in that mode, because the geometry is measured live.
    - **Mechanism: the Fullscreen API on the Start tap, capability-gated — ONE path for every platform** (Ken: *"the chosen mechanism needs to support all platforms if possible"*). Standard `requestFullscreen` with WebKit's prefixed spelling behind it; where neither exists the call is a no-op and the app is exactly as it was, per the standing rule that platform differences go behind a capability check and never a fork.
    - **⚠ AMENDED August 3 2026 (Ken, measured on his iPad — iPadOS 26.6 / Safari 26.6): NOT OFFERED ON WebKit. The "ONE path for every platform" ambition above did not survive contact with the device.** Ken: *"I don't see a value in having this setting on iPad."* Measured in both modes: in the **Home Screen app** it does nothing at all — there is no title bar, only the iOS status bar, and **fullscreen does not displace the status bar**, so the layout is identical either way; in a **Safari tab** it actively breaks the app — Settings closes and its button goes dead, the Listen button latches on and cannot be tapped off, and Safari overlays its own persistent exit-**X** (WebKit's counterpart to the Chromium "press Esc" bubble, and unlike that one it is genuinely tappable). **Best current explanation for the dead Settings button, not yet proven: WebKit refuses `showModal()` while the page is fullscreen**, so every later tap fails the same way. That makes it a trap rather than an inconvenience — **the only control that can turn the setting off is the one that stops working.**
        - **`requestAppFullscreen()` returns early on `platform.isIOS()`, and the checkbox is hidden.** Gating the REQUEST is the half that matters: a profile with the setting already saved `true` would otherwise stay broken with its off-switch hidden. In practice that is one device (Ken's), so this is a one-line convenience rather than a safeguard — but it makes the fix land without an ordering dance. On Chromium `isIOS()` is false and every path is exactly as before; verified against real user agents, including that the touch-points test separates an iPad from a **real Mac with a byte-identical UA**.
        - **The general lesson, and it is the same one the probe taught: "capability-gated so it works everywhere" is an ambition, not a verification.** The API is *present* on WebKit and the call *succeeds* — a capability check passes — and the feature is still worthless in one mode and destructive in the other. Presence of an API says nothing about whether the platform's chrome behaves the way the feature assumes.
    - **Why not the manifest.** `display: "fullscreen"` is consulted only when the app is INSTALLED, is reported to fall back to standalone on desktop Chromium, and does nothing for a browser tab — which is how the iPad's Conversation mode runs and how anyone tries the app before installing. The API covers all of those. `display_override: ["fullscreen"]` is declared as well since it costs nothing where ignored, with `display: "standalone"` still the fallback. **The manifest half is unverified on the Surface and on the iPad** — treat it as a hint, not a mechanism, until measured (the 50%-research-error finding applies).
    - **The request must be made SYNCHRONOUSLY, above the first `await` in `handleStart`.** User activation ends at the first await, and a fullscreen request after that is refused. Placed beside `chime.unlock()` and `tts.unlockAudio()`, which are there for the same reason.
    - **The toggle is also the way back in.** Ken dismissed the Esc concern (*"only possible if the user is working with a physical keyboard and if that's the case they likely have a mouse or trackball as well"*), but the change handler applies the setting immediately, and a Settings tap is itself a gesture — so restoring fullscreen never needs a relaunch, for free.
    - **Verified in preview:** default is ON for a profile that has never seen the key (`?? true`, not `!!`); the control is on the General tab, reflects storage, and persists both ways; the layout re-solves on the height change fullscreen produces (`applyButtonSizing` is already on the resize hook). And a *programmatic* Start — an untrusted gesture, so the request is genuinely refused — produced **no console error and no unhandled rejection**, which is the failure the try/catch and `.catch()` exist for.
    - **Consequence for the keyguard "Title Bar Height (px)" field (Ken asked, August 3 2026: *"Is it fair to assume Title Bar Height is 0 if the app is using the whole screen?"*).** Yes — in real fullscreen the page viewport IS the screen, so there is nothing above it and the offset is necessarily 0. But **the field is now a trap, and the trap is created by this feature**: someone who typed 32 for a windowed setup and later turned fullscreen on would emit a Screen Openings file with every opening 32px low — plausible-looking, and discovered only after the plastic is cut. So `generateScreenOpenings` takes the offset from **`isReallyFullscreen()`, not from the field**, and the field is disabled with a live status line while fullscreen is in effect.
      - **Scope of the field, and a correction to how the risk was first stated (Ken, August 3 2026: *"The value of this field should have no effect on the screen layout. It is only an offset used in the creation of the screen openings instructions."*).** Correct, and worth pinning down: the value is read **only** inside `generateScreenOpenings`, it is added **only** to each opening's Y, and it **has no storage accessor at all** — the input carries `value="0"` in the markup and resets on every load. So the "typed 32 for a windowed setup and later turned fullscreen on" framing was **overstated**: it cannot span sessions. The real window is a single session — set a height, turn fullscreen on, then Generate — which is a plausible sequence precisely because someone cutting a keyguard is in Settings touching both, but it is narrower than first described.
      - **The stronger reason to disable it in fullscreen is not the stale value at all: the file's geometry is measured LIVE.** Every opening comes from `getBoundingClientRect()` at the moment Generate is pressed, so the file is only ever valid for the mode the app is in right then. Entering a title-bar height while fullscreen could not rescue a windowed keyguard — it would emit fullscreen positions plus a phantom bar, which is worse than either. Disabling the field says the true thing: in this mode there is no offset to supply.
      - **Read the ACTUAL state, never the setting.** The two come apart in both directions: the request can be refused (no user activation, or policy), and Esc leaves fullscreen without touching the setting. `isReallyFullscreen()` checks `document.fullscreenElement` (with the WebKit spelling behind it) and is the one predicate anything screen-measuring may use. Wired to `fullscreenchange` so leaving by Esc re-enables the field immediately.
      - **Verified live across all three states:** setting on but *not granted* → field stays usable (the case that must not regress); genuinely fullscreen → field disabled, status shown; left fullscreen with the setting still on → usable again. *The emitted file itself was not exercised — `generateScreenOpenings` returns early without a granted data folder, the same verification boundary every data-folder feature has.*

20. **`:hover` is the wrong tool for the conversation surface — it STICKS on a touch screen (Ken, August 3 2026).** Reported as *"selecting the 4-more button causes the background color to change to black and remain that way."* It is not black and it is not the regenerate button's fault: `--slot-persistent` is `#455A64` slate, the button correctly inverts to it on tap, and then **never reverts, because on a touch screen there is no pointer to move away** — the browser leaves the tapped element in `:hover` until something else is tapped.
    - **It was systemic, not one button.** The stylesheet had **44 `:hover` rules and zero `@media (hover: hover)` guards**, so every button on a touch-only surface had the same defect. The regenerate button is merely where it shows worst: a full white→slate inversion on a control the user taps repeatedly without moving on. The Command Bar and the Express Panel toggles had it identically.
    - **Fix:** all thirteen conversation-surface hover rules moved into one `@media (hover: hover)` block at the foot of the stylesheet. That query matches only a device with a pointer that can genuinely rest on an element, so a Surface with its trackpad keeps hover feedback and a bare touchscreen never sticks. **`:focus-visible` stays OUTSIDE the block** — it is how keyboard users see focus, and it must not be lost on any device.
    - **Deliberately NOT changed: Settings, About Me and the editors.** Same defect, far lower stakes — supporter-assisted overlays rather than the surface a non-speaking user drives a live conversation from, and a stuck tint there is cosmetic. Revisit if it proves distracting on the device.
    - **The general rule for this app: a hover style is decoration, never information.** Anything that must persist and mean something is a class the code sets (`.ep-on`), not a pseudo-class the browser sets.

21. **A tapped choice chip stays visibly selected while its steering is in effect (Ken, August 3 2026).** *"Selecting the 4-more button, the selected 'closed set' button in the express panel should be applied and preserved."*
    - **The steering was already applied — `handleRegenerate` has re-sent `activeSteer.focusChoice` since v0.5.98.** What was missing was that **nothing on screen said so**: choice chips had no selected state at all, unlike the Partner and Feeling toggles beside them. So four fresh cards about the same alternative were indistinguishable from the button having done nothing, which is exactly how it was reported.
    - Chips now take the same `.ep-on` treatment as the other influencer toggles (Rule 6), with `aria-pressed` and a "(chosen)" suffix on the accessible name. **Derived at render time from `activeSteer.focusChoice`**, not stored separately, so it cannot disagree with what is actually being sent — it lights on the chip tap, survives "New N", and disappears when `clearTurnSteering()` runs at a turn boundary.
    - `handleChoiceChip` now re-renders the panel immediately on tap, so the chip lights **before** the round-trip returns rather than after.

20. **An editor for a grid edits ONE cell at a time, with the grid itself as the list (Ken, August 11 2026).** The Express Panel tab used to render every item as a row — text field, respelling field, seven colour swatches and six tools apiece, thirty-odd times. Ken: *"It is too cumbersome and it doesn't behave well if the tab width is too small"*, with a photograph of the swatch row painted over the tools.
    - **The fix was to use what was already on screen rather than to shrink the rows.** The panel is live beside Settings on that tab and tapping a button there already selected it (August 8 2026), so **the tab never needed to re-list what the panel was showing.** It now shows an invitation — *"tap a button in the Express Panel to edit or move it"* — and, once a button is tapped, that one button's properties plus **add before / add after / move up / move down / delete / done**. **Generalize this before adding another list editor**: when the thing being edited is already rendered somewhere the user can tap, the editor's job is the selected item, not the collection.
    - **⚠ THE OVERLAP WAS A FLEX-DIRECTION PROBLEM, AND THE PART WORTH REMEMBERING IS WHY `flex-wrap` DIDN'T SAVE IT.** `.ee-swatches` carried `flex-wrap: wrap` and still overflowed, because as a **`flex: 0 0 auto`** item it sizes to max-content — so there is never a shortage for it to wrap out of. Wrapping only engages once the container has a width it did not choose. In the card it takes a full line (`flex: 1 1 100%`) and wraps properly: measured at a 420px editor, 5px of overflow before and none after. **Same family as the earlier finding that a flex row overflows because of what CANNOT shrink, not what visibly grew.**
    - **⚠ `.ee-row`, `.ee-list` and `.ee-tools` ARE SHARED WITH THE CONTROLS EDITOR**, which still builds a horizontal row of one field plus ↑ ↓ ✕. Restyling the base classes silently restacked that tab; the card layout is therefore scoped to `#expressEditor` and the icon sizing to `#controlEditor`. Verified after the change that Controls rows are still horizontal with 56×56 icon buttons.
    - **⚠ THE COST KEN NAMED WHEN HE ASKED FOR IT: there are no buttons "in waiting."** The list maps onto the layout's cells one-for-one, so an item past the last cell has no button to tap and **is unreachable** — not stored-for-later. That was already true and merely invisible; with the panel as the list it becomes total. So the editor **refuses to create one** (`roomToInsert`: insert only when a cell is free or a trailing undefined slot can absorb the shift) and offers to clear any an older layout left behind. Both paths verified, including that a full panel disables Add and says why.
    - **Delete is now confirmed, which the old ✕ was not:** it does not merely lose a phrase, it **pulls every button after it up a cell**, so the positions the user has learned all move — squarely the "significant work" bar. **Delete is DISABLED on an undefined slot** (Ken: *"an empty button should grey out the Delete button since it's already deleted"*) — nothing to lose there, so it would only offer to shuffle the panel for no gain. **The way to be rid of an empty slot is Move down until it reaches the end**, where the panel draws it identically to the cells past the end of the list; no separate remove path is needed.
    - **THE ADD RULE — Add stays LIVE on a full panel and WARNS at press time (Ken, August 11 2026, changing his own rule of an hour earlier): *"If a filled button exists in the last slot the add before/after buttons should be enabled but if the user presses one, then a warning message should be displayed which says that the 'x' button will be deleted if they proceed."*** The first rule greyed them out instead, which is worse for the same reason a disabled control usually is: **it states a fact the user cannot see the cause of.** Naming the button that will go says the same thing at the moment it matters and still lets them proceed. *(The insert has somewhere to go — and so needs no warning — when the list is shorter than the layout, or when its last item is an undefined slot; the panel draws both as an empty outline.)*
    - **⚠ ON A FULL PANEL THE LAST ITEM IS REMOVED BEFORE THE INSERT, NOT TRIMMED AFTER — and this is not tidiness, it is the only order that is correct.** "Add **after** the last button" puts the new slot at the very end, so trimming afterwards would throw away **the new slot** and leave the panel unchanged, silently doing nothing. Popping first, then clamping the index, makes the new button take the deleted one's place. Verified as its own case.
    - **Save is gone rather than sitting beside Done.** Edits have committed on every keystroke since the editor was written, so a Save button described something that had already happened; **Done** does what Save actually did (drop the on-screen keyboard) and returns to the invitation.
    - **Settings → Controls stays a LIST editor, and that is right for it** — its cards are one field each with no colour, no respelling and no per-item type, so the density that broke the Express tab is not there. It now collapses by section like every other tab (see Rule 18); the two changes are independent.

*(More rules to come. Where these conflict with the UI-Design.docx defaults — notably icon-only buttons vs. the doc's text-badge move cards, and the move-card text-hint anatomy — these field-driven rules win for the control surface; reconcile fully when we pick the first concrete layout.)*

## ⚠ LOCALLY, THE SERVICE WORKER SERVES A STALE APP AND NOTHING SAYS SO (August 23 2026)

**`CACHE_VERSION` in `sw.js` is `aac-v<version>-@@BUILD@@`, and `@@BUILD@@` is only
substituted at DEPLOY time.** On a local dev server it stays the literal string, so the
cache name never changes however much code changes - and the browser keeps running the
modules it cached the first time. Every deploy busts the cache correctly; **every local
session does the opposite.**

**The failure is silent and it lies convincingly.** A change is edited, saved, the page
reloaded, and the OLD behavior is observed - so the natural conclusion is that the fix
is wrong, and the next hour goes into re-fixing code that was already correct. It cost
that twice in one session: an accordion that "did not work" and a keyboard that "would
not appear" were both the previous build still running. **`fetch()` shows the new file
while `import()` returns the old module**, which is the tell, and it reads as
impossible until you know why.

**To be sure you are testing what you just wrote:** unregister the service worker and
delete its caches (`navigator.serviceWorker.getRegistrations()` then `caches.keys()`),
and reload with a changed query string. A plain reload is not enough - an already-loaded
ES module is cached per URL for the life of the page, so `import('./x.js')` hands back
the instance the page started with.

**This is very likely what a tester is seeing when a fixed bug "comes back".** Before
diagnosing a regression on Ken's machine, ask what build he is actually running.

## Logs are ASCII, and a permission prompt is not a hang (Ken, August 2 2026)

Two fixes from one error-log reading. Ken: *"is that an 'em dash'? If so, it doesn't belong in an error log. I would think that any log should be limited to ASCII."*

- **STANDING RULE — log text is plain ASCII.** `logError` now maps typographic punctuation to its ASCII equivalent (`asciiForLog` in `storage.js`, applied to both `context` and `message`) — em/en dashes, curly quotes, ellipsis, non-breaking space, bullets, arrow, `×`, `✓`. Applied at the choke point so no future caller has to remember, and so the same scrubbed text reaches all four sinks (`localStorage` ring buffer, `errors.log`, the conversation JSON, `console`). **The map is punctuation-ONLY and this is load-bearing:** an error message can legitimately carry partner speech or a person's name, so stripping non-ASCII wholesale would corrupt "José" to make a log tidier — a worse bug than the one being logged. Verified: accented letters pass through untouched. **Why it matters beyond tidiness:** log lines get opened in text editors, pasted into bug reports and mailed around, and every hop is a chance to mis-decode a fancy character; a hyphen survives all of them. This is the *second* time typography has cost real time here — see the 0.6.1 mojibake repair, whose cause was different (a PowerShell round-trip) but whose symptom was identical.
- **Also fixed: the 0.6.1 "what's new" note about the mojibake QUOTED the mojibake** (`â€"`, `sk-ant-â€¦4f2a`), so the panel announcing the fix rendered as garbage and read as though the bug were still present. Reworded in `CHANGELOG.md` to describe it instead. *Lesson: never print the corrupted form as evidence in user-facing text — the reader cannot tell a quotation from a recurrence.*
- **The recurring `storage warm-up did not finish within 6000ms` was a FALSE ALARM that fired on EVERY session start, and the app was working correctly the whole time.** Root cause: **`Promise.race` does not cancel the loser, and `withTimeout` never cleared its timer** — so the deadline callback ran six seconds after every Start and called `logError` regardless of whether the warm-up had finished in milliseconds. Fixed by clearing the timer in a `finally` (plus a `settled` guard for the re-arm path). Measured in the preview: warm-up completes in **4 ms** with no folder, and before the fix that still logged an error every time; after it, pressing Start and waiting **twice** the deadline logs nothing and leaves the transcript unwashed.
  - **I got this wrong on the first pass and it is worth recording why.** The message says "did not finish," so I went looking for something slow and found a plausible culprit — `restoreDataFolder()` calls `requestPermission()`, which blocks on a user dialog, and FSA permission does not survive a browser restart, so that dialog really does appear at nearly every session start. It fit the once-per-session pattern perfectly. **It was not the cause**, and the thing that exposed it was running the app with *no folder at all*: no dialog, 4 ms of work, error still logged. **The lesson: when a symptom appears every single time, prefer the explanation that is unconditional over the one that merely happens often** — and test the configuration where the suspected cause is absent, because that is the one measurement that can falsify it.
  - **The permission-prompt handling was kept anyway, as a genuine second bug on the same line.** A deadline on a storage call must distinguish "the layer stopped answering" from "the layer is waiting on the USER": `storage.isAwaitingPermission()` (set around the `requestPermission` await, cleared in a `finally`) makes `withTimeout` re-arm rather than give up while a dialog is open — otherwise a slow answer both logs a false error *and* abandons the folder the user is in the middle of granting. Verified against the shipped source: fast completion never logs; a genuine hang with no prompt still times out, logs exactly once and resolves null; a prompt open past the deadline resolves with the real folder and logs nothing.

## The conversation file records HOW it was said, not just what (Ken, August 8 2026)

Ken, on being shown the pronunciation work: *"I assume that the pronunciation spelling would be captured in the JSON file as a separate field so that the relive and critique feature could behave exactly like the actual conversation"*, and then *"I don't see the STT and TTS software (browser/Deepgram) represented for each exchange."* Both right, and both now recorded.

- **⚠ I HAD STATED THE RULE TOO ABSOLUTELY AND KEN CORRECTED IT.** I wrote that a respelling *"must never appear in the saved conversation file"*. It must never **replace** the display text — a separate field is not only safe but **required for fidelity**, because replaying `selectedText` through *today's* lexicon would say it however the user has since decided names should be said, not how it actually came out. **A replay must use the stored field, never re-derive it.** The general lesson: "keep X out of the record" and "keep X out of the field that means something else" are different rules, and collapsing them loses information that cannot be recovered later.
- **What a user turn now carries:** `spokenText` (the exact string handed to the synthesiser, **null when identical** to the display text — nearly every turn, and storing a duplicate of every utterance would grow the file for no information) and `tts: { provider, voice, fellBack? }`.
- **What a partner turn carries:** `stt` — which recogniser heard it. It is the single biggest influence on how accurate that line is, so a later review can tell a **mishearing** from a **misunderstanding**.
- **⚠ THE VOICE IS PER TURN, NOT PER CONVERSATION, AND THAT IS THE POINT.** It genuinely varies within one conversation: the paid voice falls back to the device voice on any single failure and recovers on the next utterance. A conversation-level note would record a voice the user did not hear — and `fellBack` is the only place that event is visible after the fact. It reports what was **USED**, not what is **SET** (`tts.lastVoiceUsed()`), which is the whole reason it exists.
- **The recogniser is stamped from ONE place** (`storage.setSttBackend`, called beside `stt.init`) rather than passed at each of the seven partner-turn call sites — it is fixed when `stt.init` runs and changing it needs a reload, so one place to set it is also one place that cannot be forgotten.
- **Known gap, deliberately not closed:** a Practice Mode partner turn is *spoken by the app* and records no voice, so a practice replay would not reproduce the partner's side. Real conversations have a human on that side and nothing to reproduce, so this only affects rehearsal.

## The paid voice silently fell back for one sentence (Ken, August 8 2026) — retry + keepalive

Ken: *"I thought I was experiencing this but I doubted myself."* He was right, and the reason he doubted himself is the finding.

- **The fallback is deliberate and per UTTERANCE.** Any failure of the paid voice re-speaks that one sentence with the device voice — *"the one outcome that is never acceptable is that the user pressed a button and nothing was said"* — then the next utterance tries Aura again. **Nothing in Settings changes**, because the setting was never the problem, so there is no state to inspect afterwards that looks wrong.
- **⚠ THE CAUSE OF A MID-CONVERSATION DROP: the connection is REUSED between utterances and nothing was sent over it in between.** A quiet stretch is long enough for Deepgram, a proxy or the network to close it, and the app only discovers this on the next tap. That produces exactly the reported symptom — one odd sentence after a pause, everything fine afterwards.
- **Built:** a **KeepAlive** every 8s while a connection is open and idle (skipped mid-utterance; **tied to the socket's life, not the module's** — `createVoice()` runs again on any provider or key change, so a module-level timer would leak one per call and ping a connection nobody owns), and **one retry** on a connection-shaped failure only, dropping the dead socket first so the retry cannot reuse it. **A rejected key or an empty response is not retried** — it will fail identically and would only add delay before the same fallback.
- **⚠ THE KEEPALIVE IS UNVERIFIED against a genuinely long idle period** — that the message is accepted was checked; that it prevents the close needs a real conversation with a long gap. **That is why the retry exists rather than the keepalive being the only fix.**
- **Standing observation, not built:** a voice fallback is logged through the same faint red transcript wash as every other error. But every other error is a malfunction, whereas this one changes **the voice the user speaks in**, which is identity. It deserves its own signal. The per-turn `tts.fellBack` field now at least makes it visible in the record.

## Transcript mirrors the conversation pane — incremental partner writes (Ken, July 13 2026)

**Decision (Ken):** the recorded transcript (`conversations/<id>.json`) must **mirror the on-screen conversation pane at all times**, so it is a reliable record of the app's behavior while it misbehaves during testing. Explicitly accepted trade-off: keep this **until/unless it causes performance problems** (each pause rewrites the file). **Terminology fixed this session: "the transcript" = the JSON file; "the conversation pane" = the UI element.** Reverses the prior "commit at response-selection" model (where the partner's turn was buffered in memory and only written when the user responded).

**Why fidelity matters beyond debugging — the relive-and-critique feature (Ken, July 13 2026).** Accurately representing the *actual spoken conversation* in both the transcript and the conversation pane — correct turns, correct order, correct wording, correct attribution (who said what), proper spacing — is not only about diagnosing bugs during testing. It will be **critical** when we add the ability for the user to **relive and critique previous conversations** (the Phase-3 offline review / conversation-review feature — see the roadmap items). That feature is only as good as the record it replays: if turns are merged, mis-ordered, mis-attributed, or run together, the user's review of their own communication is misleading. So the string of July 2026 transcript/pane fidelity fixes (incremental mirroring, pane ordering, echo excision, pardon re-speak as its own turn, inter-segment spacing) is **foundational work for conversation review**, not just test hygiene — hold the bar high on transcript accuracy going forward. **[→ relates to the Phase-3 conversation-review / offline feedback roadmap item and the "Further Out — Future Directions" conversation-review entry in the Product Overview.]**

**Behavior now (the spec Ken gave):**
- **File created on Listen / Start**, not lazily at the first turn. `startFreshListening()` calls `storage.startConversationLog()`, which is now **idempotent** (`if (currentLogData) return`), so an empty `<id>.json` exists as soon as capture begins.
- **Partner pause → raw line written immediately.** `handleSilencePeriod` calls `storage.logPartnerInterim({rawTranscript, partner})`; the first pause appends a pending partner entry (raw set, `cleanedTranscript:''`).
- **Partner continues → overwrite raw, clear cleaned.** Each later pause overwrites the SAME pending entry's `rawTranscript` and re-clears `cleanedTranscript` (no duplicate turn).
- **User responds → user turn written immediately; partner finalized in place.** `commitExchange` `detachPendingPartnerTurn()`s the pending entry, writes the **user** turn at once (no longer waiting on the AI cleanup round-trip — fixes the old lag), then a background pass cleans the partner text and `finalizePartnerTurn(handle,…)` fills its `cleanedTranscript` **in place**, so order stays partner-then-user.
- **Interruption / repair / End-Start** all finalize the pending entry (raw, no cleanup) rather than appending a duplicate; End/Start prefers `heardPartnerText()` (may hold speech captured since the last pause).

**Implementation:** new pure module [`transcript-log.js`](app/js/transcript-log.js) (`upsertPartnerInterim` / `finalizePartner` — the array-shaping rules, unit-tested in `tests/transcript-log.test.mjs`); `storage.js` holds the `pendingPartnerTurn` pointer and exposes `logPartnerInterim` / `detachPendingPartnerTurn` / `finalizePartnerTurn` (the old `logPartnerSpeech` is removed). Verified end-to-end through the real `storage.js` against an in-memory FSA folder mock: file-on-start, raw-then-overwrite-clearing-cleaned, user-immediate, finalize-in-place with order preserved. On-device confirmation (a granted folder) is the same boundary as all conversation logging.

**Follow-up 4 — "Ask them to repeat" re-speak = a separate turn + spaces between segments (Ken, July 13 2026).** Two issues from a pardon-flow transcript. (1) After **Ask them to repeat**, the partner's re-speak was **appended to their earlier turn** (and rendered before the pardon), e.g. `"Oh, OK, yeah, I had a great weekend.Yeah, I had a great weekend too."` This **refines v0.5.87** ("their repeat appends to it") — Ken now wants the re-speak as a **new turn after** the pardon, while still not discarding the original. Fix (`app.js handlePardon`): after `logSpokenUserTurn(pardon)` commits the partner's kept turn + the pardon, call `finalizePendingPartnerTurn()` (detach + finalize the storage pending turn, cleaned=raw) and `stt.resetTranscript()` + clear `currentPartnerText`/`pendingPartnerHistoryIdx`, so the partner's next speech starts a fresh turn. The engine's repair resolution is unaffected (it operates on the stack + the re-speak text; the AI still gets ordered turns). (2) The recognizer returns segments with **no separating space**, so `"Good morning." + "How was your weekend?"` concatenated into `"…weekend.How…"`. Fix (`stt.js`): a `joinParts()` helper (trim each, join with single space) now builds `accumulatedText`/`getCurrentTranscript`/the checkpoint text/`dropLastStatement` — segments are spaced. Unit-tested (spaced concat + checkpoint); 116/116. Boots clean; the live pardon flow is the on-device retest.

**Follow-up 3 — a placeholder interrupted a speaking button mid-speech (Ken, July 13 2026).** Ken: clicking **Repeat what I said** was cut off partway by a placeholder. Cause: **Say again / Hold on did not invalidate an in-flight `generateOptions`** (unlike a response selection, which does `generationToken++`). When the partner's LLM round-trip — already running when the button was pressed — completed, it called `placeholders.start()` again; with the initial-delay clock measured from the partner's pause (already elapsed), the placeholder fired *immediately* and its `tts.speak` cancelled the button's speech. Ken's rule: **pressing any button that can speak must abort placeholders instantly** (command bar OR Express panel). Fix, two layers: (1) a `placeholderEpoch` (app.js) bumped by `abortPlaceholders()` — Say again / Hold on now call it, and `generateOptions` captures the epoch and skips `placeholders.start()` if it changed while generating, so the placeholder is suppressed WITHOUT discarding the response options (which the user still needs — that's why it's separate from `generationToken`); Wind down uses `generationToken++` (it abandons the options for closings). (2) A hard backstop in `placeholders.js`: a `setUserSpeakingGate(() => speakingUserStatement)` so `speakNext` defers (never barges) while the app is speaking the user's own statement. Buttons that already `generationToken++` (response / Ask-them-to-repeat / Express / composer) were already covered. Unit-tested (`placeholders.test.mjs` — the gate defers then speaks once the user is done); 115/115. Boots clean; the acoustic timing is the on-device retest.

**Follow-up 2 — hardened TTS-echo excision: partial / mis-heard / lagged echo (Ken, July 13 2026).** Ken's next on-device catch: after **Repeat what I said**, the mic caught only the TAIL of the re-spoken response ("had some time to relax") and appended it to the partner's turn; a placeholder "Still mulling that over" came back mis-heard as "Steel mulling that over." **Ken's directive: do NOT sacrifice the keep-listening (talk-over) feature — the mic stays hot; instead excise the app's own phrases from the STT stream more robustly.** So the fix is entirely in `stt.js`'s `isEcho`, not by muting. Three gaps closed: (1) the matcher only did exact/prefix/embedded — it never matched an arbitrary **contiguous slice** of what we said, so a suffix/middle fragment slipped through → added `fuzzySlice()` (a token-window scan, either direction); (2) no tolerance for mis-transcription → `tokensSimilar()` allows a small per-token edit distance (2 for len≥4, 1 for len 3; short words exact) so "still"→"steel" still matches; (3) the cloud recognizer returns our echo a couple seconds late, after the 1.5 s tail, so the phrase had already expired → **decoupled the two windows**: `ECHO_TAIL_MS`=1500 still gates the checkpoint (so a genuine reply isn't delayed), but a new `ECHO_MATCH_MS`=4000 keeps a spoken phrase *matchable* for excision much longer. Accepted tradeoff (unchanged in spirit from the v0.3.4 content-filter design): within the match window a partner utterance that genuinely repeats our phrase is treated as echo. Unit-tested in `tests/stt.test.mjs` (suffix slice, still→steel, late echo past the tail, and a no-over-drop case); 114/114. The robust long-term answer is still Phase-2 partner voice recognition.

**Follow-up — the conversation PANE was out of order relative to the (correct) transcript (Ken, July 13 2026).** Ken's on-device catch: after the partner replied, tapping **Repeat what I said** showed the user's re-spoken line ABOVE the partner's reply in the pane, though the transcript had it correctly after. Root cause: the pane rendered the partner's in-progress turn as a bottom-pinned **live line** (`ui.setLiveTranscript`), while a spoken-command user turn (`logSpokenUserTurn` — Say again / Hold on / Ask-them-to-repeat) was appended into the committed history *above* that live line; the transcript was already correct because it writes the partner turn at its pause. Fix (app.js, pane-only — storage untouched): when a command user turn is logged mid-partner-turn, **promote** the live partner turn into `conversationHistory` first (`flushLivePartnerToHistory`, tracked by `pendingPartnerHistoryIdx`) so the user turn renders after it; `commitExchange` / repair now use `placePartnerTurn` (update the promoted entry in place, else append) and `updatePartnerLive` keeps a promoted turn updating in place instead of re-showing the bottom live line. `logSpokenUserTurn` also calls `storage.logPartnerInterim` first so pane and transcript stay identical even if the command precedes the partner's first pause. Traced through the reported sequence (partner1→user1→partner2→say-again→response) → pane and transcript both order partner2 before the say-again. Verified boot-clean; the live mic ordering is the usual on-device retest boundary.

## Interrupting the partner — capture their partial speech; keep capturing after (Ken, July 8 2026)

Ken: interrupting the partner with an instant statement (e.g. an Express "Bye") was making it look like the partner never spoke — their in-progress speech wasn't recorded. Fixes + confirmations:

- **The partner's partial speech is now captured at the interruption.** `currentPartnerText` is only refreshed at a silence checkpoint, so interrupting *before* a pause left it empty and the partner's words were dropped. New `stt.getCurrentTranscript()` returns the live accumulated transcript (finalized segments + interim); app.js `heardPartnerText()` prefers it (falls back to `currentPartnerText`). `speakAsUserTurn` (Express phrase / composer Speak) and `handleResponseSelected` capture it BEFORE `stt.stopListening()`, so the partner's speech up to the interruption is committed to the transcript + JSON, interleaved before the interrupting statement.
- **No AI cleanup on the interruption fragment (Ken).** `commitExchange` gained `opts.cleanup` (default true); `speakAsUserTurn` passes `cleanup:false` — a mid-interruption fragment isn't a completed utterance, so we record the raw heard text verbatim with no cleanup round-trip. (`handleResponseSelected` keeps cleanup — it fires only after the partner paused, i.e. a complete utterance.)
- **Auto-listen keeps capturing after the interruption (confirmed, already worked).** `speakAsUserTurn` ends in `resumeOrIdle()`, which resumes listening when `manualListenArmed && loadAutoRelisten()`; `startFreshListening()` resets the accumulator so the partner's continued speech becomes a fresh partner turn. Verified: after an interrupt the mic re-starts and "…continued speech" is captured.
- **The ENTIRE session conversation is sent to the AI each turn (answers Ken's question).** `generateOptions` passes the full `conversationHistory` + the current partner text; `llm.generateResponses` maps every entry to the `messages` array (partner→user, user→assistant). Verified: `[partner partial, user "Bye", partner continued]` all reach the request. So the interruption and everything around it are in context — "not an issue," as Ken expected. (There is no history windowing yet; a rolling window is a future token-cost concern, not a correctness one.)
- **One inherent gap (noted, not a defect):** while the app is speaking the interrupting statement the mic is off (so it doesn't capture its own TTS), so partner words spoken *during* that ~1 s overlap aren't captured; capture resumes immediately after. Verified in preview with a fake recognizer (the live mic still needs the tablet).

## Word prediction — local tiers, no AI per keystroke (Ken, June 21 2026)

Resolves the prediction half of the long-recorded composition roadmap (the keyboard `pred` slots + the "AI-assisted composition" build-order item). **Predicted-word boxes on the on-screen keyboard are fully LOCAL — no API round-trip per keypress** (per-keystroke latency + token cost make AI infeasible there, and it's a solved offline problem). Three layerable, instant, offline techniques:
1. **Prefix completion** from a bundled word-frequency list (trie / sorted array, ~10–30k words ≈ 100–300 KB, precached): "th" → the / that / this / they.
2. **Next-word n-gram** (bundled bigram/trigram table, pruned to top-k per context): fills the boxes before the next word is even started.
3. **Personalized local learning**: track the user's own word/phrase frequency + recency in the data folder (the user-owned-data model), boosting their actual vocabulary over time — no network, no privacy cost.
