**Two complementary tiers, kept distinct:** *local prediction* handles **every keystroke** (free, instant); the *LLM* is reserved for occasional, **explicit phrase expansion** — a few words → a full sentence in the user's voice (the existing "In My Own Words" build-order item #3, "AI-assisted composition") — **never per keypress**. So the keyboard's prediction boxes are NOT an AI feature; the AI tier is a separate, deliberate action.

**Where the boxes live (Ken, June 21 2026):** the keyboard already scaffolds them — `pred` cells in `keyboard-layouts.js` + `.kbd-pred` CSS. **Plan: convert the existing keyboard toolbar buttons (Cut / Copy / Paste / Hide) to ICON buttons (rule 4)** — they're control buttons, icon-only anyway — which **reclaims the horizontal space their text labels took, and that toolbar row hosts the word-prediction buttons.** (Prediction buttons show predicted words as text → content, exempt from rule 4, like the phrases panel and move cards.) **BUILT v0.4.4 (icons) + v0.4.5 (engine):** `icons.js` converted Cut/Copy/Paste/Hide to icons; `prediction.js` + `data/words.json` provide prefix completion + personalized local learning; the toolbar's reclaimed space hosts the prediction buttons. Personalized counts persist in `localStorage` (`aac_word_freq`) — a data-folder move + a next-word n-gram layer remain future extensions; the bundled list is a ~220-word starter to grow.

## Single-instance enforcement — design question (Ken, June 16 2026; my recommendation, NOT built)

Ken asked whether we can prevent multiple instances/launches of the app. **Yes, and it matters for this app specifically:** two live instances (two tabs/windows) each run their own mic + TTS, so instance A's spoken placeholder is captured by instance B's mic — **cross-instance feedback the v0.3.4 content-echo filter cannot catch** (B doesn't know what A said). Two instances also race on the FSA data-folder writes (`worldview.json` / `relationships.json` / conversation logs) and can double-speak. So single-instance is a robustness requirement, not just polish.

**Recommended approach (two layers, covers the realistic cases on a dedicated Surface in one browser):**
1. **Web Locks API (`navigator.locks.request`) for detection + a blocking "already open" screen.** On startup request an exclusive named lock (e.g. `aac-single-instance`) held for the page's lifetime via a never-resolving promise. The first instance gets it; later instances' requests stay pending → they detect another instance holds it and show a full-screen "AAC is already running in another window — switch to it" overlay instead of starting the mic/engine. Well-supported in Edge/Chrome (Chromium). Covers multiple tabs *and* windows in the same browser profile.
2. **PWA `launch_handler: { client_mode: "focus-existing" }` in the manifest** so re-*launching* the installed app focuses the running window rather than spawning a new one (the app is intended to be installed app-style on the Surface). Complements layer 1, which still backstops a manually-opened second tab.

**Boundary / limitation:** web APIs can't coordinate across *different* browser profiles or *different* browsers (Edge AND Chrome both open) — they don't share locks/localStorage/BroadcastChannel. On a dedicated device using one browser this is an acceptable boundary; if it ever matters, an FSA folder lock-file (write a PID/heartbeat into the data folder, stale-timeout to recover from crashes) is the only cross-browser option and is heavier/more fragile. *Not built — awaiting Ken's go-ahead. When built: bump version, add to the privacy/consent behavior already in flight, and note the cross-browser boundary in the docs.*

**Future: partner voice recognition as an extraneous-speech filter (Ken, June 16 2026).** When Phase-2 partner recognition lands, real-time speaker identification could filter out speech that isn't the recognized communication partner (the app's own TTS, bystanders, background voices) — a more general solution than content-matching the app's own fillers. Records alongside the v0.3.4 echo filter as the eventual upgrade path; the content filter is the no-extra-infrastructure stopgap until then. **[→ Overview To Do — Phase 2 partner recognition / privacy section]**

---

## Worldview Questionnaire (June 2026) — Build Steps 1–4 DONE; core build complete

The worldview model (Architecture Overview §6) has a concrete questionnaire design, a build-ready implementation plan, and **Build Steps 1–4 are implemented and verified** (June 14 2026). Remaining steps (5 expand content, 6 RAG-lite) are enhancements, not blockers — the core worldview feature is functional end-to-end.

**Follow-up questions — built (v0.3.13) then reverted (v0.3.14); passion-tailored depth deferred to the LLM track (Ken, June 19 2026).** v0.3.13 added inline follow-up cards: when a field with a `followups[]` array is answered, the follow-ups render as indented child cards below the parent (each with its own input/Save/Speak), and their answers inject into `buildBlock()` indented under the parent. **Ken's catch killed the design:** the only field carrying `followups` was `passions`, a **`multi`** field — so "How long have you been into it?" was asked **once for the whole list** ("music, hiking"), which is meaningless; that depth is inherently **per-item**. We then established the deeper problem: **the follow-up question itself is often a function of the answer** (Music → "play or listen? which instruments?"; Sports → "play or watch? which teams?"). A static registry can only express that via **`when:`-gated branches** authored per known value (the `when` field on each follow-up is the seam for it) — but that is an authoring treadmill that **still gives zero coverage for "in my own words" / typed passions.** **Decision: do not build static passion-specific follow-up branches.** Passion-tailored follow-ups are a **conversation feature, not a questionnaire feature** — they belong to the **LLM-driven "Conversational Worldview Collection"** already specified in Architecture Overview §6 (the AI sees the answer and asks adaptive, tailored follow-ups; answers stored as free-form per-item attrs). So: `followups` removed from `passions`; `passions` stays a clean `multi` list (already useful worldview context on its own); the entire v0.3.13 follow-up infrastructure (registry indexing of `_fu_` keys, `buildBlock` injection, `buildFollowupCard`/`wv-card-group` rendering, the `.wv-followup-card` CSS) was **reverted** rather than left as dead, half-applied code with no consumer — the correct future implementation (LLM-generated) shares none of it. *(If a single-value field ever wants a fixed generic follow-up, the pattern is re-addable; it was the `multi`+shared-answer combination that was wrong.)*

**Keyboard / field tabbing — shipped (June 14 2026).** The "About Me" questionnaire supports physical-keyboard users. **Decisions (Ken):** (1) *every* control is a Tab stop (native order — inputs and option chips alike), not a reduced inputs-only order; (2) opening a module **autofocuses its first answerable field** (accepted that on a touch device this also auto-pops the OS keyboard). Two fixes in `app/js/worldview-ui.js`: a `focusFirstField()` call at the end of `renderModule` (and on the targeted card when arriving from a "suggested next" chip), and `setBackgroundInert()` which marks the app `<header>` + `<main>` `inert` while the overlay is open so Tab stays inside the form instead of leaking out to Settings / the conversation controls behind it (cleared on close). CACHE_VERSION → `aac-v0.2.5`. Verified in preview: first field focused on module open, header+main inert while open and cleared on Done, no focusable leak outside the form (the only out-of-form stop is the pre-Start `#startBtn`, which is hidden in normal flow), no console errors. *Note — Windows touch keyboard:* when the cursor enters a worldview `<input>` the Windows on-screen keyboard (TabTip) pops; this is the **OS**, not our code (FSA-era black-box behavior already documented in "In My Own Words") — Windows auto-shows it whenever an editable element gets focus in a touch context. Levers if unwanted later: `inputmode="none"` per field, or the Chromium `navigator.virtualKeyboard` API (neither can *summon* a keyboard on demand). This is a config decision (hardware-keyboard vs. touch-only users), not a defect.

**Storage fallback (where worldview data lives without/with a stale folder) — confirmed June 14 2026.** Source of truth is `worldview.json` in the granted FSA data folder; `localStorage` key `aac_worldview` is a write-through cache (`worldview.js` `load`/`save`, `storage.js` `readFile`/`writeFile`). **No folder granted:** answers persist *only* in `localStorage` (per-browser/per-machine — not in the portable file, lost if browser data is cleared; `writeFile` is a silent no-op without a `dirHandle`). **Folder moved/renamed since last session:** `restoreDataFolder()` re-acquires the handle from IndexedDB; if the OS can't resolve it the handle is **deleted** and the app silently falls back to the `localStorage` cache, writing new answers to cache only until the user re-picks a folder.

**Cache → disk promotion (built June 14 2026).** `worldview.syncToFolder()` closes the cache-only gap: the moment a data folder becomes available it promotes cache-only answers to the portable `worldview.json`. Called at the three points a folder becomes available — `app.js` `handleStart()` (start-up restore), the Settings "Choose Folder" handler (`pickDataFolder`), and `worldview-ui.open()`. Logic: no folder → `'noop'`; folder + no on-disk file → write the cache to disk (`'wrote'`); folder + existing file → the **newer of {disk, cache} by `updated` wins** (write-through to both stores + in-memory) so neither side's recent edits are clobbered (`'wrote'`/`'adopted'`). Timestamp-wins, not field-level merge — adequate for the real scenarios (the main case is "no disk file yet"); a future field-level merge / "reconnect your folder" prompt remains a possible refinement. *Verified in preview only for the no-folder `'noop'` guard + wiring (no throw, cache write-through intact); the actual on-disk promotion needs a user-granted folder (FSA gesture) to exercise — confirm on the test tablet.*

**Cross-PC copy of `worldview.json` — bug fixed (June 15 2026), v0.2.25. The file in the folder is the source of truth — full stop.** *Symptom (Ken):* copied `worldview.json` from PC1 into PC2's data folder; on PC2 the app showed no progress. *Root cause:* this was **not** a timestamp problem, it was an "ignore the current file" problem (Ken's framing). `syncToFolder()` ran a cache-vs-disk **timestamp contest**, and `defaultProfile()` stamped `updated: now` — so on a fresh PC the empty browser cache (stamped *now*) beat the copied file (saved earlier on PC1), the cache "won," and `save()` wrote the empty profile **back over the copied file (silent data loss).** *Decision (Ken, June 15 2026): the data folder is the portable source of truth; the localStorage cache is only a same-machine mirror + a no-folder stopgap. **If a `worldview.json` is present in the connected folder, that file wins — always; the contest is gone.*** `syncToFolder` now: file on disk → **adopt it** (mirror to cache, never overwrite); no file on disk → **promote the cache** (answers entered before a folder was granted). `load()` already preferred disk; this makes the promotion path agree. Removed the timestamp comparison + the `hasContent` scaffolding; `profile.updated` is now informational only (nothing compares it). CACHE_VERSION/APP_VERSION → `0.2.25`. *Verified in preview:* app boots clean, `load()` + `syncToFolder` (noop without a folder) run with no console errors. *The on-disk adopt path needs a user-granted FSA folder — confirm the cross-PC copy now shows up on the tablet.* **Accepted trade-off:** answers entered while *no* folder was connected, then connecting a folder that *already* has a file, are dropped in favor of the file. A future "this folder and this browser differ — which do you want?" prompt is how that case becomes loss-free (the only remaining refinement here). *(This also makes [[relationship data]] portability simpler if it moves to its own file — same "file in folder wins" rule.)*

**About Me prompts for a data folder when none is assigned (June 15 2026), v0.2.26.** Without a granted folder, About Me answers persist only in the localStorage cache (machine-local, lost if browser data is cleared, not in the portable file). The About Me **home now shows a prompt banner** (`renderFolderPrompt()` in `worldview-ui.js`, `.wv-folder-prompt` styles) whenever `storage.hasDataFolder()` is false, with a **Choose data folder** button. *Why a button, not an auto-popped picker in `open()`:* `showDirectoryPicker()` requires transient user activation, and `open()` runs several `await`s (restore/loadRegistry/load/syncToFolder) before render that would consume the gesture from the click that opened About Me — so the picker would throw. The button is a fresh gesture. Clicking it → `storage.pickDataFolder()` → `wv.syncToFolder()` (file-in-folder wins per v0.2.25: adopt an existing `worldview.json`, else promote cache-only answers) → `renderHome()` (banner clears, progress reflects adopted data). Non-blocking — the user can still answer without a folder; the banner just persists until one is chosen. CACHE_VERSION/APP_VERSION → `0.2.26`. *Verified in preview:* opening About Me with no folder shows the styled banner + button, no console errors; the actual pick→adopt path needs a real FSA gesture — confirm on the tablet.

**Build Step 4 — shipped (interim Name/About You removed):** No migration was needed — there were no meaningful legacy `userName`/`userAbout` values to preserve (Ken's call). Removed the stopgap entirely: the two Settings inputs (`index.html`), their wiring (`app.js` `openSettings` + init), `storage.loadUserProfile`/`saveUserProfile`, `llm.setUserProfile` + the name/about branch of `buildProfileBlock`, and the orphaned `.setting-optional` CSS. The General tab now points users to **About Me**. The worldview profile (`buildBlock()`) is the sole personalization channel; the no-bracket safety rule remains. CACHE_VERSION → `aac-v0.2.4`. Verified in preview: Settings opens/saves with the fields gone, profile injection still carries the worldview block + no-bracket rule with no stale remnants, no console errors, no remaining references to the removed symbols.

**Build Step 3 — shipped:** the generation call now injects the worldview profile and harvests gaps.
- `app/js/llm.js`: added `setWorldviewBlock(text)`; `buildProfileBlock()` now layers worldview block → interim name/about ("Also: …") → the standing no-bracket rule. `generateResponses()` now requests a single structured JSON object `{classification:{fpp,about}, options[], missing_facts[]}` and returns `{options, classification, missingFacts}`; `parseGeneration()` parses robustly and still tolerates a legacy bare array. `max_tokens` 300 → 500. **Model unchanged (`claude-sonnet-4-6`)** — kept deliberately for the user-pays-per-token cost model; this was not a model migration.
- `app/js/app.js`: imports the worldview model; `initApp()` loads the registry + profile (cache at startup); `handleStart()` reloads the profile from the data folder once granted; `generateOptions()` calls `llm.setWorldviewBlock(worldview.buildBlock())` each turn (so edits take effect immediately), destructures `{options, missingFacts}`, and routes `missingFacts` → `worldview.recordGaps(...)`.
- `app/sw.js`: CACHE_VERSION → `aac-v0.2.3`.
- **Verified in preview** (fetch stubbed — no API key needed): structured output parses to classification + options + `missing_facts`; legacy bare-array tolerated; system prompt contains the worldview block + name + no-bracket rule; app boots with registry loaded and no console errors. *Not yet exercised against the live Claude API* — that needs Ken's key in the running app.
- **Guardrail honored:** interim Name/About You Settings fields untouched (removed in Step 4).

**"Suggested next" removed from the About Me home (June 15 2026, v0.2.31).** Ken: those questions are already asked on the first topic sub-page, so the gaps-driven "Suggested next" chips on the home screen were redundant. Removed the section from `renderHome` plus the now-dead `openModuleForField` helper and `.wv-suggested`/`-chip` CSS. Home now shows just intro → (folder prompt if needed) → **Topics** → **People & relationships** → Restart. The model's gaps subsystem stays intact (`recordGaps`/`listGaps`/`suggestedNext` still record and are available for a future surface); only the home UI section is gone. Verified in preview: home shows only Topics + People sections, no console errors.

**Build Step 2 — shipped:** `app/js/worldview-ui.js` + an "About Me" header button + a full-screen `#worldviewScreen` (index.html / styles.css), precached in sw (CACHE_VERSION → `aac-v0.2.2`). Home shows an intro, gaps-driven **Suggested next**, a module list with answered/total progress bars, and **Restart** (confirm → `resetAll`). Tapping a module opens its fields as cards (the "chunk"). Card supports every field type — choice (single-select chips + in-my-own-words), multi-with-options (toggle chips + add-your-own), free multi (comma-separated), text/number, and repeat (sub-field rows + add/remove) — plus three visible states (✓ Answered / Prefer not to say / unanswered), edit-in-place, and **Undo** for declined. Each card carries the in-flow **🔊 Speak my answer** control (Build Step 2 design intent; opt-in, speaks the saved value in the selected voice). Verified in preview: all field types persist incrementally to `worldview.json`/cache; decline/undo; progress + suggested-next update; restart clears; Done closes. *Step-3 note:* `buildBlock()` returns '' until `loadRegistry()` has run on the worldview instance — Step 3 must call `worldview.loadRegistry()` + `load()` at app init so generation can inject the profile even before the user opens "About Me".

**Build Step 1 (recap) — shipped earlier (June 14 2026), committed:**
- `app/data/worldview-questions.json` — static registry. Scope per resolved decisions: **full Tier-A** (A1 About You, A2 Where You Are, A3 People, A4 Daily Life, A5 Contact) + **Tier-C starter** (C1 Favorites, C2 Passions). Each field: `key`, `q`, `type` (text/number/choice/multi/repeat), `options`, `fills` (placeholder synonyms), `sensitive`, `defaultPrivacy`. A5 fields are `defaultPrivacy: private`.
- `app/js/worldview.js` — model layer: `loadRegistry()`, `load()`/save (data-folder `worldview.json` write-through + localStorage cache), `getField`/`setField`/`declineField`/`resetField`/`resetAll`, three states (unanswered=key absent / answered / declined=sticky), `effectivePrivacy`/`setPrivacy`, gaps `recordGaps`/`listGaps`/`clearGaps`, `getModules` (progress), `suggestedNext` (gaps-first), and `buildBlock()` (compact profile text; labels from `fills[0]`; declined + private fields surface only as a phrase-around instruction, never with their values).
- `app/sw.js` — precache list updated (worldview.js + registry), CACHE_VERSION → `aac-v0.2.1`.
- **Verified in preview:** set/decline/reset/un-decline state transitions; cache round-trip; `buildBlock()` includes shareable facts and withholds private (phone) values while listing age+phone as phrase-around; gaps add/bump/drop-answered/clear; suggested-next ordering. *Caveat:* verification exercised the in-memory + localStorage-cache path; the on-disk FSA `worldview.json` write goes through the same `storage.writeFile` that conversation logging already uses, but confirming an actual on-disk file requires a user-granted data folder (FSA needs a user gesture).
- **Not touched:** the interim Name/About You Settings fields (they stay until Build Step 4), per guardrail.

**Remaining build order:** ~~1) data model~~ ✓ → ~~2) questionnaire UI~~ ✓ → ~~3) LLM integration~~ ✓ → ~~4) remove interim Name/About You~~ ✓ (no migration needed) → ~~5) expand Tier-B + rest of Tier-C content~~ **✓ COMPLETE — C3/C4 and Tier B's personality and values shipped August 7 2026; humor, beliefs, group register and conflict style on August 8 2026. See "Tier B is complete".** → **6) RAG-lite (enhancement, not needed until cross-session memory lands)**.

**Register and turns of phrase are NOT capturable today, and when they are they belong on the PERSON, not the profile (Ken, August 5 2026).** Found through the demo persona, which describes Marc as *"into gaming, Marvel, and the Packers; he keeps his replies short and snappy; he says things like 'Let's go!', 'That's clutch,' and 'Nah, I'm good.'"* **The registry currently holds A1, A2, A4, A5, C1, C2 and no Tier B at all**, so that sentence splits cleanly: the **interests** half is real (`fav_games`, `fav_team`, `fav_screen`/`fav_reading`, `passions`) and the **voice** half — brevity, register, catchphrases — has no field anywhere. Ken's rule: **a persona attribute the app cannot capture cannot appear in the demo**, because the demo would be claiming a capability that does not exist.
- **Ken's parenthetical is the design finding, and it kills the obvious fix:** *"even these phrases are communication partner dependent. Marc would never say these things to his mother except as a momentary joke."* So building a **global** `register` / `catchphrases` Tier-B field would be wrong — not merely incomplete. Register varies per relationship, so it is graph data.
- **This is the SECOND instance of one rule, and the rule is worth stating on its own: when a questionnaire field's correct answer varies per person, it is not a questionnaire field.** The first instance was module **A3 "People in Your Life"**, which was deleted and became the relationship graph in v0.2.27. Tier B's *"how you treat different people"* topic anticipated the content but not this consequence — it cannot live in a flat key→answer registry. Apply the test when authoring any remaining Tier-B question.
- **FOUR separate features now want per-partner attributes on the same `me→person` edge**, which changes the build economics — this is one People-editor section ("how I talk with this person") plus four small prompt contributions, not four features: (1) **standing relationship goal** (June 15 2026), (2) **per-partner openers/closers** (July 3 2026), (3) **vulgarity permission** (August 3 2026, explicitly *"a property of a PERSON… an identified partner with this property set"*, blocked on guardian approval), and (4) **register / turns of phrase** (this). Build them together. **→ BUILT August 7 2026 — 1, 2 and 4 shipped together as predicted; 3 remains blocked on guardian approval. See "Per-partner profile" below.**
- **Nothing to change in the app right now** — the finding is about what the demo may claim and how Tier B must be authored when it is built.

**CATCHPHRASES ARE EXPRESS PANEL BUTTONS, AND THE AI NEVER PRODUCES THEM (Ken, August 5 2026).** *"I think this can be addressed by making catchphrases Express Panel buttons (possibly dimensioned by person and place) and not something that the AI ever responds with or builds on."* Raised as the answer to a flagged risk — that a model given "he says 'Let's go!'" will sprinkle it everywhere — and it **removes** the risk rather than managing it, since there is no reliable prompt instruction that stops over-application and idiolect used slightly wrong reads as impersonation, which is worse than idiolect absent.
- **The stronger reason is honesty, and it is the anti-fabrication rule applied to STYLE instead of FACT.** A catchphrase the model guessed at reaches the partner as though it were spontaneous, when it was inferred. A catchphrase on a button is the user deliberately choosing their own words. Same principle, same justification.
- **THE CONSEQUENCE WITH TEETH: Phase 2 of the voice plan harvests the user's own composed prose, and that prose CONTAINS their catchphrases** — so the prohibition needs enforcing at the **harvest boundary**, not merely as a prompt instruction. **The Express Panel list is itself the catchphrase list, so it doubles as the redaction list**: strip known phrases from harvested samples before they ever reach the model. Cheap, and it stays correct as the user edits their buttons.
- Also: the questionnaire's "what do you say a lot?" is therefore **not** a voice field — it **seeds Express Panel buttons**, producing something tappable rather than prompt text nobody sees. And catchphrases become an early concrete use case for the place/person-scoped Express Panel work, since the phrases used with friends are not the ones used with a doctor.

**PLAN OF RECORD for the voice layer: [`Conversant AAC Sounds Like Me.docx`](Documents/Conversant%20AAC%20Sounds%20Like%20Me.docx)** (August 5 2026, generator `generate-sounds-like-me-doc.js`, regenerable; tracked in DOC-SYNC). Ken: *"We need to get to the 'sounds like me' feature (which we only touch on) sooner rather than later."* Five phases — **0** a voice block the prompt can use (small, no user effort; the prompt has said *"You speak AS the user, in their voice"* since v0.3.0 with **nothing behind it**), **1** a short sample-based "How I Sound" module plus the missing C3/C4, **2** harvest voice from actual use (self-populating — `logUserResponse` already records `selectedText`, `selectedIndex` and `allOptions`, so chosen-vs-rejected is on disk and unread), **3** per-person register on the graph edge, **4** the personality/values inventory, last.
- **⚠ SECOND EDITION (August 7 2026) — Ken challenged the plan's central judgment and it did NOT survive. The document was rewritten; the phases above are superseded by the table below.** He asked what authoritative sources backed *"voice is an imitation problem, not a knowledge problem… three sentences the user actually wrote beat any number of trait scores."* The honest answer, after looking: **the first half is supported and the second half was an inference stated as a finding.** Real literature was found for every claim below and is cited in the document's new §10; the searches are what produced the reordering, not the other way round.
  - **What survives:** for an LLM, examples DO steer style better than instructions — few-shot gave up to **23.5× higher style-matching accuracy than zero-shot**, and prompting strategy mattered more than model size (Jemama & Naous 2025); imitation plateaus at ~4–5 examples. **The caveat lands on us**: the largest study of imitating *ordinary* authors (400+ authors, 40k generations) finds models adequate on structured registers and **struggling on informal writing** (Bhandarkar et al. 2025) — which is the only register this app operates in. **No head-to-head of trait-scores vs exemplars exists**, so the "beat any number of trait scores" comparison was never measured.
  - **⚠ THE CORRECTION THAT RESHAPED THE PLAN (Ken): writing style is not speaking style.** Biber's multidimensional analysis of 23 spoken and written genres (*Variation across Speech and Writing*, 1988) separates conversation from written prose systematically. Some idiolectal features cross modality (vocabulary, hedging, humor, directness); **register-level features do not, and register is most of what "sounds like me" means live.** The wrinkle that matters: Biber's dimensions track *production circumstances*, and **AAC composition is slow/planned like writing but interactive like speech**, so it sits in its own place — meaning the best evidence of how this person sounds is **their own AAC output in real conversation**, and the worst is prose written at leisure. That inverts the original Phase-1/Phase-2 priority.
  - **⚠ AND THE COST CLAIM WAS BACKWARDS.** The first edition said "one open box beats ten scale questions." Ken: a writing sample depends on **the slowest feature in the app**. Worse than slow — **the instrument distorts what it measures**: a sample composed over ten effortful minutes is shortened *by the effort*, so you sample "how they write when writing is exhausting" and teach the model they are terse.
  - **THE NEW PRIMARY INSTRUMENT IS FORCED CHOICE (Ken's proposal): "which of these would you be happy to have said?"** Supported four ways — Thurstone's law of comparative judgment (1927) with adaptive comparative judgment reliability ≈0.95–0.97 against noisy rating scales; forced-choice formats reduce social-desirability bias; **recognition is far cheaper than production** (one tap); and it is how LLM preference alignment is actually done. **The project already uses this format** — the Schwartz Portrait Values Questionnaire is comparative. **THE SYNTHESIS THAT DISSOLVES THE TENSION: forced choice yields EXEMPLARS, not adjectives** — the user selects sentences, and selected sentences are examples, so we buy exemplar-shaped prompt material at *recognition* price. **Load-bearing constraint: the item bank must be HAND-AUTHORED**, or the instrument can only discover which corner of the *model's own* range the user prefers; and candidates must vary style while holding content constant, or the user chooses on content.
  - **"The speaker isn't the best judge of what they sound like" (Ken) — correct, and it STRENGTHENS the argument against descriptions.** Vazire's SOKA model (2010): others are better on traits high in **observability and evaluativeness**, which is exactly where speaking style sits; Gosling et al. (1998) on self-reported act frequencies; and the features that most mark an individual (function words) are produced **below conscious awareness** (Tausczik & Pennebaker 2010). It is a weaker caveat on forced choice, because **the app can check self-report against behavior for free** — what they say sounds like them vs. what they actually select live. **Where the two disagree, behavior wins.**
- **DECIDED (Ken, August 7 2026) — the question is AUTHORIZATION, not self-description.** Ken: personality tests are self-perception too, so the objection hits the whole class (Big Five and the PVQ included), not this instrument; and *"is it so bad for the app to speak as the user WOULD LIKE to speak? Authenticity is lost and it can be hard for the user to actually live up to their AI voice."* Three things weighed: aspiration can become real (Gonzales & Hancock 2008 — publicly presenting a trait leads to **internalizing** it); **the descriptive baseline is contaminated by the technology** — terseness after twenty years of typing is a property of the *device*, and Kane et al. (2017) documents AAC users' expression being constrained by their equipment, so **faithfully modeling the observed baseline risks modeling the disability rather than the person**; against which stands Ken's real cost, authenticity and living up to it. **Resolution: change the question rather than pick a side.** Not "which sounds like you" (descriptive — §3.3 says they can't answer accurately) nor "which would you rather be" (drifts), but a question about which one they would **say** — the same judgment the user already makes tapping a card, it sidesteps accuracy entirely (a preference cannot be *inaccurate*), and it is what the app actually needs. **Sounding like a better version of themselves is the user's call.**
  - **⚠ THE EXACT WORDING IS "Which of these sounds most like something you would say IN RESPONSE?" (Ken, August 7 2026 — his phrasing).** The stem is **not one fixed string**: `questionFor(item)` drops "in response" for an item with no partner turn, because that phrase is correct for all twelve items today and **wrong** for the initiating items the bank is still missing (see the gap note below). Responding and initiating are different tasks and the question should say which is being asked. It went through two rejected drafts, and both rejections are the useful part.
    - **DRAFT 1, "would you be happy to have said?" — killed by Ken:** *"'Happy' is a vague term. It could be interpreted as which of these is 'good enough' or 'acceptable'."* The defect is worse than vagueness: **"happy" admits a THRESHOLD reading**, so on a satisficing interpretation several candidates qualify, the user picks the first adequate one, and **the instrument stops discriminating** — the failure mode already flagged for near-equivalent options, arriving through the *question* instead of the candidates.
    - **DRAFT 2, "which would you rather say?" — also killed by Ken.** It fixed the threshold ("rather" is comparative and has no bar to clear) and introduced a subtler one: **in a MADE-UP situation "which would you rather say" is faintly nonsensical**, because the user would not say any of them — it never happened. The stipulation only half-repairs that. "Rather" also leans on preference, which pulls toward an *aspirational* answer at exactly the moment we want the descriptive one.
    - **WHY THE FINAL WORDING DOES NOT REOPEN THE §5.3 SELF-REPORT PROBLEM, which is the thing to check before ever rewording it again:** the psychometric objection (Vazire 2010, Gosling et al. 1998, Tausczik & Pennebaker 2010) lands on **TRAIT-LEVEL self-description** — "how formal are you?", "are you funny?" — which people genuinely cannot answer. "Does this one sentence sound like something you would say" is a **concrete, item-level** judgment, and resemblance survives a hypothetical intact where preference does not. So the wording names the axis as resemblance without asking anyone to characterize themselves.
    - **General rule for any future item wording here: it must be COMPARATIVE (never a bar to clear), ITEM-LEVEL (never a trait question), and answerable about a situation that did not happen.**
  - **The satisficing answer gets its OWN button rather than contaminating the choice** — the two escapes are **"They all sound like me"** (a weak/absent preference, recorded as what it is instead of as a spurious first-place vote) and **"I wouldn't say any of these"** (the genuinely different state, and at least as informative — an unprompted negative constraint). **Both were reworded onto the question's axis when the question changed (Aug 7 2026):** the old "They're all fine" read as *tolerance*, which is the satisficing note the separate button exists to keep OUT of the main choice, and "I wouldn't say any of these" is now the exact negation of the question rather than merely adjacent to it.
  - **The phrasing also replicates the LIVE constraint, which is what licenses the Section-7 convergence measure:** a real palette forces one choice among four, so an item that forces one choice among four measures the same act. A question that collected approval *ratings* would not be comparable to behavior.
  - **⚠ THE USER AND THE PARTNER ARE ASKED DIFFERENT QUESTIONS ON PURPOSE — this is SOKA applied, not an inconsistency.** User → **preference** ("which would you rather say?"); partner → **descriptive** ("which sounds most like how he talks with you?"). The user cannot accurately describe their own style but *can* express a preference; the partner cannot express the user's preference but *can* report observed behavior. **Each is asked the question they are qualified to answer, and swapping them would produce the weakest version of both instruments.**
- **DECIDED (Ken, August 7 2026) — partner review is OFFERED, never required.** Per SOKA the people who know the user well are better judges of observable style; agreement rises when the observer has seen the person **across many contexts** (family/close friends, not coworkers or acquaintances), so the invitation names that kind of person. Fits the person-centered profile tradition the question bank already draws on (Millar & Aitken 2003, Communication Passports). **Four constraints, because the failure here is not a bad profile but a person being described by others in a product built so they would not have to be:** (1) the **user** invites, the app never approaches a partner; (2) the user sees every answer and can discard any of it; (3) partner input is marked **second-hand** and never outranks the user's own — a disagreement is information, not an error to correct in the user's direction; (4) partner answers are **private to the user** unless they choose to share them back (Ken: *"as long as it doesn't lead to hurt feelings"* — a mother answering about her son is answering about *their relationship*). **Design dividend: a partner is qualified about their OWN relationship with the user, which is exactly Phase-3 graph-edge data — so the partner channel IS the Phase-3 instrument, not a parallel one.**
- **DECIDED (Ken, August 7 2026) — the Express Panel ships HALF-populated.** Ken's middle way between an empty grid (blank holes on day one, feature never discovered, app reads as broken) and a full one (complete, so it invites nothing): **half the cells carry defaults, the rest are displayed blank, inviting the user's own words.** Supported by the **endowed progress effect** (Nunes & Drèze 2006) — a task framed as begun-but-incomplete is completed more often than the same task framed as not started. **The plumbing keeps its cells** (Yes/No/Okay/Please/Thank you/Sorry/Wait/Help/Hi/Bye — immediately useful, and makes no claim about the user); **the flavored items vacate** ("That's funny", "See you later", "I think so", "I'm not sure" — they *look* like voice while being ours).
  - **⚠ §3.3 of the first edition was FACTUALLY WRONG and Ken caught it: it called the Express Panel "user-authored."** As shipped **all 32 items are ours** (6 feelings + 26 phrases, [express-items.js:83](app/js/express-items.js:83)); it becomes theirs only after non-trivial per-row editing. The defaults are **not** a mistake — they are conversational plumbing, and nobody's identity is in "Yes," so unlike a fabricated catchphrase they make no false claim. **The mistake was counting the panel as voice evidence without distinguishing whose words are in it.**
  - **PROVENANCE MARKING is therefore a dependency of Phase 0, not a later refinement** (default / user-added / user-edited). Three payoffs for one field: only user-touched items count as voice signal; the **Phase-2 catchphrase redaction list becomes the user's phrases, not ours** (redacting against defaults would strip "Yes" and "Thank you" from every harvested sample); and it yields the **"Express items added or edited"** personalization-depth measure the Aug 7 2026 instrumentation decision asked for. **Seeding the voice block from shipped defaults would tell the model this user's characteristic vocabulary is "Yes", "No" and "Thank you."**
  - **DECIDED (Ken, August 7 2026) — tapping a blank cell OPENS THE EDITOR for that cell, but NOT during a conversation. BUILT August 8 2026, together with the Settings half below.** Ken: *"a great idea since it serves to make positioning the tile easier. I'd just turn that option off when the user is involved in a conversation."* **His reason is the better half and is not the one I had:** the win is not discovery, it is that the user edits the cell **in the grid position they want it**, instead of typing into a list and then reordering rows until it lands there — so **position and content stop being two separate operations**, which is a direct answer to the "editing is a non-trivial exercise" complaint.
    - **The restriction is doing real SAFETY work, not tidiness.** A filled cell **speaks** on tap; a blank cell would **open an editor**. Those are radically different consequences for adjacent holes in a keyguard, and this population taps imprecisely (the reason the double-tap safeguard exists at all). A stray tap on a blank cell today does nothing; **opening a full-screen editor mid-conversation is worse than nothing.** Confining it to the gaps between conversations removes the only genuinely bad outcome and costs nothing, since nobody composes their button set mid-exchange.
    - **The predicate is the CONVERSATION, not the moment** — the span between Start conversation and End conversation, **not** instantaneous engine state (`STANDBY`/floor/palette). A control that works at some moments *within* a conversation and not others is less predictable than one simply unavailable for the duration, and predictability is worth more than reach for this population. **One-sentence rule: you can edit your buttons between conversations, not during one.** *(Note the panel is not tappable before the first Start at all — `main.disabled` blocks pointer events on everything but `#startBlock` — so the real window is after Start and outside a conversation span.)*
    - **DECIDED (Ken, August 7 2026) — the same tap-to-edit/tap-to-speak works on the Settings → Express tab, with NO conversation gate.** Ken: *"It could be valuable to allow the tap to edit/speak feature of express panel buttons when on the express tab of settings. That means making the panel enabled for that tab."* This makes the editor what it should have been — **a view of the panel as it will actually appear, edited in place** — and the gate is unnecessary here for exactly the reason it exists on the conversation surface: **in Settings every tap is already deliberate.**
      - **⚠ IT IS CHEAPER THAN "make the panel enabled" SOUNDS, and CLAUDE.md's own v0.5.25 note ("the dock is empty/reserved on About Me & Settings") will mislead the build — VERIFIED against shipped source August 7 2026:** nothing hides `#expressPanel` when Settings opens, and `#settingsDialog` is **deliberately inset to clear the dock** (`right/left: calc(--app-margin + --dock-w + --dock-sep)` for a side dock, `bottom: … + --dock-h + …` for a bottom dock — [styles.css:1519](app/css/styles.css:1519)). **So the panel is ALREADY rendered and visible beside Settings on every tab. It is merely INERT**, because `showModal()` makes everything outside the dialog non-interactive.
      - **The work is therefore only to make it REACHABLE, and there is an exact precedent:** the keyboard escapes modal inertness by **reparenting itself into the open dialog** to share its top layer — `field.closest('dialog[open]') || document.body` in `show()` ([keyboard.js:641](app/js/keyboard.js:641)) and, the closer precedent, **`previewShow()`** ([keyboard.js:785](app/js/keyboard.js:785)), which does it with **no focused field at all** — a dock element made live inside Settings purely so the user can look at it while changing settings against it. That is precisely the Express Panel's case.
      - **The genuine conflict: the panel and the keyboard occupy the IDENTICAL dock footprint, which is the whole point of the shared grid (Rule 9).** On the Express tab both are wanted — panel at rest, keyboard once a text field has focus (`#expressEditor input` has been in the keyboard's `IN_SCOPE` since v0.5.36) — so **the dock swaps between them**, tolerable on a Settings surface in a way it would not be during a conversation. **In physical-keyboard mode there is no conflict at all** and the panel simply stays up.
      - **~~Decide at build time~~ — DECIDED at build time, August 8 2026: a tap EDITS and a labelled 🔊 button SPEAKS. The two questions turned out to be separable, and separating them is what makes both easy.** The flagged worry was that a preview tap is audible to anyone in the room and should not enter the transcript. **The real problem was that a bare tap is AMBIGUOUS** — on a keyguard-backed grid it might be a mis-hit — so making it speak means a stray touch says something aloud, while making it silent loses the ability to hear a phrase at all. **A per-row 🔊 in the editor is unambiguous by construction** (nobody presses a button marked "hear this" by accident), so the tap can safely be the quiet action: it selects and reveals that button's row. The Settings tap therefore has no speech, no transcript entry, and no influencer toggle — tapping a Partner or Place there would otherwise change conversation state the user cannot see from an editing screen.
        - **⚠ THE 🔊 WAS AN OMISSION I DID NOT NOTICE, AND KEN CAUGHT IT ("is there a reason we didn't include a Speak button... per phrase?").** There was no reason. Worse, **the project had already written down the argument for it twice** — About Me's "Speak my answer" (a Build Step 2 requirement in its own right) and Sound Check's per-candidate speaker, whose comment states the case exactly: *this user's whole output channel is a synthesizer, so reading a phrase on screen is not the same test as hearing it.* **The lesson is not "add a speak button" — it is that when a new editing surface handles the user's spoken words, the existing speak-it-here precedents are the checklist**, and I reasoned about the new surface in isolation instead. Phrases only: partner/feeling/place are toggles whose labels are never spoken, so a speaker there would offer to play something the app will never say. Deliberately NOT disabled on an empty field — text commits on input *without* re-rendering (so the field keeps focus while typing), so a disabled state set at build time would still say "empty" after the user had typed; it reads the live value and no-ops. Verified: it speaks a freshly-typed value, not a stale one.

  - **AS BUILT (August 8 2026) — three things a future session needs, and the first is the non-obvious one.**
    - **⚠ THE ITEM LIST IS DENSE — position N in the list IS cell N of the grid — so a cell PAST THE END OF THE LIST CANNOT BE ADDRESSED AT ALL.** Without something to hold the gap, "put a button in this particular cell" could only ever mean "append to the end", which is precisely the two-step operation tap-to-define exists to collapse. Hence a fourth item type, **`empty`** (`express-items.newEmptyItem`): it holds a position and carries nothing else. Tapping a cell pads the list with empties up to that index, so the new button lands in the cell that was actually tapped. **They are stamped `origin: DEFAULT` deliberately** — an empty slot contains no words, so counting it as the user's would inflate the personalization-depth measure with cells that say nothing about them, and `ensureOrigin` needs its own branch for them because the text test would otherwise read a missing text as "not one of ours".
    - **An undefined cell is an `.ep-btn` with a modifier class, NOT a bespoke element**, so its box is inherited rather than re-declared. This is the `.ep-cell-blank` lesson (a flex item's `flex-basis: 0` floors at its own padding + border, so a differently-boxed cell resolves narrower and shifts every cell after it). The rule may change colour and nothing else — including keeping the 5px left bar at 5px and merely making it transparent. Guarded by a test in `tests/styles.test.mjs` that fails on any box property appearing in the rule. **Measured in both docks: an undefined cell and a defined one are identical to two decimal places.**
    - **The panel is made live in Settings by REPARENTING `#expressPanel` into the open dialog** (`ui.setExpressPanelHost`), exactly as `keyboard.previewShow` does. **`#dockArea` itself is not movable** — it is an in-flow flex child in the bottom dock — so the panel moves and `.ep-in-settings` pins it back onto the identical rect from the identical variables. **Measured in both docks: a cell sits at the same coordinates docked, hosted, and docked again**, which is the property Rule 11 requires. `hostExpressPanel(false)` runs on every close path *and* at the top of `openSettings`, because a missed close would strand the panel inside a hidden dialog and the dock would open empty.
    - **The conversation gate is evaluated at TAP time, not at render time.** Nothing about the cell changes with conversation state, so there is no render to trigger and no re-render to miss — and the cell looks and measures the same either way, which Rule 1 wants anyway. `conversationInProgress()` is derived (`practiceMode || isListening || history || partner text || a live palette || a static palette showing`) rather than a flag set at each conversation-opening site; `terminateConversation()` clears every one of those, which is what makes the answer go back to false. Verified: Start conversation → a tap does nothing; End conversation → the same tap opens the editor.
    - **Limitation accepted (Ken: *"That limitation is fine. By the time the user has all the buttons filled they will have internalized the purpose and behavior of the express panel."*):** the affordance disappears once every cell is filled, so in-situ positioning helps while *populating* and not while *rearranging*. Editing a filled cell in place would need a distinct gesture (long press), and **adding a gesture to the conversation surface is a bigger decision than this one** — deliberately not proposed.
      - **⚠ THE PER-PERSON/PLACE SCOPED PANEL CUTS BOTH WAYS HERE (Ken flagged the complication).** It **softens** the limitation — each new scope starts with **empty cells**, so the affordance keeps earning its place long after the default set is full instead of retiring once. It also **sharpens a question the scoped-panel build must answer: when a cell is tapped, WHICH SCOPE is being edited?** A user who edits what they believe is their clinic panel and in fact edits the global one has been misled, and **the reverse error is worse because it is silent.** The active scope must be **unmistakable at the moment of editing**, not merely selectable somewhere on the tab.
      - Also note **choice chips claim leading cells dynamically**, so a cell blank at rest may be occupied during a closed-set turn — **the conversation gate already prevents that collision**, a second thing it buys beyond the safety argument.
- **⚠ DECIDED (Ken, August 7 2026) — the CYRANO PROBLEM is a first-class risk, and the voice layer MAKES IT WORSE.** Ken (from his presentations): *"a more insidious problem is when the user defers to the AI because they believe that the AI is a better communicator. See: Cyrano de Bergerac and his friend Christian."* **This is measured, not speculative:** Jakesch et al. (CHI 2023) gave 1,506 participants a writing assistant tilted toward one side; it changed what they wrote **and the opinions they subsequently reported on a survey** — they name it **latent persuasion**. General form: automation *misuse* is over-reliance (Parasuraman & Riley 1997). The AAC-specific felt version is Valencia et al. (CHI 2023), where choosing a generated phrase made AAC users feel the **system** had chosen.
  - **The uncomfortable part, stated rather than buried: today a card that doesn't sound like the user produces FRICTION, and that friction is what sends them to "In my own words." It is the user's own defense against deferring — and it is powered by exactly the mismatch this plan exists to eliminate. Succeeding at the goal removes the signal.**
  - **Ken's counterweight, recorded because it constrains the mitigations:** *"falling back on a less than 'totally me' response may be more than acceptable if it keeps you in the conversation."* That is the product's founding premise (the 4-second silence), so **the answer is emphatically not to make the cards worse.**
  - **What actually mitigates:** (1) free composition must not be so much slower than tapping that deferring becomes *rational* — this reframes the **composition-speed work (prediction, phrase/abbreviation expansion) as a DEPENDENCY of the voice layer**, not an unrelated convenience; (2) **never rank cards by quality** — position carries category, not rank (Aug 7 2026), and a best-first palette is a standing invitation to defer to slot 1 (a second, independent reason for a decision taken on other grounds); (3) measure it, accepting the ambiguity — **a falling composed-turn share moves the same way whether the layer is succeeding or the user is deferring**, so only the "does this sound like you?" mark separates them, which is why it is the primary instrument; (4) keep the catchphrase decision in force — a device producing the user's own idiom unprompted is the hardest of all to tell from the user.
- **The failure mode to design against in Phase 2: a selected card is the MODEL's wording, not the user's.** Feeding selections back as style exemplars teaches the model to imitate itself and converge on its own house style *while looking like personalization*. **REWEIGHTED in the second edition:** the first edition ranked selections lowest, which is right about *exemplars* and wrong about *preference* — a selection is a forced-choice judgment against three rejected alternatives, made in real conversation, with no observation effect, at zero cost, i.e. **behavioral evidence, and behavior beats self-report.** So selections are now the **authority for preference** and remain **unusable as style exemplars**; composed "In my own words" prose stays the exemplar authority. The split between the two columns is the design.
- **⚠ GAP IN THE SHIPPED BANK (found August 7 2026 while settling the question wording; NOT yet filled): every one of the twelve items is RESPONSIVE.** All give a partner turn and ask how the user would reply, so the bank measures only their **responding** voice. The app generates a great deal of **initiating** text — conversation starters, the INITIATIVE slot, wind-downs, and the statements the floor-aware Reframe produces — and **none of it is governed by anything the user has told us.** The fix is items with no `partner` field ("Suppose you want to ask someone for help." → "Could you give me a hand?" / "Would you mind helping me?" / "Can you help?"); `questionFor` already asks them the right question and the renderer already omits the partner line, so it is authoring work only. A test asserts the bank is currently all-responsive, so adding one trips a visible reminder rather than sliding in unnoticed.
- **REVISED PHASES (second edition):** **0** voice block (+ Express provenance marking) → **1** **"Sound Check"** forced-choice module (~12 taps, hand-authored bank) + negative constraints + C3/C4, with **writing samples demoted to optional** and framed as conversational turns → **2** harvest from use, **selections promoted to the preference authority** (the raw material has been on disk since v0.3.0 and read by nothing; the aggregation half can start before the rest) → **3** per-person register on the graph edge, sharing the Phase-1 instrument via the partner channel → **4** personality/values last (the *values* half retains independent worth; the personality half is the weakest signal per unit of effort in the bank).

---

**Build Step 2 design intent — "Speak my answer" control (Ken, June 14 2026).** The questionnaire UI must include an on-demand **"speak my answer"** control during worldview collection, rendered **in-flow and close at hand** next to each answer — explicitly **not** in the Settings panel. Speaking an answer is opt-in/on-demand, never the automatic behavior that selecting a conversation *response* has. Rationale (scenarios that make spoken answers useful during collection): supporter-assisted entry (speech is the user's channel to the human helping); auditory proofread of free-text / "in my own words" answers; voice-match calibration (esp. B8 "Your Voice, in Your Words" — judge "does this sound like me?"); and doing the "getting to know you" session as a real conversation with another person. An **auto-speak toggle** (for the supporter-assisted and conversation-as-collection scenarios) is a reasonable later addition, but the always-available per-answer control is the requirement.

**Origin:** The interim *Your Name / About You* fields in Settings (shipped June 2026 to stop the LLM emitting `[Name]` placeholders) are a stopgap. The worldview questionnaire replaces and generalizes them. Bracketed placeholders are the signal of which personal facts conversations demand; the design turns that signal into a self-populating profile.

**Documents produced (in this folder, styled like the other design docs):**
1. `Worldview-Questionnaire-Draft1.docx` — the question bank. Three tiers: **A** Concrete Core (identity, place, people, daily life, contact), **C** Interests/Passions/Preferences (the layer the frameworks miss — Ken's explicit add), **B** Abstract Characteristics (personality via Big Five, values via Schwartz Portrait format, humor, register/style, beliefs, how-you-treat-different-people, emotional landscape, voice-in-your-words). Grounded in Big Five, Schwartz basic human values, and the AAC Communication Passport / "All About Me" tradition.
2. `Worldview-Implementation-Plan.docx` — build-ready plan. Slots into the Architecture Overview as **§17** (or expands §6). Covers data model, files, the gaps-log mechanism, conversation integration, UI flow, migration, build order, verification, and decisions-to-confirm.

**Settled design decisions:**
- **Three tiers** A (concrete, fills placeholders) / C (interests) / B (abstract, shapes voice). Abstract questions organized by *conversational lever* (what they change in output), not psychological taxonomy. Values use Schwartz **Portrait** format ("here's a person — how like you?").
- **Three field states:** Unanswered (eligible to ask/resurface) / Answered / **Declined** (sticky — never ask again, always phrase around; un-declinable). Skip ≠ Decline. Every question optional; full value at zero answers; chunked sessions.
- **Privacy model — three levels (decided Ken, June 19 2026):** A field's privacy is independent of its answer state. The three levels are:
  - **Shareable** (default): value is sent to the AI freely; the AI may use it naturally in any response.
  - **Private** (user-set flag, or `defaultPrivacy: private` in the registry): value **is** sent to the AI — the AI uses it for context — but the AI is instructed not to raise it on its own initiative. This is the correct behavior for sensitive-but-useful facts (phone number, home address, people the user wants the AI to know about without naming unprompted). *Previously, Private incorrectly withheld the value entirely — making it equivalent to Declined. That was wrong: a user who fills in their phone number intends the AI to be able to offer "My number is X" as a response option when asked; the privacy guarantee is that it isn't spoken unless the user chooses it.*
    - **"Unprompted" MUST be stated with its referents, or the rule is unfollowable — and from the June 2026 privacy model until now it wasn't (Ken caught this August 3 2026).** All three models (`worldview.js`, `relationships.js`, and the freshly-written `places.js`, which faithfully copied the defect) ended the private instruction with *"only include them if the user's chosen response requires it"* — or *"…if the user selects a response that does"*. **Ken's question is the whole finding: how does the assistant "bring anything up" other than through the response palette? And if it never writes a card carrying the fact, what is there for the user to select?** The clause was describing the **speaking** gate but phrasing it as an **authoring** rule, so it named a mechanism that cannot exist at the moment the options are written. It is now replaced everywhere by the two prompts that are real:
      1. **The PARTNER asks about it.** Conversationally prompted; this is the case the phone-number example above has always intended.
      2. **The USER steers** — types what they want conveyed in "In my own words" and taps **Reframe**. This already worked and needed no new mechanism: `llm.js`'s `steerBlock` hands the text to the model as *"user-authored, they are TRUE and override the 'keep it general' caution"*, which is exactly the deliberate "I want this said" gesture. Naming it inside the private block closes the gap from the other side, since `steerBlock` had only ever named *one* caution it overrides. (**In my own words → Speak** is a third route, but it bypasses the AI entirely rather than unlocking anything.)
    - **The separation to keep straight, because collapsing it is what caused the defect:** *"nothing is spoken until the user taps it"* is a property of **SELECTION**, is always true of every card private or not, and is the actual privacy guarantee. *"Don't raise it unprompted"* is a property of **AUTHORING** and is the only part the model can act on. Never write the first as an instruction to the model.
    - **Guarded by tests** in all three models (`tests/{worldview,relationships,places}.test.mjs`): each asserts the private block names both prompts and asserts the old circular clause is *absent*, so it cannot creep back.
    - **Also fixed in the same pass — the A5 module note was stale and user-visible**: `worldview-questions.json` still told the user Contact & Logistics was *"strict phrase-around for this build"*, i.e. the pre-v0.3.12 behavior where the value was withheld outright. It has said the wrong thing on screen since Private was corrected.
  - **Prefer not to say** (declined field): the user chose not to answer. The AI receives **no** value — only a phrase-around instruction. For information the user does not want the AI to know at all.
  - *The same three levels apply to people in the relationship graph: a non-private person is described freely; a private person's details are sent with "don't bring them up unprompted"; a person the user chose not to add is simply absent.*
- **Gaps-log mechanism:** the no-bracket / phrase-around rule stays, so gaps can't be read off the output. Instead the single combined generation call returns structured `{classification, options, missing_facts}`; `missing_facts` (normalized field keys) feed a gaps log that drives the questionnaire's "suggested next." Regex-on-output is only a fallback. This is **progressive profiling** — the questionnaire fills itself from real conversations.
- **Storage:** user-owned → `worldview.json` in the FSA data folder (localStorage cache), plus static `app/data/worldview-questions.json` registry. Contact info (A5) and beliefs (B5) are **private by default** — the AI receives the values for context but is instructed not to raise them on its own initiative; it offers one only when the partner asks or the user steers via Reframe, and (as with every card) it is spoken only when the user taps it. See the privacy-model bullet above for why those two prompts must be named explicitly.
- **Sequencing guardrail:** keep the Name/About You Settings fields live until the questionnaire UI ships, then migrate legacy `userName`/`userAbout` into `worldview.json` and remove them. Do NOT remove early — it would regress the live app.

**Build order (from the plan doc):** 1) data model + registry → 2) questionnaire UI → 3) LLM integration (structured `missing_facts`, profile block, gaps recording) → 4) migrate + remove Name/About You → 5) expand Tier-B/Tier-C content → 6) RAG-lite.

**Token budget / RAG timing (plan doc §6):** We are *not* close to needing a RAG database. The questionnaire profile is bounded (~1–3K tokens fully answered = 0.1–0.3% of Sonnet 4.6's 1M context; ~a penny per conversation with prompt caching). RAG is driven by *unbounded cross-session memory* (Phase 3), not the structured fields — rough trigger is an always-relevant slice exceeding ~10–20K tokens. `buildBlock()` ships as simple heuristic selection (always-include core + topical facts on-topic; many turns need none); progression is heuristic → client-side embeddings (IndexedDB) → server-side vector store, the last only if cross-session memory becomes a headline feature (awkward in a no-backend app).

**Prompt caching — BUILT August 8 2026 (Ken: "build it… we should watch this going forward"). Supersedes the June 15 2026 "not built" entry; the padding decision below still stands and was never the lever.** Triggered by an Anthropic notice that the org's cache hit rate was low. It was **zero** — there was no `cache_control` anywhere — and the cause was one line of ordering, exactly as the June entry predicted: `generateResponses` interpolated the per-turn engine context into the **middle** of the system prompt, so nothing after it could cache. **`generateResponses` now sends `system` as two blocks with a breakpoint between them:** `[fixed instructions + perCat + profile]` cached, `[situation + engine context + avoid/steer/focus]` not. Measured in the running app: head **13,214 bytes ≈ 3,570 tokens**, byte-identical across two calls that differ per-turn; tail 136 bytes.
- **CORRECTION to the June figures: the floor for Sonnet 4.6 is 1024 tokens, not 2048** (2048 is Opus 4.7; Opus 4.6/4.5 and Haiku 4.5 are 4096; Opus 5 and Fable 5 are 512). **The floor is NOT monotonic across generations** — do not infer it from how new a model is, and re-check it on any model change, because a prefix below it fails silently (`cache_creation_input_tokens: 0`, no error). The June conclusion is unaffected: the prefix clears every one of those numbers *without* a profile, so there is still nothing to pad.
- **ONLY `generateResponses` is cached, and this is a decision, not an omission.** A cache entry costs 1.25× to write and 0.1× to read, so it needs two reads to break even. That call is the only one that fires repeatedly against the same prefix — every silence checkpoint regenerates for the same partner turn, and the 0.5s silence period (Aug 7 2026) makes that several calls per turn plus regenerates. The other five fire once or rarely per conversation, so caching them would write entries nobody reads. **A shared SUFFIX is not a shared prefix:** all five share `buildProfileBlock()`, and that buys them nothing, because each opens with different instructions.
- **`buildProfileBlock()` was split, and the situation block is the piece that moved.** Voice/worldview/relationships/places change only on an About Me / People / Places edit — never mid-conversation — so they sit *inside* the cached prefix. The **situation block** (Partner / Feeling / Place) does not: **Feeling can be tapped mid-conversation**, and one changed byte would discard a ~3,500-token entry to save re-sending ~50. It is now `buildSituationBlock()`, appended after the breakpoint. **The trap this creates: the three uncached callers got the situation *through* `buildProfileBlock()`, so they must append `buildSituationBlock()` themselves or the model silently loses who the user is talking to.** Guarded by a test.
- **⚠ THE COST DISPLAY HAD TO CHANGE OR IT WOULD HAVE LIED — this is the load-bearing half, not polish.** With caching on, **`usage.input_tokens` is the UNCACHED REMAINDER**, not the prompt size (the prompt is `input_tokens + cache_creation + cache_read`), and the three bill at different rates. Pricing the one field would have **under-reported the bill by the hit rate — ~90% of the input on the hot path** — in a product whose entire funding model is "you pay only for what you use". A cost display that reads low is worse than none. So the three are counted separately end to end: `trackUsage` → `addUsageTokens({input, output, cacheWrite, cacheRead})` → `pricing.json` multipliers (1.25 / 0.1) → the About tab. **`addUsageTokens` now takes an OBJECT** — the bucket count has changed twice, and a caller passing three of four positionally is a silent mispricing rather than an error. Verified live: 1k/5k/10k/90k tokens renders **$0.14**, where the old arithmetic would have shown **$0.08**.
- **The watch mechanism Ken asked for: Settings → About shows "N% of prompt cached"** beside the spend, shown only once there is cache activity. **A falling number is the signal**, and it means something is invalidating the prefix — which surfaces as spend and nothing else. Nothing in the request or the response ever says "your prefix moved."
- **THE FAILURE MODE THESE TESTS EXIST FOR: caching breaks SILENTLY AND PERFECTLY.** Move per-turn content back into the cached block and the app still works, every response is still correct, and the bill quietly triples. There is no error to catch, which is why `tests/llm.test.mjs` asserts the *split* — sentinel values for context/steer/avoid/focus/situation must be absent from the head and present in the tail — rather than only asserting prompt content. **Same shape as the dropped-CSS-rule and the false-timeout findings: when the screen looks right, suspect the thing that reports nothing.**
- **A second lesson from fixing the 18 tests this broke:** they read `body.system` as a string, so a transport change failed eighteen tests about *prompt content*. They now go through a `sysText()` helper that flattens whatever shape `system` is. **Assert what the model sees, not how the request carries it** — otherwise a future breakpoint move fails a wall of unrelated tests.
- **Deliberately NOT done: a second breakpoint on the growing `messages` history.** It would cache the conversation incrementally, but the partner's turn is *rewritten in place* after the background cleanup pass, which mutates the history prefix and would cost the entry anyway. Revisit only if measurement shows history is a real share of the input; the system prefix is the dominant win.
- **⚠ WHO THE SAVING IS FOR — token-cost work is justified by TESTERS and product economics, NEVER by Ken's bill (Ken, August 8 2026): *"The development of the app dwarfs the API key cost."*** **The two terms, since both are Anthropic charges to Ken and the names blur (clarified by Ken the same day): "development cost" = his sessions with Claude Code building the app; "API key cost" = his own key exercising the app to test it.** The second is noise against the first, so a future session that proposes token work as a saving for *him* has the economics backwards. **⚠ AND THE STRONGER CONSEQUENCE: an org-level cost signal is NOT a measurement of the app.** The August 8 notice quoted ~46%, which is close to the app's own arithmetic — but the app is the small share of that org's traffic, Claude Code is the large one, and Claude Code already caches by default, so the resemblance is coincidence rather than corroboration. Do not treat an Anthropic cost or cache notice as evidence about app behavior; the app's own numbers are in Settings → About. It matters because **at beta each tester brings their own key, and their spend is the app's spend with no development cost mixed in** — the same ~46% is a rounding error here and roughly half the bill there. That is the "user-funded AI, pay only for what you use" premise the whole no-backend strategy rests on. **Consequence for Ken's own instrumentation: the hit-rate readout is a REGRESSION signal, not a cost line** — a drop means something invalidated the prefix, and spend is merely the only place it shows.
- Cache mechanics (`cache_control`, the floors, the `usage.cache_*` fields) are Anthropic-specific — one more item for the multi-vendor adapter list.

**Decisions resolved (Ken, June 14 2026) — these scope Build Step 1:**
1. **First-cut Tier-A set: FULL.** Author all Concrete-Core fields from the question bank now (not a smaller starter). Plus a starter of Tier-C interests (per build order).
2. **Contact info (A5): STRICT phrase-around for now.** Stored but never volunteered or shared in output. *Note / TODO:* add a per-field "assistant may share if I pick it" sharing control in a later build.
3. **Chunk size: NO FIXED CHUNK.** No enforced per-session stopping point; user answers as many or as few as they like each sitting. (The "suggested next" from the gaps log still guides ordering.)
4. ~~**Tier-B beliefs (B5): DEFERRED** to a later build.~~ **SUPERSEDED August 8 2026 (Ken: *"Let's finish all of Tier B"*) — B5 is BUILT.** See "Tier B is complete" below.
5. **Symbol/picture answers + supporter-assisted mode: LATER.** Build Step 1 supports typed/selected text answers only.

Also decided this session: the **type-and-speak composer** ("In My Own Words", § "Free Composition + Virtual Keyboard") is built alongside Build Step 1 as the manual worldview test pathway. See that section.

**Next-session kickoff prompt:**
> We're continuing the AI-Driven AAC worldview questionnaire work. Read the "Worldview Questionnaire (June 2026)" section of CLAUDE.md and the two design docs in this folder: `Worldview-Questionnaire-Draft1.docx` (question bank) and `Worldview-Implementation-Plan.docx` (build-ready plan). Two phases this session.
>
> **Phase 1 — Resolve the open decisions first, with me.** Plan doc §11 lists five decisions to confirm before building: (1) size of the first-cut Tier-A set (full vs. smaller starter); (2) contact info (A5) — per-field "assistant may share if I pick it" toggle vs. strict phrase-around for now; (3) target chunk size (questions per session); (4) whether Tier-B beliefs (B5) ships in this build or waits; (5) symbol/picture answers + supporter-assisted mode now or later. Use AskUserQuestion, recommend a default for each, and record my answers in this CLAUDE.md section.
>
> **Phase 2 — Build Step 1: data model + question registry** (plan §2–3). Create `app/data/worldview-questions.json` — the static registry (modules → fields with key, question text, type, answer options, placeholder synonyms `fills`, `sensitive`, `defaultPrivacy`, follow-ups), authored from the question-bank doc, scoped to whatever first-cut set we agreed in Phase 1 (Tier-A core + a starter of Tier-C interests at minimum). Create `app/js/worldview.js` — load/save `worldview.json` to the FSA data folder (follow `storage.js` patterns) with a localStorage cache; get/set/decline/reset fields; the three field states (unanswered = key absent / answered / declined = sticky); gaps add/list/clear; and `buildBlock()` returning the compact profile text for LLM injection (simple heuristic selection, no vector store — plan §6).
>
> Guardrails: do NOT touch the interim Name/About You Settings fields (they stay until Build Step 4). Honor declined/private at buildBlock time (never volunteer; phrase around). Gaps come from structured `missing_facts` in the generation response, not regex (plan §4) — that wiring is Build Step 3; for now just build the gaps add/list/clear API. Verify in the preview that worldview.js round-trips `worldview.json` through the data folder (set/decline/reset, buildBlock output, gaps add/list) before stopping. No commit or deploy unless I ask.
