## "In My Own Words" — Free Composition + Virtual Keyboard (June 2026) — decided, not yet built

Free composition is one of the **invariants** (Configuration Model): the user must always have a path to type/compose something the AI did not generate and have the app speak it in the selected voice. The UI's **composer-access region** (UI-Design.docx four-region layout) is where this lives. "In my own words" is the user-facing name for that path.

**Why this surfaced now:** to test the worldview Tier model (and any response shaping) by hand before the AI generation path exercises it, we need a manual way to compose an utterance and speak it. So the simple composer is no longer just a far-future nicety — it's the **test harness for worldview output** and is wanted alongside Build Step 1.

### Settled decisions

- **The keyboard is the easy part; voice + return-to-UI are essentially free.** Any keyboard (OS or in-app) just deposits text into a focused field in the page. The app then speaks it via the existing `tts.speak()` path (already uses the app-selected voice), and the app UI was never actually left. No integration risk on the "speak in the app's voice" / "return to the app" requirements regardless of keyboard choice.

- **OS / browser keyboard cannot be reliably summoned, and is a black box — so it is a *fallback*, not the foundation.** Technical reality on a Windows tablet (Edge/Chrome):
  - There is **no web API to programmatically launch the Windows touch keyboard.** It auto-appears only when an editable element (`input`/`textarea`/`contenteditable`) receives focus *and* the device is in a touch context. "Invoke from a button" therefore means "focus a text field and hope the OS pops the keyboard."
  - On a Surface with the type cover attached (laptop mode) it often won't auto-pop at all. Behavior varies by tablet mode, Windows version, and browser.
  - The Chromium **VirtualKeyboard API** (`navigator.virtualKeyboard.show()`) only manages an already-available keyboard's overlay; it still requires focus on an editable element. Not a reliable on-demand "open system keyboard" switch.
  - It gives **zero control** over layout, key size, spacing, prediction, or access method — exactly the things that matter for a CP user's motor accessibility.

- **The real path is an in-app virtual keyboard.** It is the only option that:
  1. **Honors access-method independence** (engine emits a palette descriptor; renderers present it — direct select now, scanning / eye-gaze later). An OS keyboard can't participate in the future scanning renderer; an in-app keyboard *is* just another renderer.
  2. **Unlocks AI-assisted composition** — type a few words → expand into a full sentence *in the user's voice* (worldview model); personalized word/phrase prediction. The OS keyboard can touch none of this.
  3. Keeps visual + behavioral consistency (key sizing, triple-coding, dark mode, latency dots).

### Build order (decided)

1. **Type-and-speak composer — DONE (June 14 2026).** Added an "In your own words:" region to `index.html` (textarea + Speak + Clear), `ui.js` helpers (`onSpeakClick`/`onClearComposerClick`/`getComposerText`/`clearComposer`), and `handleSpeakComposed()` in `app.js` → `tts.speak()` in the selected voice. MVP behavior: speaks the composed text, guards empty/whitespace input, returns status to Listening/Ready. Works with whatever keyboard the OS offers (and a hardware keyboard in dev). This is the MVP realization of the free-composition invariant. A "use system keyboard" affordance was not added (and must never be depended on for summoning the OS keyboard). **Deferred sub-decision — RESOLVED (Ken, Pass 14, June 21 2026), v0.3.19: tapping Speak IS taking the floor, so it behaves like selecting a response.** Previously the composer only spoke the text and left the partner's turn open, recording still running. Now `handleSpeakComposed()` mirrors `handleMoveSelected`: it stops the filler ladder, invalidates in-flight generation (`generationToken++`), **stops recording** (`stt.stopListening()`), captures + clears the partner's `currentPartnerText`, speaks, **terminates the partner's statement** (`engine.selectMove({text})` pops the partner FPP → floor OPEN, records `lastUserUtterance` so Say-again works on it), clears the stale palette + composer, **commits the exchange to history** (`commitExchange(raw, text, -1)` — partner turn cleaned + logged, then the composed user turn), and **resumes listening iff auto-resume is armed** via the shared `resumeOrIdle()` (same `manualListenArmed && loadAutoRelisten()` gate as a palette pick — so it only re-listens when the user has manually started at least once this session). `commitExchange` now logs `allOptions: []` for index < 0 (free-composed / repair utterances aren't palette picks, so no stale palette is attributed to them). Verified in preview: `selectMove` pops a seeded partner INVITATION FPP (floor self→open, stack emptied); clicking Speak clears the composer + `#responseOptions`, runs the idle branch of `resumeOrIdle` (listening not armed), no console errors. *Mic-stop and the armed-resume branch need the tablet mic to confirm live.*
**"Reframe" — a second verb on the composer (proposed Ken, June 21 2026; one-shot version BUILT v0.3.20).** In addition to **Speak** (say the box text verbatim), a **Reframe** action hands the box text to the AI as *steering/context* and regenerates the suggested responses around it — "give me new options taking this into account." The box is dual-purpose: same input, two verbs.
   - **BUILT (v0.3.20):** `index.html` adds `#reframeBtn` between Speak and Clear (placeholder updated to name both verbs); `ui.onReframeClick`; `keyboard.js` `KEEP_OPEN_CONTROLS` includes `#reframeBtn` (so the on-screen keyboard doesn't hide when it's tapped — same fix as Speak/Clear). `llm.generateResponses` takes an optional `opts.steer` string → a `steerBlock` appended to the system prompt that frames the text as "additional context AND direction… being user-authored, they are TRUE and override the 'keep it general' caution" (so it is exactly the channel for supplying the specifics the v0.3.18 anti-fabrication rule forbids the model from inventing). `app.js` `handleReframe()` mirrors `handleRegenerate`: guards to an active partner turn with a palette (`if (!currentPartnerText || !lastPalette.length)`), re-runs generation with `{ steer }`, refreshes ONLY the palette via `engine.refreshPalette` (stack/mode/floor untouched — no duplicate FPP), and **clears the box on success** (kept on error for retry). Verified in preview: button renders (v0.3.20); the guard fires with no API call and keeps the box; a stubbed call confirms the steer text + guidance framing + user-authored-TRUE override reach the system prompt and the result parses. *Full success path with a real captured turn + live API needs the tablet.*
   - **Why it's strong / where it fits:** it's the channel by which the user *injects ground truth the AI lacks*, so it's the natural complement to the v0.3.18 anti-fabrication guardrail — type "I actually beat Tyler at chess last night" → Reframe → the AI can now build options around a *true* event instead of being held to the general. (A lightweight, in-the-moment version of the future episodic-events idea, thought #2a.) The text may carry content ("I'm worried about the cost") and/or direction ("keep it short, lean toward declining"); frame the box to the model as "the user's guidance for how to respond / what to convey" — no need to make the user pick a mode.
   - **Mechanism — ~80% already built (it's a *guided* regenerate).** Reuse the v0.3.16 "Show me different options" seam: re-run `llm.generateResponses` for the SAME partner turn with an optional `steer` string (alongside the existing `avoid`), then refresh ONLY the palette via `engine.refreshPalette` (stack / mode / floor untouched — no duplicate FPP). Regenerate *is* "Reframe with empty guidance," so the two can share one code path. Touches `llm.js` (a `steer` opt in the prompt), `app.js` (a `handleReframe` modeled on `handleRegenerate`), `ui.js` + `index.html` (the Reframe button).
   - **DECIDED — one-shot, not sticky (Ken, June 21 2026).** The steer applies to *this* regeneration only, then is discarded — it is NOT carried into subsequent turns. **Clear the box after Reframe** (Ken's call): both Speak and Reframe *consume* the box, so an empty box reliably means "nothing pending." Keeping the text would falsely signal that the steer is still in effect (sticky). (Unlike the earlier thought of keeping it to iterate — consistency of "empty = no pending action" wins.)
   - **The sticky/persistent version is the conversation-goal control — deferred to the Goals subsystem.** A steer that *persists* across turns is literally the **conversation goal** layer of the three-layer Goals model (see *Conversation & Relationship Goals*). That version needs a way to *see* and *clear* the standing steer and rules for layering with relationship/disposition goals — build it there, not here. **[→ folds into the Conversation & Relationship Goals build as "Reframe = the mid-conversation conversation-goal control."]**
   - **Phase:** the one-shot Reframe is **Phase 1, near-term** — no situational awareness or RAG needed, slots onto the regenerate plumbing, patch-level. **Smaller things to pin at build time:** button label ("Reframe" is Ken's word; "Steer options" an alt — must be unmistakably distinct from Speak for the target user); the no-partner-turn case (first cut can guard like regenerate's `if (!currentPartnerText || !lastPalette.length) return`, or later shape openers in INITIATING).
   - **Academic grounding (Ken, July 13 2026):** `Reframe-Concepts-for-AAC-Architecture.docx` synthesizes the academic frameworks behind "common ways a user might want to redirect a conversation" — Goffman's frame analysis/footing (the literal origin of the word "reframe"), Halliday's SFL register theory (field/tenor/mode), Martin & White's Appraisal Theory (attitude/engagement/graduation), and Grice + Brown & Levinson's pragmatics/politeness theory — and turns them into a 7-category taxonomy of redirect types (topic pivot, tone/register, stance/assertiveness, length/pacing, ground-truth injection, emotional coloring, footing/alignment as the umbrella). Notes that two already-shipped design choices are, in hindsight, direct implementations of these frameworks: the anti-fabrication rule (v0.3.18) = Grice's Quality maxim; the DISPREFERRED slot's hedge+account rule = Brown & Levinson negative politeness. Recommends any future preset/quick-select layer for Reframe (see the sticky-steer note above) be organized by these independent axes rather than as one flat list. Not built — a reference doc, same status as `CA-Concepts-for-AAC-Architecture.docx` for the rest of the engine.

2. **In-app virtual keyboard (later).** A real renderer: on-screen keys sized for direct select, integrated with the app's look and the future access-method renderers.

   **Keyboard-selection design — BUILT & verified (June 14 2026); awaiting Ken's usability review on the test tablet.** Shipped: `app/js/keyboard.js` (the shared on-screen keyboard — digit row + QWERTY + shift/caps, space, backspace, Enter, and `, . ' -`; keys act on `pointerdown` + `preventDefault` so the target field keeps focus and the caret never moves; inserts at the caret and dispatches `input`; Enter saves single-line fields / inserts newline in the composer textarea). Settings → General radio "Keyboard for typing" (`index.html`), wired in `app.js` `openSettings`/`saveSettings`; `storage.loadKeyboardMode`/`saveKeyboardMode` (default `physical`); CSS for the panel + radios; `keyboard.init()` + `setMode(loadKeyboardMode())` at app init; sw precache + CACHE_VERSION → `aac-v0.2.6`. A `MutationObserver` on `#worldviewContent` sets `inputmode="none"` on dynamically-built `.wv-text` cards so the Windows keyboard is suppressed before first focus. Verified in preview (synthetic `focusin`, since the harness window isn't system-focused): onscreen mode shows the keyboard + suppresses the OS keyboard and types into both the composer and worldview fields; physical mode never shows it and leaves `inputmode` empty; Settings radio round-trips; no console errors. *Build details below remain the authoritative description.*

   **Keyboard-selection design — original spec (Ken, June 14 2026).** Motivating problem: on a Surface, Windows shows *no* on-screen keyboard when the type cover is attached in laptop position, but auto-pops its own keyboard when the cover is folded back or detached. We want the app's keyboard in that second case. **Detection finding:** the browser exposes *no* reliable signal for physical-keyboard attachment or Surface type-cover posture — `navigator.devicePosture` only reports foldable-*screen* state (`folded`/`continuous`), the Keyboard API reports layout not attachment, and there is no `isKeyboardAttached`. The only *effective-posture* inferences are reactive (watch `visualViewport`/VirtualKeyboard `geometrychange` to see whether Windows pops its keyboard on focus) or one-way (a trusted hardware `keydown` proves a keyboard exists; silence proves nothing). **Ken's decision: do NOT auto-detect or fall back. A user-set Settings parameter decides, and it drives behavior until the user explicitly changes it** ("won't be an issue in practice"). Spec:
   - **Setting:** Settings → General, two radio buttons "Keyboard for typing": ① *My physical keyboard* (default) ② *On-screen keyboard (app's own)*. Stored in `aac_settings` as `keyboardMode: 'physical' | 'onscreen'` via new `storage.js` `loadKeyboardMode()`/`saveKeyboardMode()` (default `'physical'`).
   - **Behavior:** `physical` → app renders no keyboard, inputs keep normal `inputmode` (if the OS pops its keyboard when folded, that's the OS; the user owns that choice). `onscreen` → in-scope text fields get `inputmode="none"` (the reliable Edge/Chrome switch that suppresses the Windows keyboard) and focusing one shows the app's own virtual keyboard. Read at field-focus time so a Settings change takes effect on the next field without reload.
   - **Scope:** the "In your own words" composer + the "About Me" questionnaire inputs, built as one shared keyboard component. Settings' own fields (API key) stay on OS/physical for now (Setup-tier, rare, often supporter-entered).
   - **Dependency / phasing:** the parameter is the easy ~10%; the virtual-keyboard *renderer* is the bulk and is this build-order item #2. The two MUST ship together — a setting that suppresses the OS keyboard and then shows nothing is a regression. Build = (a) `storage.js` accessors, (b) Settings radio group + wiring + CACHE_VERSION bump, (c) the shared app keyboard component (accessible key sizing for CP motor needs, inserts into focused field, Speak/Enter consistent with composer, dark mode, future scanning-renderer compatible), (d) composer + worldview focus handlers that summon/dismiss it. *Not yet started — awaiting Ken's go-ahead to build.*
3. **AI-assisted composition (later, after worldview lands).** Few-words → full-sentence expansion in the user's voice; personalized prediction.

Open sub-questions deferred to build time: where the composer sits relative to the move palette (screen allocation — already an open UI question), and whether the type-and-speak composer should also push the spoken utterance into conversation history like a selected response.

## API-key feedback: missing-key prompt, format warning, live Test button (Ken, July 17 2026)

**Origin:** Ken (testing the 0.5.93 deploy) asked why there's no visible message when no API key is set, and why pasting an incomplete key (missing 3 chars) showed no error. Checking the **User Manual §3.2** settled it: the manual *promises* "a red warning about an invalid key" at the bottom of the General tab — **which was never built** (no key validation existed at all), and the code's "No API key set…" message was routed through the **visually-hidden status bar** (v0.5.2), so it never showed. Ken: build all three surfaces.

**Built (dev 0.5.94; not a new subsystem — patch-level):**
1. **Format warning under the API Key field** (`#apiKeyStatus`, Settings → General) — `llm.validateKeyFormat(key)` (no network) flags the gross paste mistakes: missing `sk-ant-` prefix, embedded whitespace, or too short (<40). Red note as you type; clears when empty (empty = *missing*, not *invalid*) or well-formed. Realizes the manual's promised red warning.
2. **"Test" button** beside Paste — `llm.testApiKey(key)` does a live **`GET /v1/models?limit=1`** (authenticates the key but **bills no tokens**) with the `anthropic-dangerous-direct-browser-access` header: 200 → "✓ Your key is working"; 401/403 → "✗ The key was rejected — check you copied all of it"; else → "couldn't reach the service." **This is the only surface that catches a subtly-wrong/truncated key** (Anthropic keys have no fixed public length, so the format check can't). *Verified in preview that `/v1/models` works with browser CORS from localhost — a bogus well-formed key returns 401 → "rejected."*
3. **Pre-start "no API key" prompt** (`#apiKeyPrompt` in `#startBlock`, over the transcript region like the What's-new panel) — amber "action needed" card shown when no key is saved, with an "Open Settings to add your key" button (→ `openSettings`). Informational, not blocking (the user can still Start and use manual AAC — the AI-optional property). `refreshApiKeyPrompt()` shows/hides it on load and whenever the key field changes.

**Files:** `llm.js` (`validateKeyFormat` + `testApiKey`); `app.js` (`showApiKeyStatus`/`reflectApiKeyFormat`/`refreshApiKeyPrompt`, Test-button + prompt-button wiring, oninput now reflects format + prompt, `initApp` calls `refreshApiKeyPrompt`); `index.html` (Test button, `#apiKeyStatus`, `#apiKeyPrompt`); `styles.css` (status line + amber prompt card + Test button). No version bump yet (0.5.94 dev cycle). **Verified in preview:** prompt visible with no key / hidden once a key is present / re-shown when cleared; all three format warnings fire and clear on a well-formed key; live Test returns "rejected" for a bogus key; 119/119 tests; no console errors. **Docs follow-up (deferred to a "sync docs"):** the User Manual §3.2 wording ("bottom of the General tab") is slightly off (the warning is directly under the field) and doesn't yet mention the **Test button** or the **pre-start prompt** — update on the next manual sync. *(The manual's promised warning now exists, so §3.2 is no longer describing vaporware — but it undersells the new Test button, the real fix for a truncated key.)*

## Wind-down and closing are two distinct steps; static palettes paginate (Ken, July 17 2026)

**Decision (Ken):** "Wind down" and "closing" are separate CA closing-sequence steps and must be separate card categories. **Wind-down statements** signal an intent to end *without* saying goodbye ("I should get going.", "Great catching up with you."); **closing statements** are the actual goodbyes ("Bye!", "Take care!"). This splits the old single mixed `closers` list into two.

**Flow built:**
- **Wind down** button → always shows the **wind-down** statements (was: the mixed closers).
- Selecting a wind-down statement → **speaks it, then automatically offers the closings (goodbyes).** So the user winds down first, then signs off.
- Selecting a closing → re-offers the closings (a partner may say bye back). Partner-initiated closing (AI `CLOSING` classification, or the `looksLikeClosing` fast-path) also shows the goodbyes so the user reciprocates.
- **If the partner doesn't reciprocate**, pressing **Wind down** again re-offers the wind-downs — Ken: no *escalation*, just "a different 4" (page to the next set of the defined wind-downs).

**Pagination of static palettes (Ken's "does the New-4 button work for wind-downs?" → yes, and it extends to all three static sets).** Openers / wind-downs / closings are predefined lists that can define MORE cards than the footprint shows (4, or 8 in 2-per-category mode). The user pages through the extras two ways, both a "dip into the pre-defined set" (no LLM):
- the **"New N"** regenerate button, when a static palette is showing, dips to the next page (wrapping) instead of calling the AI — for openers, wind-downs, AND closings;
- **re-pressing Wind down** advances the wind-down page (first press of a conversation shows page 0; each re-press dips further, wrapping).
Per-kind offsets persist for the conversation and reset at Start/End. When normal AI response cards show, the static cursor is cleared so "New N" regenerates (doesn't page).

**Code:** `SLOT.WIND_DOWN` added (`engine.js`/`ui.js` — slate "wrapping-up" hue, shares CLOSING's color since they never co-display). `engine.js`: `DEFAULT_CLOSERS` → `DEFAULT_WIND_DOWNS` + `DEFAULT_CLOSINGS`; `windDown()` now returns the wind-down palette; new `showClosings()` returns the goodbyes; `setConversationPhrases({openers, windDowns, closings})` (still accepts a legacy `closers` as the wind-downs). `control-phrases.js`: `closers` → `windDowns` + `closings` (defaults, `normalize`, `mergeNewDefaults`, `seeded` watermark, reset). **Legacy migration:** a file with the old single `closers` list and no new keys simply reseeds both new lists from defaults (the two senses can't be auto-classified) and the old list is ignored — single-user pre-beta precedent; re-edit via Settings → Controls if the old closers were customized. `control-phrases-editor.js`: the one "Closings" section becomes two — **Wind-down statements** + **Closings (goodbyes)**. `app.js`: `renderStaticPalette`/`pageWindow`/`staticOffsets`/`resetStaticPaging`, `offerClosings` (replaces `reofferClosings`), `handleWindDown`/`handleInitiate`/`handleRegenerate` + the CLOSING paths route through the pager. **Verified in preview** (static flows need no API key): Wind down → wind-downs p0; New-4 → wind-downs p1; select one → goodbyes auto-appear ("Say goodbye, or wait for their reply"); New-4 → goodbyes p1; re-press Wind down cycles wind-down pages; openers page via New-4; End→Start resets paging to p0; 119/119 unit tests, no console errors. Not version-bumped (patch-level work on the current 0.5.93 dev cycle; committed locally per the release rule when Ken directs).

## Versioning

Format: **major.minor.patch** (e.g., `0.3.8`). All pre-release versions use major `0`.

**When to bump which digit (agreed Ken, June 18 2026):**
- **Patch (third digit)** — the default for almost everything: bug fixes, refinements to existing behavior, and small self-contained features that *extend* the existing feature set. Mental test: "Is this changing or fixing how an existing capability works?" → patch.
- **Minor (second digit)** — reserved for a substantial **new architectural layer or subsystem** that later work builds on (e.g. `0.2` continuous partner capture; `0.3.0` the Conversation Engine). The bar is deliberately high: "new layer," not just "new feature" — the worldview questionnaire and relationship graph shipped as `0.2.x` patches because they slotted into the existing app rather than restructuring it. Roughly aligns with crossing a Phase boundary or adding a Phase's defining capability. Mental test: "Did this add a genuinely new subsystem the app didn't have before?" → minor.
- **Major (`1.0` and beyond)** — **Ken's call alone** (Ken, June 18 2026), based on maturity and readiness. Claude Code does not make the 0.x→1.0 transition without Ken explicitly directing it.

**Release process — the PC is dev, GitHub is release (Ken, July 4 2026; formalized in [RELEASING.md](RELEASING.md)).** This **supersedes** the earlier "commit and push every completed step" rule — testers are coming online, so we are now conservative about what reaches GitHub. The full ritual lives in `RELEASING.md`; the essentials:
- **Commit to local `main` = save work; push `main` = release.** A GitHub Actions workflow deploys `app/` to Pages on **every** push to `main`, so **any** push (even docs-only) reaches users. Between releases Claude **commits each change locally but does NOT push** — local commits sync/back up via OneDrive. There is one branch (`main`); no `dev` branch.
- **Changelog-as-you-go.** Every likely-**user-visible** change gets a plain-English bullet under `## Unreleased (next release)` in `CHANGELOG.md`, **in the same commit as the code**, written the way a *target user* reads it (not engineering language). `CHANGELOG.md` is the single source of truth for the in-app "What's new" notes (via `scripts/apply-release-notes.mjs`).
- **⚠ A BUG THAT ONLY EVER EXISTED INSIDE THE CYCLE IS NOT A FIX (Ken, August 24 2026).** A long cycle fixes things it broke itself; the people upgrading never had those builds. **The test: could somebody running the LAST PUBLIC RELEASE have hit this?** If not, say nothing or fold it into the feature's description — a "Fixed:" bullet for a problem they never had makes a brand-new feature sound unreliable before they touch it. **The near-miss is the REFRAME, not the deletion**: a caveat about how the new thing behaves is usually worth keeping, it just must not be worded as a repair. Watch for "used to", "before this", "no longer" and "Fixed:" attached to something the cycle itself created. Full rule and worked examples in [RELEASING.md](RELEASING.md).
- **Pre-bump.** At the end of each release the dev copy is pre-bumped to the **next patch** (`APP_VERSION` + `CACHE_VERSION`), so the dev About screen always reads higher than the last public release and you know you're on a dev build. It's provisional (local, unpushed); the **final digit is decided at release**.
- **~~iPad work is pushed live but NOT announced~~ → RETIRED at 0.6.0; there is no longer a `## Held back` section, and there should not be one again (Ken, August 3 2026).** *"I assume that since iPad support is now part of the official release there will no longer be a ## Held back section."* Correct. The arrangement existed only because iPad work had to reach the device by a push that also deployed to Windows users, while the iPad was still an experiment nobody had been told about; **0.6.0 made iPad a supported platform**, so there is nothing left to withhold. It wound down cleanly — both flagged entries, **Export/Import** and the **API-key Paste button**, were announced in the 0.6.0 notes (verified in `CHANGELOG.md`), so nothing was parked and forgotten. **Platform-specific notes are now handled the right way instead**, by the `### On an iPad` / `### On a computer` scoping the generator understands: each user sees what applies to them plus one line saying the other device had improvements too — announced but not confusing, rather than silent. *The `## Held back` stop-condition stays in `RELEASING.md` as a general guard (it costs nothing and would catch a future deliberate withholding), but it is not in use and a release that finds one should treat it as a question, not a routine.* **Historical mechanism, if it is ever needed again:** `apply-release-notes.mjs` ignores every H2 except `## Version X.Y.Z` and `## Unreleased`, so notes parked under any other heading are kept as a record and never bundled.
- **Trigger phrase "bump Conversant AAC"** — or **"bump Conversant"**, the original short form; both mean the same thing (Ken only, after he's verified `CHANGELOG.md`). Runs the whole ritual **through the push**: finalize the digit → rename `## Unreleased` to `## Version <final>` → regenerate the bundled notes → add the version-table row to [VERSION-HISTORY.md](VERSION-HISTORY.md) → commit → push (deploys) → pre-bump the next cycle.
  - **Prefer the trigger over ad-hoc wording** ("let's push the latest code" — Ken, Aug 1 2026). The value is sequencing: an ad-hoc instruction leaves it ambiguous whether the full ritual is wanted, and the ritual has an order that is easy to get wrong (the iPad-publish-before-pre-bump trap below was hit exactly that way).
  - **What it decides on its own, so "no second confirmation" is honest:** the version is the **pre-bumped patch digit** already in `APP_VERSION`, and the release announces **everything under `## Unreleased`**, unchanged.
  - **The only two things that stop and ask**, because they are Ken's calls and were both live on Aug 1 2026: (a) the cycle added a genuinely new **subsystem**, so the minor digit is arguably due rather than the patch — propose it with the reasoning; (b) a **`## Held back`** section exists in `CHANGELOG.md`, so something is deliberately unannounced and its disposition has to be settled. Neither is normally present.
- **Docs get restamped after the push, not before.** `DOC-SYNC.md`'s `At commit` must name a commit that exists on `origin/main` (see the ordering rule at the top of that file), so any documentation synced during the dev cycle is restamped at the release commit once the push lands. The `VERSION-HISTORY.md` table is **released-versions-only** (rows added at bump, not during dev).

**Phase-to-version mapping — the per-release table now lives in [VERSION-HISTORY.md](VERSION-HISTORY.md)** (moved August 7 2026; 142 rows, unedited). It had grown to a fifth of this file, which is loaded in full every session, and nothing reads it to make a decision. Consult it when you need to know why a specific version did what it did — a regression that bisects to a release, or the history behind a subsystem. A release adds its row **there**, not here.

## Relationship Data — its own graph model, separate from the questionnaire (decided + BUILT June 15 2026, v0.2.27)

**BUILT (June 15 2026), v0.2.27 — the relationship graph is live end-to-end.** Files/wiring:
- **`app/js/relationships.js`** — the model layer. Graph shape `{ version, updated, migratedFromWorldview, people:[{id, name, private, attrs:{about}}], edges:[{from, to, type, attrs}] }`; the user is the implicit node `"me"` and a person's relationship is the `type` of the `me→person` edge (person↔person edges are supported by the schema, not yet by the UI). API: `load`/`save`/`syncToFolder` (**same "file in the folder wins" rule as worldview v0.2.25** — its own cache key `aac_relationships`), `listPeople` (denormalized name+relationship+about+private), `addPerson`/`updatePerson`/`removePerson`, `resetAll`, `count`, `migrateFromWorldview(wv)`, and `buildBlock()`.
- **`buildBlock()`** contributes a "People in my life:" block to the LLM profile. **Private people are never named or described** — surfaced only as a phrase-around instruction by their relationship (mirrors the worldview privacy rule).
- **No migration (Ken, June 15 2026).** A first cut wired an idempotent `migrateFromWorldview()` into app boot to import the old module A3 answers; **removed** — Ken is the only user and had entered little relationship data, so he re-enters people via the People editor rather than carry migration code (and no standalone script either). The old A3 answers remain in `worldview.json` but are **orphaned** (no longer in the registry, so `worldview.buildBlock()` ignores them) — harmless dead data.
- **Registry change** (`app/data/worldview-questions.json`): **module A3 deleted**; its one genuinely-flat field `address_terms` ("what do you call people") moved into **A1 About You**. People/pets are now the graph, not questionnaire fields.
- **UI** (`worldview-ui.js`): About Me home shows a **"People & relationships"** section with a "People in Your Life" row (count) → a dedicated **People editor** (`renderPeople`): per-person cards (name (relationship) — about, Private badge, Edit/Remove) and an add/edit form (name, relationship, about, "Private — never name this person" checkbox). Reuses `.wv-text` so the on-screen keyboard's existing scope + side-dock + `inputmode="none"` handling applies automatically. **Restart** now clears the graph too.
- **Relationship picker (v0.2.29):** the relationship field is a **standard grouped `<select>`** (`REL_GROUPS`: Family / Partner / Friends & social / Care & support / Pet — 30 options) **+ "Other…"** which reveals a free-text field. Case-insensitive match on edit re-selects a standard option or falls back to Other (prefilled). **One relationship per person for now** (single `me→person` edge type); multiple relationships with one person ("cousin" + "wife") is a deliberate later refinement (Ken, June 15 2026).
- **LLM wiring**: `llm.setRelationshipsBlock()` + inclusion in `buildProfileBlock()`; `app.js` `generateOptions()` calls it each turn alongside `setWorldviewBlock`. `app.js` init + `handleStart` load/sync the graph.
- CACHE_VERSION/APP_VERSION → `0.2.27` (build); → `0.2.28` (migration removed). `relationships.js` added to the sw precache. **Verified in preview:** model CRUD + private-withholding `buildBlock`; the People editor add→display→mark-private→badge flow; registry shows no A3 and `address_terms` under A1; after migration removal `migrateFromWorldview` is gone with CRUD/editor still working; no console errors. *On-disk persistence to `relationships.json` needs a granted FSA folder — confirm on the tablet.* **Scope held:** small typed people+edges with a free `attrs` bag — no graph DB, no third-party-relations editor yet.

**Decision (Ken, June 15 2026): relationship data is a separate concern in its own file — `relationships.json` — not flat question/answer fields.** Ken's insight: relationship data has a fundamentally different structure from the worldview questionnaire. It is a **graph** — *nodes* (people) and *edges* (relationships), **both of which carry their own data** — whereas the questionnaire is flat key→answer.

**Why the current model is wrong for this.** Today module **A3 "People in Your Life"** stores people as flat `repeat` lists (`household`, `family_key`, `pets` = arrays of `{name, relationship}` tuples; `friends_key` = a name list). That cannot represent: per-person attributes (how you address them, where they live, what you share, topics to avoid, how often you see them); the *same person* appearing in multiple lists with one identity; **edge** attributes (closeness, current state — "we're not talking right now" — shared history); or relationships *between third parties* ("my sister is married to my brother-in-law"). This matters specifically because the architecture's **relationship modeling** + Phase 2 **partner recognition** ask "the partner in front of me is *X* — who are they to me, what's our history?" — a node+edge lookup, not a questionnaire answer.

**Agreed shape (build-ready spec; coexists with the flat questionnaire):**
- New file in the FSA data folder: `relationships.json`, shaped `{ version, updated, people:[{id, name, attrs:{address_term, lives, likes, avoid, …}}], edges:[{from, to, type, attrs:{closeness, seen, notes, …}}] }`. `from:"me"` for the user↔person edges; person↔person edges allowed. `attrs` is a free-form bag per node and per edge (typed later as patterns emerge).
- New model layer `relationships.js` mirroring `worldview.js` conventions: data-folder write-through + localStorage cache, **and the same "file in the folder wins" reconciliation just settled in v0.2.25** (see the cross-PC fix above) — its own `syncToFolder`. Node/edge CRUD; privacy per node (contact-info-style strict phrase-around carries over); a `buildBlock()` contribution to the LLM profile (and, with partner recognition, a *focused* lookup for the current partner).
- The flat questionnaire **stays** for flat facts (name, place, interests, personality/values). The **A3 "People" module becomes the capture UI** that writes nodes/edges into the graph instead of `repeat` tuples; a migration reads existing A3 `repeat` answers into `people`/`edges`.

**Scope guardrail (Ken's "small, not a graph DB"):** build a small typed people+edges structure with a free `attrs` bag — *not* a full graph database or a third-party-relations editor — enough now, RAG-friendly later. Build is a dedicated session (model → migration → A3 UI rework → LLM wiring), authorized separately. Not started.

## Per-partner profile — "how I talk with this person" (Ken, August 7 2026), BUILT

Voice-layer **Phase 3**: register, a standing relationship goal, a free note, and a
person's own conversation phrases, all on the **`me→person` edge** (`edge.attrs`).
Three of the four features queued for that edge are now built; the fourth,
**per-partner vulgarity permission, is still blocked on guardian approval** and the
blanket no-vulgarity rule stands until it is settled. The seam is the same object.

**A MENU HERE, FORCED CHOICE IN SOUND CHECK — and the distinction is the decision (Ken).** *"When generally trying to ascertain the user's voice, I think a forced choice approach is appropriate. However, when capturing the user's voice for a specific, well known person with whom they have a history of conversations, I think we should provide more flexibility and take them at their word."* The §3.3 self-report objection — people cannot accurately describe their own style — is about **trait-level** description in the abstract. Per-partner register escapes it three ways: it is **comparative** ("more relaxed than I usually am") rather than absolute; it is grounded in a **specific remembered history** rather than an average over all behavior; and code-switching by relationship is largely **deliberate**, so it is a recalled policy rather than a trait the user would have to infer about themselves. Forced choice would also cost a full item run **per person**, which does not scale across a graph. *(I recommended forced choice and was wrong on the first two points; recorded so it is not re-argued.)*
- **Two consequences of "take them at their word", both load-bearing.** The dimensions are stated **assertively** in the prompt ("this user is more relaxed with Mum"), never hedged as a self-report, because hedging invites the model to discount them. And the free-text note is declared to **override** the menu where they conflict — the menu is five dimensions we chose, the note is whatever the user actually wanted to say.
- **NEUTRAL MUST PRODUCE NOTHING.** Every dimension defaults to "same as usual", neutral values are not stored, and a person with an empty profile emits **no prompt text at all**. Without that, the feature quietly starts shaping responses for every person in the graph the moment it ships. Guarded by tests at both layers.

**THE GUARD IS THE POINT, and it is the August 5 place lesson applied to a second kind of context.** A fact with no stated purpose reads to a model as material to work into the conversation. A standing relationship goal is the dangerous case: **"Repair things between us" is a reason to choose warmer wording and would be a catastrophe read as an instruction to raise the subject.** So the block states its purpose *before* the content ("shapes the WORDING only — none of it is a topic to raise") and *again* on the goal itself ("never mention it"). Do not remove either on the grounds that it is redundant; they guard different failures.

**Openers ADD to the global list, theirs first — they do not replace it (Ken).** Adding one starter for one person then costs nothing and loses nothing: page 1 of the palette is theirs, paging reaches the global set (static palettes have paginated since v0.5.93). Replacing would mean authoring a full set per person or silently narrowing the palette. Merged case-insensitively, and re-merged on **every** partner change — selecting a partner adds their phrases and clearing one has to take them back out, so `clearInfluencers()` re-runs it too or the next conversation opens with the last person's phrases still on top.

**⚠ THE HAZARD THIS CREATED, now fixed: `updatePerson` used to DELETE the me→person edge when the relationship was cleared.** With the profile living on that edge, blanking a dropdown would have destroyed "how I talk with this person" silently, while the user believed they had only cleared a relationship type. It now empties the `type` instead when the edge carries attrs, and still drops a genuinely empty edge. **General rule: once data lives on an edge, no path may delete the edge as a side effect of editing something else.**

**Deliberately NOT built: the partner-review channel** (inviting someone to answer descriptive items about how the user talks with them). It is the right Phase-3 instrument per SOKA and the storage is now in place for it, but it needs invite/collect/merge machinery and its four constraints, and it would have delayed testing. The user-entered half stands alone.

**⚠ A VERIFICATION LESSON WORTH MORE THAN THE FEATURE.** The first check of the openers merge appeared to pass and was a **false positive**: the test phrase was `"Hi Mum, got a minute?"`, which is byte-identical to the global template `"Hi {name}, got a minute?"` rendered with name=Mum. The merge had never run — the partner was never activated, and the global list alone produced the expected string. **When verifying that a personalized value is being used, choose a value the fallback path could not possibly produce.** The same trap is available to every test of a per-person override.

## Tier B — personality and values (voice-layer Phase 4, Ken August 7 2026), BUILT

Two modules, ten items each: **B1 "What I'm Like"** (Big Five, two items per factor) and **B4 "What Matters to Me"** (Schwartz's ten basic values in the PORTRAIT format the question bank specifies — a person is described and you say how like you they are).

**⚠ THE IDS ARE THE QUESTION BANK'S, NOT A FRESH SEQUENCE — values is B4, and B2/B3 are deliberately absent.** `Worldview-Questionnaire-Draft1.docx` and **all ten test personas** have numbered Tier B since June 2026: **B1** energy/how you come across, **B2** humor, **B3** register & style, **B4** values, **B5** beliefs, **B6** how you treat different people, **B7** emotional landscape, **B8** voice in your words. This shipped as B1/B2 for a few hours and **B2 collided with Humor**; renumbered before release, while only test answers existed. **Anything added to Tier B later takes its number from the bank**, never the next free integer — the personas are the spec and they are what a tester loads. *(Of the rest: B3 and B6 became the per-partner profile, B8 became Sound Check and the Express Panel, B5 is Ken's June deferral, and B2 and B7 are simply unbuilt.)* Ken's reason for pulling it ahead of the plan's ordering: *"I'm going to ask you to extend all the personas to include answers to these questions anyway."*

**SHORT FORMS ON PURPOSE — do not "complete" these to full length.** The standard instruments are PVQ-40 and BFI-44; using them would have made this the largest authoring job in the plan and the most tiring module in the app, against a questionnaire whose governing constraint is that answering is **physically draining** for the target user. Twenty items is enough to shape prose. This is not psychometrics and must not be treated as though a reliability coefficient were at stake.

**THE PROMPT SHAPE IS THE DESIGN, not a detail.** Twenty Likert answers rendered the ordinary way — one `- Label: Very much like me` line each — would be twenty lines of noise that leave the model to infer what a scale point means, which is **precisely what the second edition criticized about trait scores**. So a field carrying `trait` is aggregated into **one sentence of plain descriptions**, extending the `directive` pattern already in `buildBlock`. Two properties hold it up:
- **Neutral produces nothing.** "Somewhat like me" contributes no clause, so a half-answered module is cheap and a user with no strong feeling exerts no pull. Same rule as the per-partner register; keep them consistent.
- **The scale strings live in TWO places and a test asserts they match.** `worldview.js` recognizes the endpoints by string; the registry authors them. A silent drift would make **every** trait answer neutral and the whole module would stop working with no error anywhere — the same failure class the settings-help drift guard exists for.

**THE GUARD IS STRONGER HERE THAN FOR ORDINARY FACTS, and the reason is worth keeping.** Every other entry in the worldview block is something the user could plausibly say out loud. A personality description is not: *"sociable and comfortable around people"* worked into a response as *"I'm a really sociable person!"* is both false-sounding and faintly absurd, and it is exactly what a description with no stated purpose invites. So the block says what the text is for **before** it and forbids stating it **after** — *"never state it, never quote it back, and never have them describe their own character."* Third instance of the same lesson, after the place block and the per-partner goal.

**The rest of Tier B followed on August 8 2026** — see "B2 — humor" and "Tier B is complete".

## Tier B is complete (Ken, August 8 2026: *"Let's finish all of Tier B"*), BUILT

Reading the bank end to end is what made this smaller than it looked: **of the five topics still open, three were already covered by a stronger instrument.** What was genuinely missing was **B5 beliefs**, **B6 how you treat different people**, and **half of B7**.

**⚠ B3 (register & style) IS DELIBERATELY NOT A QUESTIONNAIRE MODULE, and this is the entry to read before anyone "notices the gap" and builds it.** Every one of its five questions is answered better somewhere else, or is closed by a settled decision:
- **Directness, formality, verbosity** → these are exactly Sound Check's `economy`, `formality` and `directness` dimensions, and **the August 8 measurement says a description is the WEAK instrument for style**: told "witty, sarcastic" the model wrote "my history knowledge is pretty patchy", while three chosen exemplars produced the real thing on the first try. Adding Likert style questions would be adding the weak instrument beside the strong one and inviting them to disagree.
- **"What's okay for the assistant to use"** → the swearing option contradicts the standing **no-vulgarity** decision (absolute, and the eventual control is per-PERSON, blocked on guardian approval), and "emoji-style expressions" contradicts the speakability rule, since everything here is spoken aloud. What remains is too thin to be a question.
- **Greetings and terms of address** → `address_terms` already lives in A1, and **signature greetings are catchphrases, which the August 5 2026 decision routes to Express Panel buttons rather than to prompt text** — precisely so the model never produces them unprompted.
**So B3's content ships in four places and none of them is a flat registry field. Do not re-add it.** *(B6 and B8 were similarly reassigned earlier — B8 to Sound Check and the Express Panel — but B6 turned out to be only half-covered; see below.)*

**B6 IS NOT A DUPLICATE OF THE PER-PARTNER PROFILE, which is the mistake the earlier note made.** CLAUDE.md had recorded "B3 and B6 became the per-partner profile"; that is true of the *content* and false of the *coverage*. The per-partner profile is **per NAMED person on the relationship graph**. B6 is **per relationship CATEGORY** — and the category version reaches the people the per-person version never can: **strangers, who by definition are not in the graph**, and the large majority of partners for whom nobody will ever sit down and write a profile. Filling in five categories once covers everyone; per-person covers one person at a time, and **overrides where it exists**. The prompt says so explicitly, so the two cannot fight. *Verified live: the same partner question produced "Mixed, if I'm honest. Some days are better than others" for a consultant and "Getting on alright, you know. Could always be worse" for a close friend.*

**B7 SHIPPED WITH TWO OF ITS FOUR QUESTIONS, and dropping the other two is the right answer, not a shortcut.** "What makes you light up" is `topics_welcome` (C3) plus `passions` (C2); "what frustrates or annoys you" is `dislikes` (C4). **Answering is physically draining for the target user** — the founding constraint of the whole questionnaire — so the same question is not asked twice for the sake of a tidy module. What was genuinely uncovered: **conflict style**, which is aimed squarely at the DISPREFERRED slot so that declining sounds like this person rather than like a generic hedge; and **"one thing you'd want people to understand about you"**.

**⚠ B5 NEEDED A COMBINATION NOTHING ELSE IN THE BLOCK HAD: shape the responses (like a trait) while never being volunteered (like a private fact).** The plain `private` treatment gives only the second half, and **for faith and politics disclosure is the SMALLER risk.** The larger one is a card that takes a **position** — argues a side, agrees with a claim, concedes a point — spoken in the user's own voice to somebody who will remember it. **That happens whether or not the belief is ever named, and it happens most easily when the model knows nothing and fills the gap with the average view.** So the values ARE sent, and the rule is what makes sending them safe: *"here so that you do not guess wrong about this person — not material to use."* **Knowing is safer than guessing; this is the one place in the app where withholding a fact would make the output worse rather than safer.**
- **The escape route is mandatory, same shape as `topics_avoid`:** the app cannot stop a partner raising politics, so when they do, one option must always be a way not to engage at all.
- **Outlook (optimist / realist / skeptic) is deliberately OUTSIDE the beliefs block.** It is a disposition, not a conviction, and lumping it in would saddle "optimist" with the never-raise rules and make it unusable.
- *Verified live in all three directions: asked directly → hedge plus an out; invited to agree with the opposite view → neither agrees nor argues; ordinary weekend question → no trace of either subject.*

**The `directive` mechanism now carries eight kinds** (`avoid`, `seek`, `expert`, `humor`, `belief`, `outlook`, `register_group`, `conflict`, `understand`) and a test asserts every Tier B field is wired to one buildBlock knows — **because a field authored without a directive silently becomes a plain fact line, which for a B5 field means a private belief listed as an ordinary fact about the user.**

**Follow-up not done:** the ten test personas answer B2/B5/B6/B7 in the bank's wording but have no "as answered in the app" section for them, the way B1 and B4 were given one. Ken has asked for that before and will likely want it again.

## B2 — humor, and the finding that DESCRIBING a voice is not SHOWING it (Ken, August 8 2026), BUILT

Ken, on being told the wry brush-off needed the unbuilt humor module: *"I thought we had Tier B support in place... Let's add it."* Correct — B1 and B4 shipped the machinery on August 7; humor was simply never authored. **Module B2, three questions, straight from the question bank and matching what all ten personas already answer:** sense of humor (multi), teasing, and whether the app may offer a cheeky response. **B2 is the bank's number for humor — not the next free integer.** *(Still unbuilt in Tier B: B5 beliefs, deferred by Ken June 14 2026, and B7 emotional landscape.)*

**⚠ THE FINDING, measured rather than assumed, and it is the transferable part: a DESCRIPTION of someone's humor reliably decides WHETHER to be light and reliably fails to be funny.** Told *"witty, likes clever wordplay, sarcastic"* the model produced *"my history knowledge is pretty patchy"* — self-deprecating, not witty. Three prompt rewrites did not move it. Adding the **Sound Check levity items** — the same user picking a wry line off a list of three — produced *"That one sailed right out of my head years ago"* on the first try. **This is the Sounds Like Me second edition confirmed on our own prompt: few-shot beats description for style by a wide margin, and a trait word is a description.** General rule: **if a future field is about how someone SOUNDS, an item bank is the instrument and a questionnaire adjective is not.** The two are complements, not alternatives — B2 grants permission and sets the key, the exemplars supply the wording, and neither alone reaches the target.

**HUMOR IS A MANNER, NOT A MOVE — so it modifies a slot rather than occupying one.** A first cut said "one of the options should be a light brush-off" and produced nothing at all, because **all four slots already have jobs** (PREFERRED plainly, DISPREFERRED with an account, INITIATIVE a counter-move, REPAIR a clarification) and there is nowhere to put a fifth kind of card. It works when aimed at **PREFERRED specifically** — for a person whose humor is licensed, the wry deflection *is* the most likely thing they would say, which is Ken's point exactly. Apply the same test to any future style property: does it name a new move, or a different way of making an existing one?

**THREE GUARDS, each of which failed once before it was written:**
- **Silence is not permission, and neither is a style.** A humor style with no answer on the cheeky question emits the taste and an explicit *do not offer one*. **"I'm not much of a joker" with no permission answer reads as a decline** — that person has answered it in substance, and defaulting them to licensed would offer jokes to the one user who said they do not make them.
- **⚠ A STATED "NO" MUST BE TOLD IT OUTRANKS STYLE EVIDENCE.** With wry exemplars in the prompt and humor declined, the model **went light anyway and the refusal was simply lost.** The model does not rank two kinds of instruction on its own, so the decline now says it overrides the examples. **A refusal that quietly loses to an example is worse than never having asked**; generalize this to any future preference that competes with harvested evidence.
- **The ONE-slot cap is a hard limit, not a preference.** A card can be tapped by mistake — the reason the double-tap safeguard exists — and a joke in the user's own voice cannot be taken back, so a straight way to say the same thing must always survive. Plus: never on a serious, upsetting or medical turn (verified: a scan-results turn stays entirely straight).

**⚠ THE LEVITY ITEMS BROKE AN ASSUMPTION THE ITEM BANK WAS BUILT ON, and the fix is structural rather than a prompt plea.** Every prior exemplar is deliberately bland, so **reuse verbatim is harmless and arguably right** — the user picked "Good, thanks." because it is what they say, and nobody notices it twice. A **distinctive** line reused is a **verbal tic**: the same brush-off on every unanswerable question is the placeholder-predictability failure ("predictable fillers become a joke to partners over time") arriving through the voice layer. Observed live — the model handed the chosen sentence straight back. A blanket "do not copy anything" was followed about two times in three, so `voice.buildBlock` now **lists levity exemplars separately with the opposite instruction** (bland: match them; levity: never reuse, write fresh in the same spirit). **When adding a dimension to the bank, ask whether its exemplars are safe to say verbatim** — that is the property that decides which list they belong in.

**DECIDED: choosing the lighter option in Sound Check IS permission**, so a user with wry selections and no B2 answers gets a light PREFERRED. Behavior beats self-report (the project's own recorded principle), and three deliberate selections are a stronger signal than an unticked box. An explicit decline still overrides. *An earlier draft said the opposite and the model ignored it — the instruction was wrong, not the behavior.*

## My Places — situational awareness without GPS (Ken, August 3 2026), BUILT

Ken: *"Add support for 'My Places' to the Worldview model. For each place add 'facts' support. E.g., Place: Starbucks, Location: 123 main street (if specific to a particular location), 'favorite drink' = 'mocha latte', etc. Also need to add support for places in the Express panel just like the Person support that we already have. This is a way to support location situational awareness without GPS."* Built end-to-end and verified in preview.

**The framing that matters, and it is Ken's: a place is an INFLUENCER first and a stored record second.** Phase 2 was always going to get location from GPS. This gets the same signal by having the user *tap where they are*, exactly as they tap who they are talking with — which means it needs no new hardware permission, no Phase-2 dependency, and works indoors where GPS is worst. It also lands on a seam that already exists and is proven, so the whole feature is a patch, not a layer.

- **New model [`places.js`](app/js/places.js)** — `{ version, updated, places: [ { id, name, private, facts: [ { key, value } ] } ] }`, its own `places.json` + `aac_places` cache, the v0.2.25 file-in-folder-wins reconciliation. Mirrors `relationships.js` because it is the same kind of thing: user-owned data that is not questionnaire Q&A.
- **Facts are ARBITRARY named pairs, and there is deliberately NO fixed field set.** What is worth knowing about a coffee shop, a clinic and a cousin's house have almost nothing in common, so any schema we invented would be wrong for most places — which is why Ken's own example puts *Location* in as a fact with the qualifier "if specific to a particular location" rather than as a column. The editor offers 10 suggested fact names through a `datalist` (Location, What I usually order, Who I go with, …): quick to pick, equally quick to ignore.
- **Facts are an ORDERED ARRAY, not an object.** The user controls the order they are listed in (and so the order the model reads them), duplicate keys don't silently clobber, and a blank row can exist mid-typing. `normalizeFacts` drops rows with no key (an editor artifact) but **keeps a key with an empty value** — "Wi-Fi password: (blank)" is a fact someone has started recording, not junk.
- **Two blocks, and the split is load-bearing.** `buildBlock()` lists every place with its facts as standing context (`llm.setPlacesBlock`, alongside worldview + relationships). `buildHereBlock(id)` is the *situational* line for the place the user has tapped — it names the place and **repeats that place's facts**, so the current setting's details are in front of the model rather than one line among twenty. Where changes per conversation; what-you-know changes per edit.
- **Privacy is the standing three-level model, unchanged.** A private place is sent for context under the do-not-volunteer instruction, which names the two things that unlock it (partner asks / user steers via Reframe — see the privacy-model section above, whose circular-clause defect Ken found through this very sentence); `buildHereBlock` adds a matching do-not-name-it-on-your-own-initiative line. A place the user doesn't want the AI to know about is simply not added.
- **Express Panel `place` item type** — a toggle mirroring Partner exactly: picked from My Places by `placeId` in the editor (`— Choose a place —`), one active at a time, tap again to clear. **Olive `#827717`**, chosen because the palette had no yellow-green, so it cannot be confused with the nearest hues (pace deep-orange, feeling cyan). Tapping fires no round-trip — the effect lands at the next generation, so saying where you are never costs a token or interrupts a turn.
- **Turns are stamped with `place`** (`placeStamp()` → `{ id, label }`, keeping the stable `placeId`) alongside partner and feeling, so a reviewed conversation can join back to My Places — the Phase-3 relive-and-critique feature will want to know *where* an exchange happened.
- **The place PERSISTS across End conversation — `clearInfluencers()` does NOT clear it (Ken, August 3 2026).** A first cut cleared it alongside Partner and Feeling, by analogy with v0.5.31; Ken reversed it, and the reason is a genuine disanalogy rather than a preference. **Partner and Feeling are properties of the CONVERSATION** — it ends, and the next person and mood are genuinely unknown, so inheriting them is a wrong guess. **Where you are is a property of the ROOM, and ending a conversation does not move you.** A café visit or a clinic waiting room is several conversations in one place, so clearing would charge a re-tap for each. Cleared only by tapping the same place again or picking another. *(The risk this accepts, stated once: a place left on after the user has moved keeps shaping suggestions. It is visible — the button stays lit — and the frequency argument runs the other way, so the trade is the right one; the counter-argument is recorded rather than re-litigated.)*
- Also wired: sw precache, Export/Import (`places.json` joins the now-five user-owned files, with a count in the import summary), About Me **Restart** (clears places, and its confirmation text now says so), and load/sync at init, at Start, and on Choose-Folder.

**Verified in preview** (1280×800, stubbed fetch — no key needed): "My Places" on the About Me home; the form saves Ken's exact Starbucks example with both facts; **adding or removing a fact row preserves what is already typed** (the values are read back out of the DOM before every rebuild — the obvious way to write this loses them); edit prefills name + facts in order; the private badge and both privacy blocks are correct; the editor's `+ Place` and inline insert picker both work and the panel button renders olive with `aria-label="I'm at Starbucks"`; tapping it sets `aria-pressed`, tapping again clears, and it **survives End conversation** while Partner and Feeling drop; and a captured API request carries **both** the `Places I go` list and `The user is at Starbucks right now… What you know about Starbucks — Location: 123 Main Street; favorite drink: mocha latte`. 13 new unit tests in `tests/places.test.mjs`; 230/230 pass.

**Known limitation, existing behavior not a regression:** the Express Panel maps items 1:1 onto the layout's cells, so a place **appended** to a full default list (33 items, 33 cells) overflows and never renders. Use the row's **＋** insert button to place it within the grid. This has been true of every item type since v0.5.0; it is only more noticeable here because a new place is the first thing a user adds after the panel is already full.

## Conversation & Relationship Goals — three-layer model (design recorded June 15 2026; NOT built)

**Ken's insight (June 15 2026):** everyone has goals for every conversation — consciously or not — from "maintain the connection with this person" to "steer the conversation toward X." Goals should be a first-class input to generation. The key framing: **"relationship goals" is really *three distinct things on three time horizons*,** and they map to three different stores — lumping them together is what makes it hard.

**The three layers:**
1. **Disposition** — how you generally are with people (durable, trait-level): "I keep things light," "I want to be seen as capable." Not tied to one person or conversation; the *prior/default stance*.
2. **Standing relationship goal** — what you want from a *specific relationship* (durable, person-level): "stay close to my sister," "I want my boss to see me as competent." Stable across many conversations with that person.
3. **Conversation goal** — what you want from *this exchange right now* (ephemeral, session-level): "today I need to ask Dad for help," "steer toward planning the trip." Set at/just-before conversation start, injected into that conversation's prompt, then discarded.

**Mapping to existing stores (two of three already have a home):**
| Layer | Store | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Disposition | `worldview.json` **Tier-B** (how you treat different people, register/values) | Exactly what Tier-B is for; designed, not yet built. |
| Standing relationship goal | `relationships.json` **edge `attrs`** (e.g. `attrs.goals`) | **Clean fit today, no schema change** — a goal is an attribute of the `me→person` edge. |
| Conversation goal | **Session runtime** (Conversation Engine state), optionally appended to the `conversations/*.json` log for Phase-3 review | Genuinely new, but it's runtime state, **not a new persistent store**. |

**Recording mechanism (per layer):**
- Standing relationship goal → a **"what I want from this relationship"** control in the **People editor** (the one built in v0.2.27), writing to the edge `attrs`.
- Conversation goal → set at **conversation initiation** (pairs with Phase-1 canned starters and Phase-2 partner recognition — "you're talking to Dad; what do you want from this?"), with a quick mid-conversation control (fits the persistent-controls region); stored in session memory.
- Disposition → Tier-B questionnaire when built.
- **Expression format — DECIDED (Ken, June 15 2026): curated menu + free text.** A short list of common goals (stay close / be understood / be seen as capable / keep it light / get support / repair things) **plus** an "in my own words" option — fast to pick (matters for the CP target user) yet still personal. (Not menu-only, not free-text-only.)

**Why it matters / how it's used:** goals shape and **rank the four-slot move palette** (PREFERRED / DISPREFERRED / INITIATIVE / REPAIR): "maintain connection" → favor affiliative PREFERRED; "steer toward X" → surface a high-ranked INITIATIVE toward X; "wind down" → favor PRE-CLOSING. They become explicit generation inputs, **layered most-specific-wins** exactly like the prompt blocks already are: disposition (worldview) → standing relationship goal (graph) → conversation goal (session).

**Nice property:** goals are **never spoken** — they only steer generation — so they sidestep the privacy machinery entirely (unlike facts, a goal can't be accidentally said aloud).

**Open sub-questions (not yet decided — pin before building, so they aren't silently assumed):**
1. **Single vs. multiple goals per layer.** The mapping sketches `attrs.goals` (plural), but we only decided single-*relationship* (the relationship picker). Whether a relationship or a conversation can hold more than one goal at once is undecided. (Parallels the single-relationship-per-person call.)
2. **User-set vs. system-suggested goals.** Everything here assumes the user sets the goal explicitly. Whether the system may ever *infer/suggest* a goal (from partner recognition, history, etc.) is open — current default is explicit/user-set only.
3. **Per-person vs. relationship-type goals.** "With authority figures I want to seem competent" is a goal on a relationship *type*, not one person. The graph stores `type` on the edge, not as its own node, so type-level goals would need a convention (or a small types table). For now goals would attach per-person only.

**Status:** design recorded; expression format decided (curated menu + free text); three sub-questions above left open. **Build deferred** (Ken: "just record the design") — revisit when the conversation-engine work matures. Standing relationship goals are the smallest first build when we resume (they slot straight onto the existing graph edge + People editor).

**Related reference doc:** `Reframe-Concepts-for-AAC-Architecture.docx` (July 13 2026) — the academic grounding for *redirect* generally (Goffman's footing, SFL register theory, Appraisal Theory, pragmatics/politeness). Its curated-menu-plus-free-text reasoning (Section 5) is the same reasoning already applied to the "Expression format" decision above; its 7-category redirect taxonomy is a candidate source list for the conversation-goal menu's options, alongside the "stay close / be understood / …" set already chosen. **Section 7 of that document (added July 13 2026) grounds this section directly:** Dillard's Goals-Plans-Action model (primary vs. secondary goals) and Canary & Stafford's Relational Maintenance Theory, mapped explicitly onto this three-layer model — see the concrete organizational structure below, which builds on that grounding.

**Concrete organizational structure — proposed July 13 2026 (Ken + Claude; not yet built).** A specific plan for what the curated menus contain and where the controls live, worked out in conversation and building on the Dillard/Canary & Stafford grounding above.

- **Shared vocabulary — three curated lists, no free text as the primary input.** Constraint (Ken): the target user can only speak in general/generic terms about a goal, whether the partner is a stranger or someone well known.
  - **List A — primary conversation goal** (Dillard's primary-goal categories; generic across any partner, known or arbitrary): Stay connected / catch up · Get information or advice · Ask for help · Share news or feelings · Make plans together · Repair something · Ask for something specific · Just be sociable, no agenda.
  - **List B — stance modifier** (reuses the Reframe redirect taxonomy verbatim — one vocabulary serving both a standing goal and a live mid-conversation Reframe): Come across as capable · Keep it warm · Keep it brief · Stay calm / avoid conflict · Keep it smooth and on-topic.
  - **List C — relationship-maintenance goal** (Canary & Stafford's five strategies; only offered for graph people, since "maintain" presupposes an existing relationship): Be upbeat with them · Talk openly about the relationship · Reassure them of your commitment · Do things together · Support their other relationships.

- **WHERE EACH LIST IS SOURCED FROM — person and place populate DIFFERENT lists, so they compose rather than compete (Ken + Claude, August 5 2026).** Ken's question was whether goals revolve primarily around the partner or the place. **Standing relationship goals are 100% person by definition** — you have no relationship with a coffee shop, so nothing can hang a standing goal off a place. Per-conversation goals split by whether the partner is present as an **identity** or as a **role**: talking to your sister, the goal comes from her and the setting is incidental (same goal at her house or a café); at a pharmacy, clinic, or counter the partner is often a stranger you will never see again, and the goal comes from what the place is *for*. **The place determines the goal precisely when it determines the partner's ROLE rather than their IDENTITY** — which is the project's own founding distinction, transactional vs. interactional communication, so the two goal sources correspond to the two modes the product exists to span.
  - **The resolution is not a priority rule.** **Place → List A** (the primary goal: what this exchange is *for*), strongest in transactional settings. **Person → Lists B and C** (stance and maintenance: how to *be* with them), and List A as well when the conversation is relational. Test case that breaks a naive "person outranks place": the pharmacist you happen to know — Ramon supplies the stance (keep it friendly), the pharmacy supplies the primary goal (pick up the prescription), and they compose. *(A "person outranks place" precedence rule was the obvious first answer and is wrong for exactly this case.)*
  - **A place can already carry a goal with NO schema change** — `places.js` facts are arbitrary named pairs, so a `Goal` fact is expressible today, the same way `Location` is just a fact.
  - **The third source is the one that cannot be inferred and therefore most needs the UI: the user's own agenda** ("today I need to tell someone what happened"). Neither the person nor the place implies it.
  - **This converges with the context-scoped Express Panel work** (see the context-sensitive page-sets entry): a place- or person-scoped framing chip **is** a one-tap conversation goal for that context. So the Start-flow Goal picker may end up needed only for the third source, with the common cases pre-filled or reachable from the panel.

- **Arbitrary vs. graph people.** The per-conversation Goal control (List A, optionally + List B) is identical whether the partner is a stranger or someone in `relationships.json` — it never depends on the graph. The only difference: a graph person with a standing goal recorded (List A ∪ List C, via the People editor) pre-highlights that goal as the suggested default when they're the active Partner — one tap to accept, or override for this conversation. An arbitrary/unrecognized partner gets the same blank menu with no pre-suggestion. List C never appears in the live per-conversation control — it is a People-editor-only, standing attribute.

- **UI placement — supersedes the "quick mid-conversation control… fits the persistent-controls region" idea above with a concrete design:**
  1. **Primary: a Goal-picker page folded into the Start-conversation flow.** Reuses the reserved response-footprint cards that already show openers (`handleInitiate` → INITIATING mode) — no new region, no reflow (UI Layout Rule 1). Tapping "Start conversation" shows Goal chips (List A + a "Decide as we go" chip) in that footprint first; tapping one advances to the existing opener-card page in the same footprint. Net cost for the common case: one extra tap ahead of the existing "pick an opener" tap.
  2. **Revise: a small tappable "Goal" chip in the transcript header**, alongside the mode chip and the Start/End conversation icons (v0.5.40) — shows the current goal's short label (or "No goal set"), informational-but-tappable like the mode chip. Tapping it re-shows the same Goal-picker page as a brief overlay on the response footprint (Rule 8's modal-superimposition mechanism), then returns to whatever palette was showing.
  3. **Deliberately NOT an Express Panel toggle**, unlike Partner and Feeling. Rationale: importance and churn rate are different axes — Goal is high-importance (Dillard's *primary* goal, the reason the exchange exists) but low-churn (typically set once per conversation, like Partner's own use pattern), whereas Feeling is lower-stakes but plausibly changes moment-to-moment within a conversation. The Express Panel's scarce, high-frequency real estate stays reserved for Feeling; Goal gets a placement matched to its own, lower churn instead.

- **Resolves open sub-question 1 above as a v1 default:** single active goal per layer (not multiple) — consistent with the existing single-relationship-per-person call. Sub-questions 2 (user-set vs. system-suggested) and 3 (per-person vs. relationship-type) remain open.

- **What's unchanged:** Feeling stays exactly as shipped (Express Panel toggle); the storage-mapping table above, the layered most-specific-wins resolution, and `buildSituationBlock` injection + per-turn stamping (mirroring the existing Partner/Feeling stamp pattern, v0.5.30) all carry over unchanged.

- **Flagged, not acted on:** Partner arguably has the same low-churn profile as Goal (you don't usually switch who you're talking to mid-conversation), so the same Start-time-plus-header-chip placement could eventually apply to it too — left on the Express Panel for now since it is shipped and working; revisit both together later if desired.

## Recording indicator / partner awareness — near-term scope + deferred camera-LED (Ken, July 18 2026)

Full write-up lives in the **Security Remediation Backlog → SEC-7** entry (the geometry reality, the honest scope of capture, the agreed near-term build scope, and the deferred rear-camera-LED to-do). Summary: the tablet screen faces the *user*, not the partner, so on-screen indicators reach the partner only on a glance; the reliable partner-reachable signal is **audible**. **Agreed near-term build (not yet built):** (1) a prominent on-screen live-mic state (upgrade from the subtle red Listen button); (2) a **start-of-listening chime** (short synthesized Web Audio tone on the transition into capture — the single choke point `ui.setListenButtonState(true)` — Settings toggle, default on, NOT continuous); (3) a printed "This device listens and speaks for me" device card (beta-kit / manual deliverable, not code). **Deferred future to-do:** slow-flashing the rear-facing camera LED / torch toward the partner (capability-gated progressive enhancement — torch control is essentially Android-Chrome-only and unlikely on Surface; adds a camera-permission prompt + battery/thermal cost — build only after on-device testing, opt-in, default off). SEC-7 is **beta-gated** (load-bearing once a real, unaware partner is transcribed through a cloud STT service).

## Practice Mode — full plan (Ken, July 18 2026; DECIDED, NOT built)

The "Practice Communication Partner" from Architecture Overview §8. The AI plays the partner: it authors the partner's side of a scenario (spoken via TTS, shown in the transcript), and the user selects responses exactly as in a real conversation. The Strategic Assessment (§4) flags a **minimal** Practice Mode as the highest-value unbuilt feature for the alpha→beta stage — it lets testers learn the loop without spending a real partner's patience, exercises the system in acoustically clean conditions (separating UI/loop bugs from acoustic bugs), and de-risks the first real conversation (the moment most likely to decide whether a tester continues).

**Why it's a modest build — it reuses the entire Phase-1 pipeline; only the mic is replaced by the AI.** Verified against the code (July 18 2026): `handleSilencePeriod(text)` in `app.js` is **pure text processing — it never touches the mic** (sets `currentPartnerText`, mirrors the raw line to the transcript via `logPartnerInterim`, `engine.partnerSpeaking(text)`, `placeholders.arm()`, `generateOptions(text)`). So AI-authored partner text can flow through the exact same path a heard utterance takes, and the user's response cards come out identically. Three small integration points:

1. **Author the partner line + feed it in.** New `llm.generatePartnerUtterance(scenario, history, context)` produces the partner's next line from the scenario persona + conversation so far. The app speaks it (TTS, distinct voice — see below), displays it as a partner turn, then hands the text to the normal generation path (reuse `handleSilencePeriod`'s body, or call an equivalent that skips the STT-specific bits). **Cost: two LLM calls per turn** — one to author the partner line, one to classify it + generate the user's options (the second is the normal `generateResponses` path). Accepted: practice is deliberate and user-funded; reusing the pipeline verbatim is worth the extra call. (A future optimization could have the partner-authoring call also emit the classification to save the second call, but that diverges from "reuse the pipeline" — not for the first build.)
2. **Fork the post-selection step.** `handleResponseSelected` currently ends by resuming the mic (`startFreshListening` / `resumeOrIdle` / `offerClosings`). In practice mode it instead cues the next partner line — but *through the same listen-gate* (see the Start-Listening design below), so it's `resumeOrIdle`-shaped, not a parallel path.
3. **Bypass STT.** No mic in practice mode → **no echo problem** (the app speaks the partner line into a room where nothing is listening). This is precisely why practice mode is acoustically clean and separates loop/UI problems from acoustic ones.

Everything else — placeholders (rehearsed as-is), the palette, Reframe, "In my own words," the transcript, End conversation — is untouched.

**"Start Listening" IS the partner cue — reinforcing the real listening discipline (Ken, July 18 2026; the key design decision).** Rather than auto-playing the partner on entry, Practice Mode maps the **Start Listening** button (same button, same `mic` icon, same red/selected "capturing" state) to *"cue the AI partner to speak."* It does **not** activate the mic — under the hood it calls the practice-partner advance instead of `stt.startListening()` — but it rehearses the exact motor step new users most need to internalize (*"when it's the partner's turn, I press this"*). Crucially, it honors the **same gating logic as the real app** (`manualListenArmed` + `loadAutoRelisten`, v0.2.32), so practice trains the actual discipline, not a practice-only shortcut:
