Verified deliberately afterwards on a minimal reproduction. **Copy the file to `.mjs` first and check that**, which rejects it correctly; or import it, which is the real proof. A "syntax OK" from `node --check` on a `.js` module means nothing.

**AS BUILT — items 1 and 2 (August 7 2026).** New **Settings → Troubleshooting** tab holding four groups: *Report a problem* (free-text note + Save to a file + Copy), *How the app is being used*, the *Error log* (**moved here from About**), and *System information*. New modules `usage-summary.js` (pure, unit-tested) and `diagnostics.js`; 11 new tests, 256/256 pass.
- **A CORRECTION to what was recorded above: category selection distribution was NOT derivable from existing logs.** `logUserResponse` recorded `allOptions` as option **text only**, so a saved turn carried the *position* picked but nothing that says which CA category it was — and position cannot be read back as category, because the palette's composition varies (four structural slots, the CHOICE family, openers/wind-downs/closings, the three repair-of-self options). Fixed by recording **`selectedSlot`** explicitly. Logs written before this date have no slot, so the summary reports the distribution over the subset that does rather than silently mixing the two. This is what makes Ken's own point — that all four CA categories must earn their place — actually measurable.
- **Key redaction lives in `storage.reportableSettings()`, NOT in `diagnostics.js`.** A first cut kept an independent exclusion list in the report builder, on the reasoning that two lists cannot be loosened at once; that required exporting the *raw* settings bundle, which is the worse trade. Storage owns the keys, so it hands out an already-redacted bundle and no caller can forget. Verified live with both keys set: neither appears, both render as `(set - not included)`.
- **The pre-start affordance is the load-bearing half of item 2, not a nicety.** `main.disabled { pointer-events: none }` covers the Command Bar where the Settings button lives, so **Settings is unreachable before Start today**, and the 0.6.5 top-layer bug already made it unreachable mid-session on an iPad. `#startReportBtn` saves straight to a file with no note — the panel you would type a note into is exactly what may be gone. Its listener is attached **early in `initApp`**, before anything that can throw, because the tester who most needs it is the one whose app did not finish starting; and it **reports on its own label** rather than into the Settings status line, which from that screen is invisible. `logError` is deliberately NOT called on failure there: the app may be mid-failure, and logging writes to disk and dispatches a UI event, either of which could throw again and swallow the message.
- **Every diagnostic swallows its own errors and prints the reason into its box.** A panel that throws while collecting is worse than one with a gap, because it fails exactly when something is already wrong. Same reasoning as the aggregator's tolerance of malformed logs — a crash mid-flush leaves partial files, and the summary must degrade rather than break.
- **Verified in the browser:** tab and panel render, all ten controls present, the error log is gone from About, the summary shows its no-folder empty state naming the data folder as the likely cause, system info covers all six blocks (96 lines), the report puts the tester's note first and ends with the no-keys disclaimer, the pre-start button saves `conversant-problem-report-<date>-<time>.txt` and confirms on itself, three Settings columns with zero horizontal overflow (Rule 18 correctly not triggered), no console errors.
- **Not exercised, and it is the standing boundary for anything data-folder-backed:** the summary against real `conversations/*.json` needs a granted folder, which the headless preview cannot produce. The aggregation itself is unit-tested against fixtures covering all four sections plus malformed input.

**Standing caution, unchanged from July 12:** with a handful of testers these numbers rank things and generate questions — they do not answer *why*. They are interview fuel, never a substitute for asking a tester why they stopped.

**Two build items this raised that are NOT instrumentation:**
- **DONE (Ken, August 7 2026) — timing defaults reset, and the placeholder ladder decoupled from the AI. See "Placeholders are gated by partner silence" below.** Silence **2s → 0.5s**, initial placeholder **4s → 2s**; subsequent stays **10s** and max stays **2** (Ken addressed the first two explicitly and left these).
- **A "controls tour" Practice scenario (Ken):** *"I find that I often forget what specific buttons mean or when they should be used."* This is the predictable cost of **Rule 12** (icon-only, no text) on the one surface where **Rule 17**'s spoken help was deliberately excluded, and a tour is the mitigation. Practice Mode is the right host, but it is a **new scenario TYPE, not a new scenario**: it has to *instruct* ("they didn't hear you — try *Ask them to repeat*") and check the right button was pressed, where existing scenarios only converse.

**Gate: beta. Related items now folded into this decision:** the **"Copy System Info"** TODO (Aug 2 2026), the **Troubleshooting tab** TODO (Aug 5 2026), the **issue-reporting page** TODO, and the error-log/red-wash work (v0.5.72–0.5.75). The tester-facing plan lives in **[BETA-TEST-PLAN.md](BETA-TEST-PLAN.md)** (draft, August 7 2026) and becomes a house-style `.docx` tracked in DOC-SYNC once Ken has revised it.

---


## Reading the beta Sheet — trigger phrase "evaluate beta" (Ken, August 21 2026), BUILT

Ken's directions, which set the scope: **the Sheet is not how the beta is judged.** *"This will also be a person-to-person period with me as the central person. I just need a heads up to reach out."* So the tool flags who has gone quiet and stops there — it deliberately does **not** try to tell "stopped using it" from "stopped reporting", because he settles that in one message and no amount of data can. **The reporting is for the finer-grained measures**, and those are where the effort goes.

**`scripts/evaluate-beta.mjs` reads an exported copy of the Sheet and says what is in it.** Three pure modules under `scripts/beta-eval/`, 13 tests. Ken exports the `reports` tab (File > Download > Comma-separated values) and the `problems` tab if it has anything on it; both land in Downloads and are found with no argument.

**⚠ THE ARITHMETIC RULE THAT SHAPES THE WHOLE THING: MEDIANS CANNOT BE POOLED.** A report carries each tester's *median* wait, not the waits themselves, and the middle of five middles is not the middle of everybody's replies. So every pooled figure is a **sum, or a ratio of two sums**. That constraint improved the answer rather than limiting it: the founding question is not "what is the typical wait" but **"how often did the other person wait longer than they will tolerate"**, and the share of replies over four seconds is a ratio of two sums. It pools exactly right and is the more useful number anyway. Where only medians exist, a **range across testers** is printed and labelled as what it is.

**⚠ AND THE DISTINCTION THAT MAKES AGGREGATION LEGITIMATE AT N≈5: pooling across TESTERS and pooling across TURNS are different acts.** Five people are far too few to average as people — one person changing their mind moves any per-tester percentage. But those five produce thousands of turns, and a question about a **configuration** (does the paid transcription hear people better, does an iPad keep up) is a question about turns. So the tool groups by **device, what hears them, what speaks for them, and app version**, and there is no "average tester" anywhere in it. *(I recommended against cross-tester aggregation; Ken asked for it, and separating the two kinds of pooling is what makes it honest rather than a compromise.)*

**Scrubbed BY CONSTRUCTION, not by filtering.** Nothing in the renderer ever reads the fields carrying a communication partner's name or a place name — those people never agreed to anything and cannot be asked — so counts of them are printed and names never are. A strip applied afterwards is one forgotten line away from failing quietly; never touching the field cannot. Tester names *are* printed, so the output is exactly as confidential as the Sheet.

**Excluded, and named rather than dropped (Ken): *"All my devices are prefaced with Ken. You can ignore them and the SLPs as well, though I think they haven't been putting their IDs in their reports."*** Ken's own devices carry months of records written before conversations were saved the way they are now, so leaving them in pulls every pooled figure toward a history no tester has. **An unnamed report is its own category** — probably a therapist, possibly a tester who never typed their name — and is **listed for chasing**, because a report quietly binned turns that person into someone who appears to have stopped. Therapist names go in `EXCLUDE_NAMES` once known.

**⚠ THE TRAP THAT WOULD HAVE INFLATED EVERY NUMBER: a report re-counts the tester's WHOLE HISTORY every time.** Three reports from one device is not three times the conversations. So everything cumulative takes **the newest report from a device and ignores the rest** — and **errors are the sole exception**, gathered across all of them because since 0.7.11 each report carries only what is new. **Which creates the mirror trap:** before 0.7.11 a report carried every error ever, and the first report after a tester upgrades still carries the whole backlog, so simple concatenation counts the same error several times. Deduplicating on when-and-what makes both eras safe to merge and costs nothing, so it is done regardless of version. **Expect one large errors figure per tester at the version boundary, dropping sharply after — that drop is the fix working, not somebody's problems going away.**

**It ends in QUESTIONS, not conclusions**, and prints what none of it can settle: the trace carries counts and no words by design (August 5 2026), so *"it misheard me"* and *"the suggestions were wrong"* — the commonest complaints — are not decidable from any report. When one comes up, ask for one saved conversation file.

**⚠ IT READS THE RAW COLUMN, NOT THE FLAT ONES, and that is what keeps the Sheet out of the release loop.** The flat columns are for eyeballing; their headers are written only when a tab is first created, and adding one means redeploying the Apps Script through a procedure with a documented silent-failure trap. The raw column carries the whole report as it arrived, so **a number added to the app's summary appears there the following week with nothing changed at the far end.** The flat columns may drift and nothing breaks. *(Corollary: the Sheet's own headers do not update when the script's header list changes — column 39 was renamed to "new errors since last report" on August 21 2026 and the live Sheet needs that cell retyped by hand.)*

**⚠ THE SHEET SILENTLY REFUSED EVERY REPORT FOR SIX DAYS, AND THIS IS THE FAILURE SHAPE TO REMEMBER (found August 22 2026, by Ken noticing he could not scroll past column Z).** A tab created by Apps Script is **26 columns wide**; the reports row grew to 42 on August 16; **Google refuses a row wider than the sheet**, so `appendRow` threw on every report from that day. **Four independent layers each did the quiet thing:** `doPost` catches and returns a message, the app posts with `no-cors` so it cannot read the message, the app marks the week done on **enqueue** rather than on delivery, and the receiving end has no health check. Nothing errored, nothing retried, nothing was flagged — **and the symptom was the Sheet not gaining rows, which is indistinguishable from testers going quiet, i.e. from the exact signal the Sheet exists to produce.**

**Fixed by `_fit(sheet, header)`, which runs before every write:** it widens a tab that has been outgrown (columns are added on the right, so nothing moves) and **rewrites the header row when it no longer matches**. That second half also retires the older defect — a header written only at creation, so a renamed column never reached an existing Sheet and the row on screen drifted further from the data beneath it with every column added. It costs one read per report at a handful of reports a week.

**⚠ THE STANDING RULE THIS LEAVES: the row appended in `doPost` must be exactly as long as the header list, and a column may only ever be added at the END.** An insertion in the middle shifts every column after it away from the data already sitting under it, and a length mismatch reproduces the silent refusal above. Add the value and the name together.

**What was lost, and what was not.** Everything cumulative came back on its own: **each report re-counts the tester’s whole history**, so the next one to land restores the counts and the week-by-week rows in full. **Errors are the exception** — `weeklyErrorMark` advances on enqueue, so anything reported in that window is gone from the Sheet, though it is still on each device in Settings and in `errors.log`. The window is small because the mark only shipped in 0.7.11; before that every report carried the whole error list, so a tester on an older build lost nothing.
**Why not in the Sheet.** A digest emailed from the Sheet's own script editor would be zero effort and would put the judgment of what these numbers mean somewhere it cannot be reviewed, tested, or kept in step with the app — the same shape as the retired document generators and the retired second website, both of which cost real time. The one manual export is the price of keeping it here.

**ERROR ALERTING IS THE OTHER HALF, and it is the only part that does not wait for someone to look.** Ken: *"I need to hear about errors shortly after they are reported."* Reports arrive when a tester opens the app, so the receiving script mails on any report carrying errors. **`ALERT_ERROR_THRESHOLD` is now 1, not 5** — five was a volume filter written when nobody was reading the Sheet; with a handful of testers the interesting event is a **kind** of error appearing for the first time, and that arrives as a one. The cost of being wrong is an email. The mail names the tester, the kinds, and the version, and says the errors are new since that tester's last report. Mail goes to **ken@volksswitch.org** (set August 21 2026), and the same address also receives every **problem report** on arrival, threshold or not — a tester writing to us deliberately has no volume to filter. **⚠ BLANKING `ALERT_EMAIL` IS A SILENT OFF SWITCH:** an empty address disables every alert with no error and no trace, and reads as a setting nobody ever filled in rather than one that was turned off. Any change to it needs a redeploy — saving in the Apps Script editor alone changes nothing at the live address.

**Investigation is ON DEMAND, not automatic, and this is a capability limit rather than a preference:** nothing here runs unless a session is open, so an "automatic" investigation would be a promise the arrangement cannot keep. The mail is the prompt; "evaluate beta" is the investigation.

**Recommended cadence: read it WEEKLY, on the same day.** Reports arrive about weekly per tester, so a shorter cycle mostly re-reads the same rows, and a longer one lets a tester go quiet for a fortnight before anyone notices. The mail alerts are what cover the gap between readings, which is why they exist at all.

## The five untrustworthy figures — FIXED (Ken, August 21 2026)

Reading one real tester's report turned up five statistics that were wrong or unreadable. **Four of the five were the same defect wearing different clothes: a figure computed over a smaller slice of history than the heading above it, with nothing on the page saying so.** That synthesis is the transferable part, and it made the fix much cheaper than recomputing anything: **print what each figure was measured over, beside it.**

- **"Per week" is GONE from the page.** It scaled the days actually used up to a whole week, so five days of light use read as 29 conversations a week — flattering exactly the tester who is drifting away, on the first question the beta asks. The week-by-week table below it was already the honest version, which made the line both wrong and redundant. It is still computed (one test uses it to demonstrate what a cumulative figure hides) and **must not go back on the page**.
- **The wait figure has a floor** (`MIN_PLAUSIBLE_WAIT_MS`, 500 ms) and reports what it discarded. Pre-July-2026 records wrote both halves of an exchange at the same moment, so the interval between them is an artifact; those cluster near zero and dragged the median to 0.2s against 22.0s on the turns recorded properly. **⚠ IT EXCLUDES BY TIME, NOT BY WHERE THE REPLY CAME FROM.** Restricting to card-selected turns would have removed the artifacts too — and would have quietly changed the number's *meaning* from "how long did the other person wait" to "how long did the machine take". The other person waits whether the reply was chosen, typed, or tapped, and typing is the slowest of the three. **Never narrow it to card turns.**
- **The two splits print their own base** — where the words came from, and which kind of reply. Both have only been recorded since August, so printed bare against a total covering the whole history they read as a contradiction (172 against 17 in the real report) with nothing to say which was wrong. Neither was.
- **The error line says which period it covers.** The summary counts every error ever, from the saved conversations; the weekly report carries a *different* count, only those since the last report. Saying the period out loud is what keeps the two apart.

**This is mostly self-cleaning and that is worth knowing before judging how urgent a similar finding is: all four era problems exist because one device has months of old records. A tester who starts on the current version has clean data and correct figures from the first day.** The contaminated reader was Ken's own.


## Speaking as the user OPENS the mic when it starts a conversation (Ken, August 7 2026) — BUILT

**Found by Ken while designing the Express-Panel-in-Settings behavior:** *"pressing an express panel phrase speaks that phrase and (I think) starts a conversation. If that's right then pressing an express panel phrase in this way should automatically place the app in Listen mode."* Verified against source — he is right, and the two paths genuinely disagreed:

| Action | What happened to the mic |
|---|---|
| Select an **opener** ([app.js:1223](app/js/app.js:1223)) | `manualListenArmed = true; startFreshListening()` — armed **and** captured at once, unconditionally |
| Speak an **Express phrase** / composed statement (`speakAsUserTurn`) | `resumeOrIdle()` — captured **only if** already armed by a manual Listen press **AND** auto-resume on |

**An Express phrase really does start a conversation** — it takes the floor (`engine.selectResponse`), commits a turn, and `logUserResponse` lazily creates the conversation log (v0.5.69). But `manualListenArmed` starts `false` and **`loadAutoRelisten()` defaults to `false`**, so opening a conversation with a tapped phrase left the app **idle**: the partner replied and **nothing was captured, with no error anywhere.** Same silent-failure class as the July 2026 stall and the August 2026 Listen bug.

- **THE FIX IS CONSISTENCY, NOT A NEW POLICY:** opening a conversation with "Hi" from the panel is the same act as selecting an opener, so it now does what the opener path has always done. `speakAsUserTurn` captures `opensConversation` **before** `commitExchange` appends, then arms and captures. Decision extracted to **`conversation-logic.captureAfterUserSpeaks()`** (the layer the July 2026 stall surfaced in) so it is unit-tested — 5 regression tests, 272/272.
- **⚠ THE PREDICATE IS `conversationHistory.length === 0`, DELIBERATELY NOT `storage.getConversationId()`.** `setConversationSaving(false)` makes `startConversationLog` a **no-op**, so for a whole "Don't save this conversation" session the id stays `null` and every turn would report as an opening one — re-arming the mic on every Express tap and overriding auto-resume for exactly the users who chose privacy. The trap is invisible unless you go looking.
- **Mid-conversation, auto-resume keeps its meaning.** A user who turned it off asked to control the mic themselves; overriding an explicit setting is a different decision from fixing an unreachable state, and is Ken's to make if he wants it. **Verified live that this did not regress.**
- **Practice Mode routes through `resumeOrIdle()` rather than `startFreshListening()`** — there is no mic in practice, "capturing" means cueing the AI partner, and `practiceResumeOrIdle` already gates that.
- **Verified in the running app** (`aac-app-py`, mic blocked in the pane — which is itself the proof it tried): Listen never pressed, auto-resume off → **0 recognition starts before the tap, 1 after a single Express tap**, via `stt.js openSource`. A second phrase mid-conversation → **still 1** (auto-resume honored). Only console errors are `[stt] not-allowed`, the blocked-mic environment limit.
- **⚠ LATENT, ADJACENT, NOT FIXED (out of scope — flagging, not touching):** the `wasOpener` branch calls `startFreshListening()` with **no practice-mode guard**, so selecting an opener in a practice session opens a **real microphone**, which Practice Mode is supposed to never do. Cheap to fix; needs Ken's word since it is a behavior change he did not ask for.

---

## Placeholders are gated by PARTNER SILENCE, not by the AI round-trip (Ken, August 7 2026)

**Ken's statement of the invariant, which is what this section exists to protect:** *"I was under the assumption that placeholders are gated by partner silence and terminated by user speech. They should have nothing to do with the AI round trip."* That was always the intent. It was not the behavior between June 28 and August 7 2026.

**The defect.** `placeholders.arm()` was called at the silence checkpoint and only recorded a timestamp; `placeholders.start()` was what actually scheduled the speech, and `start()` is reached only *after* the classification returns — about four seconds. `start()` then waited `initialDelay` **minus** the elapsed time, so the first placeholder actually landed at **`max(initialDelay, round-trip)`**. With the old 4s default and a ~4s round-trip those are the same instant, which is exactly why it went unnoticed for six weeks and why the symptom Ken reported was *"it takes about 4 seconds to receive suggestions, so a placeholder firing at 4 seconds doesn't make sense."* **Lowering the default to 2s alone would have changed nothing** — `max(0, 2000 − 4000)` is zero, so it still fires the instant the AI answers.

**Why the AI was in the path at all, and the fix (Ken's, and it is better than mine).** The dependency existed for one reason: the acknowledgment pool was **split into question-flavored and neutral phrases** (July 8 2026), and you cannot pick between them until the partner's action is classified. My proposed fix was "neutral by default, upgrade the flavor if the classification beats the timer" — which preserves `"Good question."` at the cost of keeping the coupling and adding a state machine. Ken's memory of the original bug (v0.3.9, June 18 2026: `"Good question."` playing when the partner **hadn't asked a question**) pointed at the real fix: **make every first-rung phrase partner-statement independent, and the choice disappears along with the gate.**

**As built:**
- `arm()` now schedules the first placeholder itself, `initialDelay` after the pause. It consults nothing.
- `start()` is a **confirm**, not a start — normally a no-op that consumes the armed flag and lets the ladder continue to its later rungs. Its defensive start-without-arm path is retained.
- `placeholders.json` `acknowledgment` is a **flat array** again (was `{question:[], general:[]}`). `normalizePools` still tolerates the legacy shape and takes **only `general`** from it, because the whole point is that a question-presuming phrase must never play on a turn that wasn't one.
- **`isQuestionFlavored` and `QUESTION_ACTIONS` are REMOVED from `conversation-logic.js`.** They had exactly one consumer, and it was the coupling.
- The **defaults**: silence **0.5s** (Ken: *"err (at first) on the side of multiple roundtrips than on a delayed first trip"*), initial placeholder **2s**.

**⚠ The phrase pool was rewritten, and NOT only to drop "Good question." — the neutral pool violated a standing rule.** The July 2026 `general` set was `"Let me see." / "One moment." / "Just a second."` All three are imperative or **directed at the partner**, which is the exact class Ken rejected in June 2026 (*"anything imperative or directed at the partner — 'Let me think', 'Give me a second', 'Hold on' — reads as curt/annoyed"* through the flat built-in voices). It slipped in because it was authored for the non-question minority of turns; this change would have promoted it to **every** turn. The new set is declarative and first-person: **"I'm thinking about that." / "Thinking that over." / "I'm thinking." / "Working that out."** The `thinking` pool already complied and is unchanged.

**So there are now TWO standing constraints on placeholder phrases, both load-bearing** (stated at the head of `placeholders.js` and unit-tested):
1. **Partner-statement independent** — reads correctly after a question, a statement, an assessment, or a greeting, because nothing knows which it was. *This is what decouples the ladder from the AI; reintroducing a turn-type-dependent pool silently re-couples it.*
2. **Declarative and first-person** — never imperative, never directed at the partner.

**Accepted costs, both small and both stated once:**
- A **repair-initiator** ("What?") is the one turn warranting no placeholder, and it is only identifiable from the classification — so on a slow round-trip one acknowledgment can precede the re-speak. `app.js` calls `stop()` as soon as it knows. A mild redundancy on one turn type, against silence for the whole round-trip on every other one.
- **A 0.5s silence period multiplies generation calls.** Every pause fires a checkpoint, and since the July 10 2026 decision each one refines the *same* partner turn in place with latest-wins discarding superseded calls — so superseded calls are **billed and thrown away**. This costs tokens, not correctness, and Ken is funding the beta keys. Worth watching for **429s**, which the demo "no options" failure was already suspected to be.

**Tests:** `tests/placeholders.test.mjs` rewritten around the new contract. The load-bearing one is **`arm() alone speaks the first placeholder — the AI is never consulted`**, which never calls `start()` at all, standing in for an AI that is slow, failed, or has no key. Plus a guard that no phrase in either pool contains "question", and that `start()` after the first has spoken does not replay the acknowledgment rung. 245/245 pass.

**A greeting remains awkward for every phrase in the pool** — no floor-holder reads naturally before "Hi, how are you?" — and that is inherent, not a defect of this set. Noted July 8 2026 and still true; it becomes tunable when the placeholder editor lands.

---

## ⚠ THE ENGINE'S CLASSIFICATION IS A WHITELIST, AND IT DROPS NEW FIELDS SILENTLY (Ken found it in the field, August 23 2026)

`engine.ingestClassification` **rebuilds `state.lastClassification` field by field**
rather than passing the model's object through. So a classification field the prompt
asks for, the parser reads, and the renderer is ready to draw is **thrown away in the
middle, with no error and nothing to notice.** That is how the number button shipped
in **0.7.14 announced in the release notes and doing nothing at all** - Ken tried the
exact scenario from the note and there was no button.

**ADD EVERY NEW CLASSIFICATION FIELD TO THAT LIST IN THE SAME CHANGE.** A test now
asserts `offered_range` survives, and fails when the line is removed; extend it rather
than trusting the next person to remember.

**⚠ THE VERIFICATION LESSON IS THE BIGGER HALF, AND IT IS NOW A STANDING RULE** - see
*A change that crosses layers gets one check that crosses all of them*, under Working
Guidelines. Both ends were verified and the middle was not: the parser was tested by
handing it JSON, the renderer by handing it a chip, and the live model was later shown
to classify all four cases correctly, **including Ken's exact words**. Three of the four
links were right and the feature was still dead.

**Related shape, worth recognizing:** the same silent-drop hazard exists anywhere a
layer copies a known set of fields out of a richer object - the settings bundle, the
weekly-report payload, the conversation record. Each of those is guarded; this one was
not.

---

## The Express Panel has NO folders or pages (DECIDED Ken, August 22 2026)

Asked by an outside reviewer whether a panel button could reveal *other* buttons -
pages, folders, or a place to keep the overflow. **The answer is no: no folders, no
pages, no button whose job is to display more buttons.** Ken's own framing was that it
sounded like a traditional AAC app and the Express Panel is not trying to be one. That
is right, and it needs its legs put under it, because as stated it reads as taste and
the next person to ask will simply disagree about taste.

**THE FRAME THAT SETTLES IT, and the one to reach for when this comes up again - it
will, at every demo: the panel is the FAST LANE, not the vocabulary.** In a traditional
AAC app the grid *is* the communication channel, so it must hold everything a person
might ever say and folders are the only way to fit that on a screen. Here the grid is
the narrowest of three routes - suggested response cards carry breadth, "In my own
words" carries precision, and the panel carries the handful of things that must be
instant. **If the panel is ever carrying enough vocabulary to need a filing system,
something upstream has failed** - either the response cards are not good enough, or the
app is being used as a small traditional AAC device rather than as a conversation tool.

**Seven grounds, and the middle two were not obvious until this was thought through:**
a folder puts a *decision* in front of the fastest path in the app (two taps, the first
being "which folder?", and a tap is not free for this population); it is a machine for
making the same hole mean different things with nothing on the plastic to say which,
which is spatial stability broken exactly where it is meant to hold; **it creates a
MODE, and a panel that can be left on page two strands the user mid-conversation
reaching for a button that has been in the same place for six months**; **a stray tap
on a phrase says one wrong word, a stray tap on a folder replaces the whole panel** -
a structural consequence sitting among word-sized ones, the same hazard that already
bars tap-to-define during a conversation; hierarchy multiplies selection time under
**scanning and eye gaze**, so we would be designing in a cost paid hardest by access
methods not yet built; the filing system is unbounded setup work on the least popular
part of setup; and **the two mistakes are not equally reversible** - refusing folders
and being wrong costs a later feature, shipping them and being wrong means taking away
something people have already built their vocabulary inside.

**DO NOT ANSWER WITH THE REFUSAL. The app already has the good version of folders, and
the difference is the TRIGGER.** When the partner offers a menu, those alternatives
appear as buttons and then leave - nobody filed them, found them, or navigated back. A
folder is a place the user has to go; context is a fact the app already has. Same
cells, same benefit, no navigation, no mode, no memory burden - and the setup work
*shrinks* as recognition improves rather than growing.

**⚠ TWO NEEDS HIDE IN THE ONE REQUEST, and only one of them is being refused.** Ask
which is meant before answering. *"More phrases than cells"* is the overflow problem
and is answered by the context bands plus an honest cap (extras are unreachable and the
editor says so - the same answer already given for the panel as a whole). *"A rarely
used set I must reach deliberately"* - an emergency card, one counter's usual order -
is **not a folder**, and if it ever proves real the shape is a **summoned full-screen
overlay on the same grid**, exactly as the on-screen keyboard already works, so one
keyguard still fits and **no cell of the base panel changes meaning**. Recorded as the
concession we would make; deliberately not proposed for building.

**WHAT WOULD REOPEN THIS, stated in advance so it is evidence rather than enthusiasm:**
testers who have a full panel *and* their context bands in use, still routinely going to
the composer for things they say often. If instead they use a handful of cells and leave
the rest, the shortage was never real.

**⚠ ONE OF THE SEVEN GROUNDS WAS WRONG AND IS RETRACTED, NOT DELETED (Ken, August 22
2026): folders do NOT break the keyguard.** It is a sheet of plastic with holes and no
labels of its own, so paging changes what is behind a hole and never where the hole is.
What survives of that point is only that nothing tells the user which page is showing.
*Left in the document as a retraction because a deleted argument gets re-derived.*

**THE DESIGN THAT REPLACES FOLDERS — settled with Ken August 22 2026, nothing built.**
Three bands: **Always** (never changes), **Flex** (Ken's name; phrases that suit the
partner and place), **Context** (the buttons that **NEVER SPEAK** — partner, place,
feeling, the partner's offered choices, and the number button).
- **The speaking/influencing split is the organizing rule and it is Ken's**, better than
  the one it replaced: it explains the layout in a sentence, and **a mis-hit in the
  Context band can never say something irreversible.** It also explains something already
  true in the code that nobody had reasoned out — choice buttons ignore the double-tap
  safeguard, because that safeguard guards *speaking*.
- **Choices take the FAR END of the Context band, in solid color (Ken, August 23 2026 —
  this SUPERSEDES the earlier "front of the band" note).** The band's fixed order is
  partners, then places, then feelings, so the far end IS the feelings run — expected to
  be the least used thing on the panel — while the partner buttons at the front are the
  best learned and are never covered. The number button is identical to a choice: solid
  fill, no dashed edge on either, labeled **123** in every case. Both live one exchange.
- **The floor of four on the Context band is UNCONDITIONAL, and it reverses the July 2026
  "choice buttons get no standing reservation" decision.** The reason is the whole
  argument: if the band could collapse when empty, a menu arriving would have to conjure
  four positions and **the panel would change shape mid-conversation**, which must never
  happen. Blank reserved outlines are the price. The July decision was right when those
  cells had no resting job; under bands they hold partner, place and feeling buttons.
- **SIZING: the user sets CONTEXT and FLEX; ALWAYS takes the remainder** (Ken, August 23
  2026). Default Flex 0 and a small Context, so an untouched panel is almost exactly
  today's. **Always has NO floor — zero is legitimate**: a panel given over to steering
  the AI is an important panel. **An Always phrase with no room queues at the very END of
  the Flex band and only takes genuinely spare positions — it can NEVER displace a
  situational phrase** (Ken corrected me on this; I had it backwards). So growing Context
  shortens Always from its bottom, the remaining Always buttons never move, and the
  surplus shows only where the situational lists left room.
- **The Flex band is filled by FOUR situational lists, most specific first**: this partner
  in this place, this partner anywhere, anyone in this place, then the general set. All
  four are made by one mechanism — pick a partner and a place from two lists that also
  offer *Anyone* and *Anyplace*, so the general set is simply Anyone plus Anyplace. A
  phrase in two lists shows once.
- **The two remaining questions are SETTINGS, not decisions** (Ken, August 23 2026): band
  shape (whole rows or a count of buttons) and what tells a partner from a place from a
  feeling. There is no best answer and only the user can settle it; the SLPs are choosing
  the DEFAULT. **Ken expects the Context band to become the dominant band**, which raises
  the differentiator's priority — three kinds in one background is mild at four buttons
  and the main readability problem at twenty.
- **The grid selection moves ONTO the Express Panel tab** (Ken: rethinking the grid is
  part of the same experiment as sizing the bands, so it must not need a tab change).
  Only button size and spacing go with the keyguard settings, on a renamed tab. **Full
  administration design — the whole tab, its order, the lists, and the shipped starting
  set that replaces the hand-delivered panel file — is in the design document, §7.**
- **⚠ ROLES ARE DEAD. Ken killed them and was right.** The idea was a fixed role per
  position (greeting / their subject / …) so meaning stayed put while wording changed.
  It asks the user to think in categories somebody else invented, and it **restricts**
  them — a partner with four things to say and no fourth-role phrase gets a hole. **The
  replacement: the user orders each list by how likely they are to want it, and the band
  fills most-specific-first (see the four lists above).** The only promise is *the most
  likely thing is first*, which the user controls directly. Cost accepted: positions
  shift between situations (the weaker stability requirement; the grid never moves).
- **Turning a band off does NOT re-cut the keyguard** — bands group positions, they do
  not change the grid. My error, corrected by Ken.
- **A recognized partner ARRIVES as a temporary button**, lit, the way an offered choice
  does — because most recognized partners will have no permanent button and there is no
  general set to fall back on. **Context-band overflow is worse than Flex-band overflow**
  for that reason, and it is the one place the summoned overlay earns its keep.
- **The transactional gap is USER-INITIATED requests** (“I need something to eat”), not
  the partner-initiated case, which “what have you got?” already answers. The reply is
  the same asking move one turn later, plus a card naming the user's own preferences
  **in preference order** — which the profile may not currently store.

**The full write-up, with four figures, is
[Conversant AAC Express Panel Design.docx](Documents/) (generator
`scripts/doc-generators/generate-express-panel-design-doc.js`, figures from
`Express Panel Figures.html` via `capture-express-panel-figures.js`).**  Nothing here is built.

**⚠ THE CHOICE CHIPS ARE THE TOP LAYER OF THAT STACK, NOT A FOURTH BAND (Ken caught
that the first draft never said so, August 22 2026).** What the partner offered a moment
ago is simply *more specific* than what is usually said with this person or in this
place, so it is the same most-specific-wins resolution with one extra, transient layer -
one mechanism rather than two. **This does NOT reopen the July 27 2026 decision that
chips get no standing reservation**, which was about cells sitting blank at rest; under
this design they never are, because at rest the band holds context phrases and the chips
lie on top. Three consequences, and the first is a **defect in shipped behavior**:
- **Today chips claim the leading cells of the WHOLE panel**, and the leading cells are
  the six feelings, so a three-way menu **shifts every phrase on the panel three places
  and drops the last few off the end** (currently including the device-disclosure
  button). For that turn everything the user has learned about where things are is void.
  Confined to the band, everything outside it - yes, no, help - stays put. **FIXED ON ITS
  OWN, AHEAD OF THE BAND WORK, August 22 2026** - it was severable and small, and filing
  a shipped fault as a consequence of an unbuilt feature is how it stays shipped. **Ken's
  own framing is the one to keep, because it names the property rather than the code:
  transient buttons APPEAR AND DISAPPEAR FROM THE BOTTOM, so they push nobody around and
  at most cover the last few buttons temporarily.** `chipStartIndex` in
  [express-items.js](app/js/express-items.js) puts them at the last cells; the renderer
  now walks item cells by a FIXED ORDINAL, so an item's cell can no longer depend on how
  many chips are showing. **Measured in the running app with three alternatives: zero
  changes to any cell's position, size or shape, and exactly three cells changed what
  they held.** They flow AROUND the compose key like any phrase - a three-way set cannot
  be contiguous at the end of every layout, and treating that key as an obstacle is what
  keeps everything else still. When the context band exists they move again, to its far
  end, and nothing else about them changes.
- **This puts a FLOOR of four cells on the context band**, because the chip ceiling is
  four and a smaller band would have to drop one of the partner's own alternatives -
  against the standing rule that the partner's alternatives outrank anything we added.
  That is the first hard number anyone has for the band's size.
- **Open, and not settleable from a chair:** chips claiming the FRONT of the band
  displace the first standing roles, which are the most learned cells in it. Claiming
  from the far end would put the transient thing over the least established roles.

## Open Questions (remaining)

> **SESSION HANDOFF — August 3 2026, after release 0.6.5. START HERE — this supersedes the August 1 block below as the handoff.**
>
> **Released:** `0.6.5` (`0a18dc2`, deployed and verified live). **Local `main` is ahead by several unpushed commits and is pre-bumped to `0.6.6`** — see `git log origin/main..main`.
>
> **In 0.6.6-dev, unreleased:** the iPad fullscreen gate (Rule 19's WebKit amendment); the **Listen-button `capturing` fix** (`stt-deepgram` emits `'capturing'` when the voice gate opens, and `handleSttStatus` treated every unrecognised status as *not listening* — silently); Deepgram diagnosability (status on `ws.onopen`, close code carried, and the **Test button now opens the socket the app actually opens**); and the **diagnostic trace** (`app/js/trace.js`, Settings → About, off by default) — **which was REMOVED again on August 5 2026, in this same unreleased cycle, so it never reached a release.** See the Troubleshooting-tab entry under Open Questions.
>
> **DONE: [LISTEN-TEST.md](LISTEN-TEST.md) was run August 5 2026** (`diagnostic-session.log` + four `conversations/*.json`, desktop / Deepgram / auto-resume off / silence 2 s). All five tests behaved as scripted; Tests 1, 2 and 5 are clean, and Test 3 reproduced the loss exactly. **The decision below is made and BUILT** — see "Listen stop/start is a PAUSE" under Open Questions.
>
> **~~THE OPEN DECISION~~ — DECIDED (b), Ken, August 5 2026.** Stopping and restarting Listen mid-turn **discarded the partner's uncommitted speech**. Two options were put to him: **(a) finalize the pending partner turn on STOP**; or **(b) treat stop/start as a PAUSE** so resumed speech appends to the same turn. **Ken chose (b)**, plus a watch-item for a staleness cutoff. The earlier recommendation of (a) rested on precedent in `terminateConversation` and the pardon flow; that precedent does **not** transfer — both of those are *floor changes* (the conversation is over; the user has spoken a pardon), and a mic toggle is not. See the full entry below.
>
> **Also open, no longer urgent:** a transient `sw.js load failed` at startup on the iPad, seen once and not reproducible.

- **DECIDED + BUILT (Ken, August 5 2026) — Listen stop/start mid-turn is a PAUSE, not a turn boundary.** The Listen button controls the **microphone**; it does not end the partner's turn. A stop/start while the partner still holds the floor keeps what they have said and appends the rest. **THE INVARIANT: a partner turn ends at a FLOOR CHANGE — the user picks a response (`handleResponseSelected`), asks for a pardon (`handlePardon`), or ends the conversation (`terminateConversation`) — never at a mic toggle.** Each of those three already calls `stt.resetTranscript()` explicitly, so the invariant was already true everywhere except the one path that broke it.
  - **What the LISTEN-TEST run proved, and it is narrower than the bug report assumed.** Test 3 (stop/start) and Test 4 (uninterrupted pause) are *identical* at the transcript-log layer: both fire two silence checkpoints, both create one pending partner entry at the first and **overwrite it in place** at the second, both keep the **first checkpoint's timestamp**. Test 3's saved entry carries `"6 7 8 9 10"` under timestamp `02:45:45.446Z`, which is when `"1 2 3 4 5"` was written — proof the record was reused, not re-created. So the loss was a **double** one (buffer erased *and* the written line overwritten with shorter text), and its entire cause was one line: `stt.startListening()` zeroing `accumulatedText`/`segments`. Nothing in the log layer, nothing backend-specific, nothing to do with the iPad.
  - **Why (b) over (a).** Finalizing on stop would split one continuous utterance into two consecutive partner turns with no user turn between them — a discourse structure that otherwise only arises from an interruption, and a misrepresentation of what happened for the AI. (b) makes Test 3 byte-for-byte Test 4, which is right: two tests differing only by a mic tap describe the same real event. **And (b) would have made the original `capturing` bug harmless** — that bug fired the start branch spuriously and repeatedly; under (a) it produces a pile of fragments, under (b) the text simply keeps accumulating, which is what Ken perceived as happening anyway. That robustness against a bug class that has already bitten once is the strongest argument for it.
  - **The cost, accepted:** the old discard was the only way to scrap a garbled capture and retry. It was undocumented, silent, and duplicated **Pardon**, which is the designed gesture and also asks the partner to repeat themselves. (b) removes an accidental side effect, not a capability.
  - **Implementation.** `stt.js` splits the mic-opening into `openSource()`, shared by `startListening()` (which now calls `resetTranscript()` first) and a new **`resumeListening()`** (which does not). `app.js` gains **`resumePartnerCapture()`** — it traces and reopens the mic and **deliberately nothing else**: the offered choices, turn steering, `currentPartnerText`, `pendingPartnerHistoryIdx` and the storage-side pending entry all belong to the turn still in progress, and clearing any of them is what lost the words. `toggleListening()`'s start branch picks between them on `heardPartnerText()` — non-empty means mid-turn. *(Deleting the clear outright was the alternative; naming the two intentions keeps the invariant visible at the call site instead of depending on every future caller remembering.)*
  - **Trace change — `start fresh listening` now means something different.** A mid-turn restart emits **`resume listening keeping="…"`**; `start fresh listening` is only reached at genuine turn boundaries, which have already reset the buffer. **A non-empty `discarding=` on that line is now a BUG SIGNAL**, not routine. Re-reading an old trace, remember the field meant the opposite before this date.
  - **Regression coverage:** three tests in `tests/stt.test.mjs`, including `LISTEN-TEST Test 3 == Test 4`, which asserts the post-resume checkpoint carries both halves. Full suite 237/237.
- **TO DO — a way for Claude to edit the Known Issues page directly as development proceeds (Ken, August 9 2026; not built).** The page is live at `https://volksswitch.org/index.php/conversant-aac-known-issues/` and is the only copy (see its DOC-SYNC row for what belongs on it). Today every change means Ken pasting; the point of this item is that an issue confirmed, worked around or fixed during a session can be reflected on the page in the same pass, instead of accumulating as a note nobody actions.
  - **The mechanism exists and was probed on August 9 2026 — WordPress REST API, page id `23303`.** Reads already work unauthenticated; writes return 401 pending a credential. **Application Passwords are enabled** (the API advertises `wp-admin/authorize-application.php`), so Ken creates one in his WordPress profile and it goes in a **gitignored** file beside the other keys — never in the repo, never in a settings profile, never in a backup.
  - **⚠ THE BASE URL IS NOT THE OBVIOUS ONE, and this is the trap that will waste a session:** `https://volksswitch.org/wp-json/` returns a **404 page** — the site runs with `index.php` in its permalinks, so the working bases are **`https://volksswitch.org/index.php/wp-json/`** or `https://volksswitch.org/?rest_route=/`. A 404 there means the path is wrong, **not** that the API is disabled. (`xmlrpc.php` answers 405 and is not the route to use.)
  - **EDIT IN PLACE — fetch the page, change it, put it back. Do NOT generate it from a source file in this repo.** A repo source that publishes to the page recreates precisely the arrangement that got the .docx retired and that cost eight hand edits on the Beta Test Plan: Ken will also edit the page in WordPress, so a generator would silently overwrite him. **Fetch first, diff, then write.** WordPress keeps revisions, which is the rollback.
  - **Every write is publishing to a public page, so it is confirmed with Ken before it goes**, per the standing rule on outward-facing actions — one approval does not cover the next one. Content stays inside a Custom HTML block, so a write must preserve the block comment markers around it.

- **TO DO — calculate `L` (STT latency) and use it to set an OPTIMAL silence trigger (Ken, August 7 2026; not built, and deliberately gated behind the measurement).** The insight this rests on: because `resetSilenceTimer()` fires from `onresult` rather than from acoustic silence, the meaningful quantity is **`L + silencePeriod`** — the real pause the partner has to leave — while the setting exposes only `silencePeriod`, which is an implementation detail the user has no way to reason about. If `L` is known, the app can invert it: the user (or the default) names the **acoustic** pause that should count as "they've finished," and the timer value is derived as `target − L`. That number is comparable to the CA literature (Jefferson's ~1s standard maximum) in a way the raw timer never is, and it would **auto-correct across backends and network conditions** instead of being a single guess that is right for one setup.
  - **Measurability is asymmetric and decides the shape.** Deepgram gives `L` directly from word-level timestamps against our own audio pipeline. The Web Speech API exposes no audio timing at all, so browser STT would need a parallel `getUserMedia` + `vad.js` rig, or a fixed assumed constant. A derived-threshold feature that only works properly on the paid backend is a real design problem, not a footnote.
  - **`L` is not stable — it moves with the network**, so this needs a rolling estimate with clamping, and a floor: the slider currently bottoms at **0.5s**, and a large `L` would want a derived value below that, possibly 0. Decide whether the range extends or the derivation saturates.
  - **⚠ The risk to weigh against all of it: an adaptive threshold is a threshold that changes without the user asking.** This population depends on predictability, and "the app behaves differently today and I don't know why" may be worse than a timer that is slightly wrong but constant. If built, it likely wants to be a **one-time calibration** the user can see and re-run, not a continuously moving target — same reasoning as the one-shot spoken help.
  - **Sequenced behind the checkpoint metrics** (added the same day): the data has to exist before a target can be justified, and if firings-per-turn turns out fine at a fixed 0.5s, this may never be worth building.

- **TO DO — a TROUBLESHOOTING tab: user-runnable diagnostics for the field. REVISIT BEFORE PUBLIC RELEASE (Ken, August 5 2026). Status: PRE-BETA, not yet built.** Ken's framing: *"This is a complex engine and UI, and end users WILL encounter strange behaviors in the field. We'll need a good tool that the user can run (maybe with a script we give them) that will provide sufficient insight for troubleshooting."* He imagines **a Troubleshooting tab in Settings** where this and other error-identification and reporting activities live. Deliberately deferred on August 5 2026 — recorded now so the decision is not re-derived from scratch. **⚠ NOW SCOPED AND AUTHORIZED — see "Beta instrumentation and error reporting" (Ken, August 7 2026).** The tab is build items 1–2 there. Two things that section settles which this entry left open: the trace returns **redacted by default** (counts, not words), which removes the defect that got it removed; and **the tab cannot be the only route** — Settings is unreachable before Start today, and the 0.6.5 top-layer bug already made it unreachable mid-session on the iPad, so a "Report a problem" affordance also goes on the pre-start block.
  - **REMOVED FROM THE APP August 5 2026 (Ken) — it never shipped in a release.** Ken: *"remove it entirely. We will revisit this feature pre-beta. For all we know the engine will have undergone meaningful changes in the interim and the diagnostic will need to be modified."* That reasoning is the important part: **do not treat the old trace as a spec to restore.** It was instrumented against the engine as it stood on August 3 2026, and the point of revisiting pre-beta is to instrument whatever the engine has become. **Recover the deleted module with `git show 4bab171:app/js/trace.js`** (added in `a0aeb7a`, "Add a diagnostic trace and a scripted listen-mode test"). What was removed, so a rebuild knows the full footprint: the `.setting-group` in `app/index.html` (About tab), three `settings-help.json` entries (`diagnosticTrace`, `traceEnabledInput`, `saveTraceBtn`) plus a regenerated `app/js/settings-help.js`, `loadTraceEnabled`/`saveTraceEnabled` in `storage.js`, the `./js/trace.js` precache line in `app/sw.js`, and in `app.js` the import, the boot `setEnabled`, `saveTrace()`, `startTrace()` and all nine call sites. **A leftover `traceEnabled` key may sit in a user's stored settings; nothing reads it, so it is inert.**
  - **The module was generic and well-shaped for this** — worth reading before rebuilding, not worth restoring wholesale — `trace`/`bump`, a 4000-entry cap, ASCII sanitizing so the file survives being pasted into an email, and a never-throw wrapper so the tracer cannot break what it observes. The `trace.start()` header (version, platform, which speech backends, auto-resume, silence period) is already exactly what any bug report needs and is worth keeping verbatim.
  - **The INSTRUMENTATION is not generic — nine call sites, all in `app.js`, all on the listen/turn-taking path** (Listen taps, stt status, silence checkpoints, fresh-vs-resume, response selected, commit, conversation ended). It answers "what did the Listen button do?" and little else. **Not covered, and this is where field reports will actually come from:** `llm.js` (slow or odd suggestions — round-trip timing, failures, retries), `tts.js`/`tts-deepgram.js` (didn't speak, wrong voice), `storage.js` (data-folder permission and write failures), `engine.js` (mode/floor/sequence-stack transitions — "it offered goodbyes in the middle"). Note `errors.log` is already a **separate** channel; the Troubleshooting tab is the natural place to unify the two rather than adding a third.
  - **⚠ THE DEFECT THAT MUST NOT BE REBUILT — the old trace recorded verbatim speech and did NOT honor "Don't save conversations."** It traced `heard="<everything the partner said>"` at the silence checkpoint; six lines below, `logPartnerInterim` ([storage.js](app/js/storage.js)) begins `if (!conversationSaving) return;`. A conversation the user had explicitly marked private was refused by the storage layer and written in full by the tracer, side by side. Same exposure in `commit exchange` (`partner=`/`user=`), `response selected` (`text=`), `start fresh listening` (`discarding=`) and `resume listening` (`keeping=`). Compounding it, `traceEnabled` was persisted in settings and restored at boot, so it could be left on indefinitely unnoticed. And the **conversation partner is a third party who never consented and cannot** — in an AAC context disproportionately clinicians, carers and family, and disproportionately medical or intimate content. `trace.js`'s own header said it "is not something to leave running for a real user"; that judgment is what stood between it and a shippable feature. **Removal resolved it for now** (nothing records, and the stale setting key is inert) — **but any rebuild must honor `conversationSaving` from the first commit, and must not persist "on" indefinitely.**
  - **THE INSIGHT THAT MAKES A SAFE DEFAULT CHEAP: we did not need the words, we needed the COUNTS.** `LISTEN-TEST.md` says so in its own preamble ("counted words make a truncation obvious at a glance"). The whole Test 3 / Test 4 diagnosis survives redaction intact — `heard=<5 words> → discarding=<5 words> → heard=<5 words>` versus `heard=<3 words> → heard=<6 words>`. So a redacted mode is **not a degraded mode** for this class of bug, which is what makes the safe default practical rather than a compromise.
  - **Proposed shape (not built, not decided):** two levels with the safe one as default — **"Record what the app did"** (structure, timings and word counts; shareable; honors the privacy toggle) and **"…including what was said"** (opt-in per session, with a plain warning that the other person's words are recorded and that they have not been asked). Auto-disarm rather than persisting forever. Broaden coverage to LLM/TTS/storage/engine before beta.
  - **Two things to check rather than assume:** (1) on **iPad the data folder is OPFS**, so "send us the file" needs a route through Export or the share sheet — measure on the device before writing any instruction (the 50%-research-error finding). (2) A support flow that mails conversation content to us is a **genuine exception to the local-only promise** the Product Overview makes ("Private by Design") and needs to be stated as one, not buried.
  - **Why it is worth doing at all, and it is specific to this population:** a file the app writes is **a bug report the user does not have to compose** — testers here may have limited ability to type a description at all. The August 5 2026 session is the worked example: the trace settled in one pass what had cost several round trips of prose recollection, and a timestamp in the saved conversation file proved something nobody had thought to ask about.
- **TO DO — watch for cases where a STALENESS CUTOFF on the retained transcript would be valuable (Ken, August 5 2026).** Accepted alongside the pause decision, deliberately **not built**: with pause semantics the retained buffer survives an arbitrarily long stop, so a partner speaks, the user stops Listen, walks away, comes back much later and restarts — and the old words merge into the new turn. **Do not build it speculatively.** It needs a number nobody can currently justify, and the exposure is smaller than it looks: the same unbounded merge *already* happens within one listening session (Test 4 merges across any silence, with no stop at all), and End conversation clears the buffer. Watch for real instances; if they appear, the fix is a time-based expiry on the retained buffer, keyed off how long the mic was closed, and the evidence should say what that duration is.

Live threads:

>    - **Spacing spec (Ken, June 30 2026):** OUTER padding around the whole top-level-component collection (screen-edge → cluster) = **minimum-gap** (NOT ½·gap or 1·gap); inter-component separation = **1·gap**; the dock's internal surround (dock edge → its buttons) = **½·gap** (shipped v0.5.47). **The deferred clause — "insetting the fixed-position dock" — was DELIVERED August 2 2026, but as its own control rather than as part of min-gap: see "Screen edge margin" below.** `--gap-min` token added (root, default 0) as the seam; the outer-padding wiring — including **insetting the fixed-position dock** by min-gap — lands with the min-gap slider in the change-rules (at 0 it's flush/invisible, so deferred to be done testably).

Regenerate it before handing to Sonnet (generator: scratchpad `gen-changes-doc.js`).

- **DECIDED (Ken, July 31 2026) — named settings profiles ARE the settings-persistence and transfer mechanism on Windows; do NOT build automatic settings-to-folder persistence.** Ken's model: **every user chooses a data folder immediately — a core setup instruction** — and is also instructed to **save their current settings as a named profile**. Thereafter, every settings change ends with either **Update** (overwrite the profile in use) or **Save as a new profile**. Either way the change lands in `<data folder>/settings/<name>.json`, which is preserved with the folder and copied to another machine like any other file. **This SUPERSEDES the bulk of the PRE-BETA "Settings persistence — data folder + per-device profiles" item below**: the automatic write-through, the portable/device-specific split, the app-minted device id and the "which device is this?" picker are **not needed** — a per-device profile is just a differently-named file in the same folder, which is the same idea with no code. *(That item's remaining value is as the record of WHY; leave it in place, cross-referenced, rather than deleting it.)*
  - **Why it works now when it wouldn't have a week ago:** the **Update** button (shipped in the current dev cycle, `c5042e0`) is what makes "re-save after every change" a single tap. Before it, re-saving meant retyping the profile's exact name and confirming an overwrite — a ritual nobody sustains, and one typo produced a second near-identical profile. The workflow depends on that button existing.
  - **It also answers the SEC-1 origin move**, which was the original motivation for automatic persistence. Moving to `app.volksswitch.org` empties `localStorage` *and* the IndexedDB folder handle, so the user re-picks their data folder (already a documented setup step) and taps **Load**. Settings come back. No migration code.
  - **The multi-device worry was overstated in the earlier design, and this is worth keeping straight.** That design split settings portable-vs-device-specific because layout values are "screen-dependent." They mostly are not: **button size, gap and minimum-gap are UNITLESS slider positions** and the conversation layout is **proportional** (v0.4.2, two-axis), so the same stored value produces the same *share of the screen* on a different device — transferring it is correct, not wrong. The genuinely per-machine values are **`voiceURI`** (a voice not installed on the new machine simply isn't found and TTS falls back to the browser default — silent but harmless) and **`keyboardMode`** (physical vs on-screen depends on that device's hardware). Both are one-tap fixes after a Load, and naming a profile per device sidesteps them entirely.
  - **THE ONE RISK, and it is the load-bearing one: a profile goes stale silently.** Change a setting, forget to Update, and the file is older than you think — with no cue anywhere on screen, because the live settings are still correct *on this machine*. The gap only surfaces at the moment you Load or move machines, i.e. the worst possible time. Ken's answer is user discipline plus instruction. **A staleness indicator is the candidate reinforcement — DEFERRED to a future decision (Ken, July 31 2026): "it may be an unnecessary complication of the UI."** *(See the TO DO under Open Questions.)*
  - **The iPad is the explicit exception (Ken's own scoping).** There the data folder is OPFS: a profile is written where the user cannot see it, cannot copy it, and where a Safari tab can have the entire origin evicted after seven days. Profiles therefore provide neither backup nor transfer on iPad, and **Export/Import stays mandatory there** — it is also the only bridge between the two iPad storage silos.
  - **Still open, and separable:** Export/Import also carries About Me, people, Express Panel, starters and conversations, all of which a folder copy already covers on Windows — so the *transfer* case for it there is now gone. What nothing else covers is an **in-app** accident: profiles snapshot settings, but nothing snapshots About Me against a mis-confirmed **Restart**. So the remaining question is narrower than before — retire Export/Import on Windows, or keep it purely as that snapshot? Decide when the setup instructions are written.
- **~~TO DO — explore SPOKEN help~~ → BUILT August 2 2026; see UI Layout Rule 17 for the shipped design. Kept below for the reasoning that led to it.** (Ken, August 2 2026.) Tap a help control, then tap the setting in question, and the app vocalizes a short explanation of it. Settings first; other surfaces later if it works.
  - **This SATISFIES UI Layout Rule 14 rather than reversing it, and that is the point.** Rule 14 removed per-control help because the only in-app alternative was a tooltip, and **a tooltip needs hover, which a direct-select user cannot produce.** Tap-then-tap needs no hover. And the cost Rule 14 was avoiding was *screen space* — audio costs none, so the panel stays exactly as decluttered as Ken asked for. The rule's text should be amended if this ships, not overturned: what it forbids is per-control **text on screen**.
  - **The machinery already exists and the audience is right.** This is an app whose user communicates *by* text-to-speech; speaking is the one output channel guaranteed to be set up and tuned to their preference. Reading is not — literacy is a stated entry requirement today, but the roadmap explicitly plans to widen to lower literacy levels, and spoken help is one of the few things that would still work there.
  - **The source text exists and is written, but in the wrong register.** `SETTINGS-HELP-TEXT.md` held all 38 removed hints, grouped by tab and already triaged into three tiers of importance; it was deleted in `22d47a1` once both manuals carried it, and is recoverable in full (221 lines) at **`git show 13fa157:SETTINGS-HELP-TEXT.md`**. **But do not speak it as written** — it was authored to be *read*, and several entries run to eighty words. Listening has no skimming and no re-reading, so spoken help needs its own much shorter register, with the manual keeping the long form. That rewrite is most of the work; the corpus is the easy part.

  **DESIGN SETTLED (Ken, August 2 2026) — text written, mechanism NOT built.**
  - **The words live in [`settings-help.json`](settings-help.json) at the project ROOT and are NOT shipped as a file.** Ken: *"I'm not thinking that we would distribute this file. The user doesn't need to be able to edit it. It is static for a release… The actual content would be embedded in the app or a supporting file inaccessible to the user."* So it follows the **`CHANGELOG.md` → `whats-new.js` pattern exactly**: a root file for editing, a generator that embeds it at release time, nothing fetched at runtime (works offline, no precache entry, no CSP surface). **The staleness trap is the same one `RELEASING.md` already warns about for release notes** — forget the generator and the embedded copy is wrong; fold a generated-matches-source check into the coverage test so it fails loudly.
  - **First cut written: 85 entries** — 59 controls (one per control in the panel), 11 tabs, 10 section headings, 5 radio groups. Median 16 words, longest 24. Keyed by element `id` (all 59 have one), radio `name`, tab `data-tab`, and a `data-help` attribute on multi-control groups. Resolution from a tap: `label[for]` → that control; else the group's `data-help`; else the group's single control.
  - **Coverage is enforced by a TEST, not a process note (my recommendation, accepted in principle).** `tests/settings-help.test.mjs` parses `index.html`, enumerates the Settings controls and fails on any missing entry or orphan — prototyped against the real file, currently 59/59 with no gaps and no orphans. This is what Ken's "include it in DOC-SYNC" asked for, done as a build failure rather than a thing to remember; add the DOC-SYNC row too, but the test is the guard that holds. Needs an ignore-list (the 12 slider steppers).
  - **The load-bearing safety property: tapping a control in help mode must NOT change it.** A slider that moves, or a checkbox that flips, while the user is asking what it does would be worse than no help at all — and this population taps imprecisely, which is the whole reason the feature is attractive. Help mode intercepts the tap (capture phase) before the control sees it, and must also suppress **focus** — focusing a text field would summon the on-screen keyboard.
  - **Barge-out, and the same-target exception (Ken).** Any tap during playback aborts the speech: *"a natural way for people to indicate that they're done early."* **Exception: tapping the SAME control or the same group aborts AND passes the action through; tapping anywhere else aborts and swallows the tap.** This fixes the dead-tap window I flagged (help is one-shot, so it has already disarmed by the time speech plays — you tap a checkbox to hear about it, then tap it again to actually change it, and without the exception that second tap is eaten). **Why it is principled and not merely convenient: the tap you can trust is the one that already landed on target deliberately.** Tapping the same group again is by construction aimed; a tap elsewhere is the one that might be a stray, and a stray is exactly what the swallow protects against.
    - **Verified safe: EVERY destructive Settings button already routes through `confirmDanger()`** — Delete/Load/Update/Save-over profile, Clear error log, and both Import and Restore (those two confirm one level down, inside `importPackageText`). So the worst a pass-through can do is raise the red confirmation card. The only unconfirmed pass-throughs are `resetUsageBtn`, `resetSizingBtn` and `pickFolderBtn`, all trivially redone or non-destructive.
    - **CARVE-OUT — sliders are excluded from pass-through.** On `<input type="range">` a tap is not a neutral action: it jumps the value to wherever the finger landed, so "abort the voice" and "set this to 90%" are the same gesture and cannot be told apart. The rule is therefore *pass-through where a tap is a DISCRETE action* (button, checkbox, select, text field, tab) and not where the tap coordinate carries the value — exactly one control type, 6 of 59. It costs nothing in practice: every slider sits in a group with **− / +** steppers, which are discrete and so pass through under the same-group rule; only a tap on the track itself is swallowed.
  - **One-shot, not sticky (Ken's call, and his reasoning is the better one):** help disarms after each control *"to avoid the situation where the user only wants to be reminded about one setting's usage and operation but can't figure out how to turn off the help message so that they can actually modify the value."* Being trapped in help mode is a worse failure for this population than an extra tap. Per **Rule 6** the armed `?` shows as selected; it also needs a visible **speaking** state so a swallowed tap reads as "still talking" rather than "broken".
  - **Tapping a TAB speaks what that tab is for and does NOT select it** (Ken) — consistent with controls not activating, and one-shot means the next tap navigates normally.
  - **Voice — intelligibility first, NOT distinctness (Ken corrected me here).** My recommendation was the free device voice, for audible distinctness from the user's own speaking voice. Ken's catch kills that: *"there's only one non-silly voice available on an iPad. If they choose the one non-silly voice as their own, then a tool tip would be read in what might be an unintelligible voice."* Correct — the measured iPadOS roster is Samantha plus 45 one-per-language `super-compact` voices plus 19 novelty voices, so "a voice that is not the user's" can easily mean Zarvox. **Rule: reuse the existing novelty-exclusion machinery (`tts.isNoveltyVoice` / the `pickPartnerVoice` selection, which already refuses to hand Practice Mode a joke voice), and where no usable DIFFERENT voice exists, fall back to the user's own rather than speaking help in a cartoon voice.** If they are on a Deepgram voice, a second Aura voice gets intelligibility and distinctness together. **Cost is explicitly not a concern** (Ken: the feature will not be used often) — do not re-raise it.
  - **Scope: Settings only** (Ken: *"until we see real value elsewhere. I don't want anything in the conversation UI that could slow or trip someone up."*) — which also matches Rule 1's priority order. The conversation screen would benefit most but is keyguard-backed, and adding a modal state there is a bigger decision.
  - **Still open:** how this interacts with the future **non-English support** to-do (the JSON would need per-language versions, like every other bundled content file).

- **TO DO — keep Apple's novelty voices out of Practice Mode's "Auto" partner pick, and split the `super-compact` tier from `compact` (measured July 31 2026; not built).** Both follow from the iPadOS voice roster recorded in the iPad section. (1) **Auto picks a gag voice on iPad.** `pickPartnerVoice()` prefers a voice that isn't the user's, in the same language; on iPadOS the only en-US voices besides Samantha are the 19 legacy `com.apple.speech.synthesis.voice.*` novelty voices, so the practice partner speaks as Albert, Zarvox or Bahh. Fix: exclude that URI prefix from Auto's candidates, and consider sorting novelty voices last in both pickers (filtering them is standard practice for web speech apps). **Decide whether to hide them outright or merely de-prioritize** — a user may legitimately want Whisper, and hiding is not reversible from the UI. (2) **`voiceQuality()` labels `super-compact` as "Compact"** (the hyphen satisfies its word boundary), so two genuinely different tiers read identically. Ascending: `super-compact < compact < enhanced < premium`. Needs a label that does not imply *better* — "Super-compact" reads as an upgrade, which is backwards. Neither is urgent; (1) is the one with a user-visible wrong result today.
  - **(1) DONE July 31 2026 — hidden, reversibly, and Auto never picks one.** Ken's call was *"hide, with a checkbox to show them."* Built in `tts.js` as `isNoveltyVoice()` / `usableVoices()`, with Settings → Speech & Input gaining "Show this device's joke voices". **Matched by EXPLICIT ID, not by the `com.apple.speech.synthesis.voice.*` prefix** — Alex lives under that same legacy prefix and is a genuinely good voice, so a prefix rule would have hidden something a user might want (unit-tested, along with Eloquence voices, which are legitimate AAC voices and must never be caught). **`pickPartnerVoice()` filters unconditionally**, whatever the checkbox says: that setting governs what the user may *choose*, not what the app picks *for* them. Verified on a simulated iPad roster — Auto now chooses **Daniel (en-GB)**, a real voice, where it previously chose Albert or Zarvox. **(2) remains open.**
    - **The "Voices offered to the app" readout was REMOVED from Settings → About (Ken, August 2 2026).** It was built the same day as the hiding, to answer "why is this voice not in my list" on a device with no console, and it did its job: the iPadOS roster is now a recorded platform fact (a fixed curated list — one `super-compact` voice per language plus the 19 legacy novelty voices, with Enhanced/Premium unreachable at any price), so re-reading the list cannot teach us anything new. It was also **not actionable by a user** — the Voice picker already shows what they can choose — and it spent a 6-row textarea plus two buttons of a panel that Rule 14 had just been used to clear out. The one user-facing question it answered, "where did the joke voices go?", is now answered directly by the "Show this device's joke voices" checkbox. **Removed outright** (Ken's call), not folded into the error-log report: `voiceDiagnosticLines`, `renderVoiceList`, the `#voiceListView`/`#voiceListActions` markup and their CSS are all gone; `tts.voiceQuality` and `tts.isNoveltyVoice` stay because the pickers use them. **What this gives up, so it is on the record:** if a beta tester on an unknown device reports "my voice isn't in the list," there is no longer any way to see what their browser actually offered — they have no console either. If that happens, the cheap restoration is to append the roster to the error-log **Copy** report, which needs to go in *ahead* of `buildErrorReport`'s early `(no errors recorded)` return, since a missing voice logs no error. **Do not rebuild it as its own panel** — it belongs in the general **"Copy System Info"** item under Open Questions (Ken, Aug 2 2026), which exists precisely because one-diagnostic-per-subsystem is how the Settings panel fills back up with developer tooling.
- **TO DO — decide whether `speakBuiltin` needs a watchdog for an utterance that never ends (found July 31 2026; PRE-EXISTING, not introduced by the voice work).** `tts.speak()` resolves on the browser's `onend`/`onerror`. If the engine accepts an utterance and then fires neither — which is exactly what a silent `speechSynthesis` on iOS looks like — the promise never settles **and `speaking` stays latched true forever**. That is worse than it sounds: the STT echo filter reads that flag, so it would conclude the app is permanently talking and **suppress every silence checkpoint from then on**, ending the conversation loop with no error raised anywhere. Reproduced in the preview (an utterance the engine accepts but never completes leaves `isSpeaking()` true indefinitely). **Partly mitigated already:** the July 31 work wrapped both the voice assignment and the `speak()` call so a *throw* can no longer latch it, and audio is unlocked on Start, which is when iOS is likeliest to swallow speech. **The remaining case is accepted-but-silent.** The candidate fix is a duration-scaled watchdog (roughly `2s + 120ms × characters`) that clears the speaking state if no end arrives — deliberately generous, because firing early is the *opposite* failure: the app would treat its own still-playing speech as the partner's. **Not built: it changes timing on the core speech path, which deserves a deliberate decision rather than a drive-by.**
- **TO DO — decide whether the settings-profile STALENESS INDICATOR is worth its UI cost (Ken, July 31 2026; deferred, not built).** The named-profile workflow (see the DECIDED entry above) has one silent failure: change a setting, forget to **Update**, and the profile file is older than you think — with no cue anywhere, because the live settings are still correct *on this machine*. It surfaces only when you Load or move machines, i.e. the worst moment. **The candidate fix:** on Settings open, diff the live settings bundle against the active profile's file and say so in the status line that already exists — `In use: "Ken's Surface" — unsaved changes`. **Deferred on Ken's reservation: "it may be an unnecessary complication of the UI."** The Settings panel is already dense, and for this population a line most users read as noise most of the time is a cost.
